# Front Cover

->[Image of the front binding]<-

The volume is bound in a dark brown leather, showing the heavy wear and character of a historical artifact. The surface is aged, marked by scratches and some loss of the original finish, telling the story of its long survival.

The cover is decorated with <term>blind-tooled</term> ornamentation <note>A decorative technique where stamps or rollers are pressed into dampened leather to create a recessed design without the use of gold or ink.</note>. This decoration includes:

*   A multi-line border framing the entire cover.
*   A series of circular stamps containing stylized floral or leaf-like motifs.
*   A central rectangular panel, also defined by multiple lines.
*   Small heart-shaped or leaf-shaped stamps placed within the central panel and along the borders.

The spine of the book, visible on the right edge, features three prominent <term>raised bands</term> <note>These ridges indicate where the leather covers the cords or "supports" to which the interior pages are sewn.</note>. There is no visible text or title on the exterior of this binding.

<summary>This is the physical front cover of the manuscript, featuring a dark brown leather binding with traditional blind-tooled floral and geometric decorations.</summary>

# The 41st leaf

...who, from the movement of the flames, from their color, from the crackling, and many other things—I know not with what devilish help—could better foretell future things. In addition, the entrails of sheep and bulls and all other animals were so well known to her that through them, with other help and arts—as is said of her—she often compelled the unclean spirits to come to her, to speak and give answers to her questions.

And after the Greek kings who lay before the city <note>The city of Thebes, during the legendary war of the "Seven Against Thebes"</note> to conquer it were slain, and King Creon had undertaken to rule the city with great force, she came into that part of the world called Asia to flee the new king. There she was also the first to found the temple of the idol, Clarian Apollo <note>original: "Clarÿ appollinis"; an ancient Greek oracle at Claros, near Ephesus</note>, which from then on was magnificently built and honored. At that same place, she gave birth to Mopsus, the greatest diviner—whom we call <term>prophets or soothsayers</term> when they predict something from the divine heart of our faith—yet it is not found what father sired him.

But some others say that after the Theban war, she wandered for a long time with several others and finally came to Italy and was wed to Tiberinus <note>The god of the River Tiber</note>, by whom she conceived and bore a son, Cythas, who was called Bianor by some. With him, she came out over the mountains into Lombardy and Venetia, toward Benacum <note>The Latin name for Lake Garda</note>, the "watery land" of our lake, for she believed that same place to be suitable for her work.

And because she spent the rest of her time there, she had a dwelling built on piles and pillars in the middle of the marsh, where she was also buried after her death. And at that place, her son Cythas had Mantua built, named after her, in which marquises rule to this day. Yet there are some who truly believe...

...that she maintained eternal chastity. That would indeed be holy and highly praiseworthy, if she had not stained it with evil arts <note>A reference to sorcery or the "dark arts" often associated with the women of Lemnos or Medea in this cycle of stories</note>, but instead had kept it unblemished for God the Lord, to whom the sacrifice of all chastity is acceptable.

### ¶ Concerning the wives of the youths called Minyans, the 29th chapter.

[Woodcut illustration: In the foreground, three women in medieval dress are labeled "Minyan women" <note>original: "menie"</note>. To the right, a stone building has a barred window through which three men's faces are visible; a label below the window reads "husbands" <note>original: "vveres," likely a Germanized form of the Latin *viri* meaning men or husbands</note>.]

**T**he names and the number of these women have been worn away by age, so that they are no longer named by the chroniclers. This is indeed unjust, for through their deeds they have well deserved to be ranked among the most illustrious women. And though envious Fortune <note>literally "the wheel of luck"</note> has allowed their names to fade, they should nonetheless—as far as we are able—be adorned and honored for their praiseworthy actions, and be rescued from oblivion and placed into the memory of posterity, as they deserve. 

The Minyans <note>original: "menie"</note> were youths not of the lowest, but of the highest nobility, distinguished members of the company of Jason and the Argonauts. When these men, after completing their expedition <note>original: "her fart," meaning a military campaign or voyage</note> from the island of Colchis, returned again to Greece...

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of a group of settlers (the Minyans, descendants of the Argonauts) who, after being exiled from Lemnos, sought refuge in Sparta (Lacedaemon).</meta>

<margin>50</margin>

->The 42nd leaf<-

[when they] had come, they left their old dwelling and chose to have their residence among the Lacedaemonians <note>The classical name for the Spartans</note>. They were not only received by them in a good and friendly manner, but were made partakers of all power and were accepted among the governors of the common good <note>term: "common good" (German: *gemeiner nucz*), referring to the administration of the commonwealth or state</note>. At that same time, they were very rich and highly noble, not only by paternal blood but also because they had intermarried with the highest lineages of the Lacedaemonians. Thus their nobility was doubly enlightened, for among other things, they had the most beautiful wives from the noblest Lacedaemonian citizen families—which is regarded as no small worldly ornament. Additionally, many of the common people entered into their service. They did not consider all of this as happening to them through the grace of the common power <note>i.e., the Spartan state</note>, but rather because of their own merit; therefore, they fell into a foolish desire to rule and imprudently attempted to seize all the power of the land. For this misdeed, once it was revealed, the authorities cast them into a hard prison and passed judgment to put them to death as declared enemies of the common good, through the highest courts. And it was decreed that this sentence be carried out the very next night, according to the ancient custom of the Lacedaemonians. When their sorrowful wives heard this, they went to work and devised unheard-of assistance to show their condemned husbands, and they also fulfilled with deeds what they had planned. They put on humble clothes <note>original: "boͤſe kleyder," meaning wretched or mourning garments</note>, veiled their weeping faces, and when the sun had set, they came to the dungeon, desiring to see their dying husbands. And because they were among the noblest in the city, this was easily granted to them by the guards. But when they came inside to them, they drove away the

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the women entering the prison to visit their husbands who were condemned to death. This page describes the execution of their plan to save them.</meta>

### The 42nd Leaf

[They spent their] time not with mourning and lamenting; instead, they revealed their plan to them. They exchanged their clothes with the men and veiled their faces according to women’s custom. Thus, the youths went out of the prison in the women's clothes, weeping, with sad gestures and their eyes cast down toward the earth. The darkness of the night was also helpful to them, as was the dignity of the women, which allowed the guards to be easily deceived into letting the condemned men out to their deaths while keeping the women in their place.

This story was not revealed until the executioners <note>original: "vollender der vrteyl," those who carry out the sentence</note> arrived to kill those who had been condemned. Only then did they find the women lying in the prison in the place of the men. This is, without doubt, the great loyalty of women and an exceedingly high, fervent love. 

I shall say nothing of how the guards of the condemned were made into a laughingstock, how the prisoners were liberated, what the authorities decreed regarding the matter, or what resulted from it. Instead, I wish only to say a little about the powers of true love within holy matrimony <term>holy gemahelschafft: a term for the sacred bond of marriage or companionship</term>. 

Some maintain that, according to the ancient law of nature which cannot be changed, no greater jealousy can arise than that from the impurity of the loins <note>referring to sexual infidelity or the violation of the marriage bed</note>. Conversely, no greater love can be found than that of a friendly and harmonious marriage. For the fire of reason does not burn toward madness, but rather warms toward mutual delight, and it binds hearts together so that they always, in the same manner, desire and reject the same things <note>This "willing and not-willing" is a classical definition of perfect friendship</note>. And when this lovely habit—

<summary>This page recounts a famous story of marital devotion where wives trade places with their condemned husbands to facilitate their escape from prison. The author then transitions into a philosophical reflection on the unique strength and harmony found in the bond of marriage.</summary>
<keywords>prison escape, fidelity, holy matrimony, gender roles, law of nature, marital love, executioners</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Minyan women, famously recounted by Boccaccio. On the previous page, the husbands of these women (descendants of the Argonauts) had been imprisoned by the Spartans and sentenced to death.</meta>

# The xliij. leaf
<note>original: "Das xliij. blat" — Leaf 43</note>

whenever adversity occurred, they offered themselves unasked to endure effort, labor, and trouble for the sake of helpful counsel. That same companionable, sweet, and proven love between the <term>Minyan</term><note>The Minyan women were the wives of the descendants of the Argonauts. When their husbands were imprisoned in Sparta, the women saved them through a famous act of self-sacrifice and trickery.</note> spouses overcame their spirits, so that they devised such a clever and lofty stratagem when their husbands were in distress—a plan they otherwise could not have envisioned—involving the arrangement of clothes, the timing of the right hour, and every precaution, so that they might blind the watchful guards. 

Therefore, they set aside all pleasure and joy and employed their high reason, resolving in the depths of their hearts to leave nothing untried for the safety of their friends <note>The text uses "freündes" (friends), but in this context, it refers specifically to their husbands.</note>. Because of this, a goodness arose in their spirits through which they defiantly sought to release their husbands. Thus, those who had been condemned by the highest court, who lay harshly in the dungeon, who were abandoned, and who were now delivered into the hands of the executioners, were set free through faithful and complete partnership and gifted with life once more. 

And the greatest thing of all was that the supreme authority was thereby shamed, all statutes defied, and the will of the entire community hindered and nullified by them. In order to free their husbands, they placed themselves in mortal danger, falling into the power of those they had mocked and deceived. 

I cannot marvel enough at the great faithfulness and such total love shown here; for if the love within them had been small and cold, they might well have remained at home unpunished and with a good reputation, having never attempted such great things. But to conclude much with few words: I may well call these women "manly," bold, and "true men," while calling the young men "women," in whose guise they also went out and were saved <note>This refers to the climax of the story where the women traded clothes with their husbands; the men escaped disguised as women, while the wives remained in the cells, facing the executioners in their husbands' place.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the bravery and cleverness of the Minyan women, who swapped clothes with their imprisoned husbands to save them from execution, defying the law out of marital devotion.</summary>

# The 43rd leaf

¶ Virgil in the first book of the *Aeneid*:
Penthesilea, in a fury, leads the ranks of Amazons with their crescent-shaped shields, and she glows in the midst of thousands; a warrior maiden, she dares to clash with men. <note>original Latin: "Ducit amazonidum lunaris agmina peltis / Penthesilea furens mediisq; in milibus ardet / Bellatrix audetq; viris concurrere virgo"</note>

### ¶ Concerning Penthesilea, Queen of the Amazons, the 30th chapter.

[Woodcut illustration of an armored woman on horseback leading other armored riders into battle. A label above her reads: Penthesilea]

Penthesilea the maiden was a queen of the Amazons, and she reigned after Orithyia and Antiope. <note>Orithyia and Antiope were famous predecessor queens of the Amazon nation in Greek mythology.</note> I have not read of what lineage she was born. She despised her own exceeding beauty and cast off her soft, womanly disposition; she put on armor <note>original: "harnasch"</note> according to the custom of her ancestors and placed the helmet upon her golden hair. With her quiver at her side, she did not occupy herself with womanly tasks, but rather with rigorous, manly knightly deeds, using the war chariot and horses. She distinguished herself as more remarkable than other queens in both physical strength and the arts of war. She must also have been quite ingenious, for one reads of her that she was the first to invent the battle-axe <note>original: "wurf beyhel," literally a throwing axe or war axe</note>, which was unknown to the world before her time. Therefore...

<summary>This page introduces Penthesilea, Queen of the Amazons, describing her rejection of traditional feminine roles in favor of military prowess and the invention of the battle-axe.</summary>
<keywords>Penthesilea, Amazons, Virgil, Aeneid, women warriors, battle-axe, Orithyia, Antiope, ancient warfare</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Penthesilea, the Amazon Queen, specifically focusing on her arrival at the Siege of Troy and her motivations for aiding the Trojans.</meta>

The [crossed out] 44th leaf

Horace writes in his *Odes* <note>original: "Oratius ſchreybt in odis"; while the text cites the Roman poet Horace, this specific romantic legend of Penthesilea and Hector is more common in medieval chivalric retellings of the Trojan War</note>. That same Penthesilea, as some maintain, when she heard of the unheard-of strength of Hector of Troy, began to love him with a never-before-seen fervor and was so eager for him that she might conceive an heir for her kingdom from him. For this reason, she marched well-equipped with a great army to Troy to aid him against the Greeks. And although the Greeks were widely and highly praised for their strength and arts of war, nevertheless—so that she might please Hector with her power and her skill in war, as well as her beauty—she was diligent in fighting the enemies without fear. She often struck them from their horses to the ground with her lance, often wounded them mortally with her sword, and often pursued those fleeing with her projectiles <note>original: "geſchuͤtze"; in this context, it refers to missile weapons like bows or javelins</note>.

# Page 43

### Ovid, Metamorphoses, Book 13
"And so that my grave might not be without honor, the sacrifice of Polyxena pleases the ghost of Achilles." <note>original Latin: "Utqʒ meum non sit sine honore sepulcrum / Placet achilleos mactata polixena manes"</note>

## Concerning Polyxena, daughter of King Priam, Chapter 31

->[Woodcut illustration depicting the sacrifice of Polyxena. A woman kneels on a tomb while a man behind her holds her hair and a sword. Labels in the woodcut: **Polyxena**, **Neoptolemus**, **Achilles**]<-

Polyxena the maiden was the daughter of King Priam of Troy and Hecuba. She was of such surpassing, blooming beauty that she ignited the heart of Achilles with the <term>flames of Cupid</term> <note>a poetic way of describing falling passionately in love</note> so fervently that, through the deceitful instruction of her mother Hecuba, she arranged for him to come to her alone by night in the temple of <term>Apollo Thymbraeus</term> <note>A temple dedicated to Apollo located near Troy</note>. There, he was mortally shot by Paris. 

On account of this death—after the power of the Trojans was diminished and the city of <term>Ilium</term> <note>Another name for the city of Troy</note> was destroyed—Neoptolemus <note>The son of Achilles, also known as Pyrrhus</note> led her to his father's grave to take revenge for the death of Achilles. And when she saw the grim youth draw his sword, and all the surrounding people wept for her, the noble maiden offered herself with such a strong spirit and such an unquivering face...

<summary>This page recounts the tragic fate of the Trojan princess Polyxena, who was sacrificed by Achilles' son, Neoptolemus, to appease the ghost of his father after the fall of Troy.</summary>
<keywords>Polyxena, Achilles, Neoptolemus, Troy, King Priam, Hecuba, Trojan War, Ovid, Metamorphoses, sacrifice</keywords>

# The 45th leaf

...her innocent neck to death. That the strength of her mind was no less a wonder to people than the kindness <note>The word "gütikeyt" here implies a sense of duty or piety.</note> of the son toward his father. This is without doubt great and well worthy of memory: that such a tender age, the female sex, and so gently raised a royal daughter could so constrain and contain her spirit in the reversal of fortune. And especially under the sword of the victorious enemy, under which the manly hearts of strong men are often turned into despondency and trembling. It is good to believe that such courage came from a highly noble nature, which she showed through her contempt for death; she was created as such a bold woman, though she was attacked by evil fortune and raised up <note>or "taken away"</note> so soon.

Hecuba was also cursed <note>original Latin: "Meredicta," likely a scribal error for "maledicta" (cursed) or "mutata" (transformed).</note>. Ovid, Metamorphoses, Book 13.
"And the fields which she terrified with her strange barking."
And from Metamorphoses, Book 13:
"And her jaws being ready for words, she barked as she attempted to speak."
<note>These Latin fragments refer to the myth that Hecuba, driven mad by grief after the fall of Troy, was transformed into a dog.</note>

## ¶ Concerning Hecuba, the Queen of the Trojans, the 32nd Chapter.

**H**ecuba, the most illustrious queen of the Trojans, was in her high glory a symbol and a lesson on the transience <term>transience: the state of being temporary or fleeting</term> of power and the transformation into misery and tribulation. Cisseus <note>original: "Cipseus"</note>, the King in Thrace, was her father. She was given as a wife to the most illustrious King Priam of Troy, by whom she bore nineteen children, both sons and daughters. Among them was the greatest ornament of the Trojan dominion, Hector, whose brilliance in such rigorous knighthood was so bright that he did not only enlighten himself, but also ennobled all his friends and the fatherland with eternal glory. She was also not only radiant from the blessedness of the kingdom...

<summary>This page concludes a reflection on the stoic execution of a noblewoman (likely Polyxena) and begins the biography of Hecuba, Queen of Troy, emphasizing her noble lineage and her famous son, Hector.</summary>
<keywords>Hecuba, Troy, Hector, Priam, Ovid, Metamorphoses, Cisseus, nobility, fortune, knighthood</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Hecuba, the Queen of Troy, following the description of her noble lineage. It focuses on the "misfortune of luck" that befell her during and after the Trojan War.</meta>

# Page 45

...and many of the noble children; but also because of the **misfortune of luck**, which rules all this world, she became widely and broadly known to many. For when the death of her dearest son **Hector**, and of the youth **Troilus**—who showed a greater manhood than was typical for his age—occurred, both were slain by the hand of **Achilles**. These two were the strong foundation upon which the kingdom had stood, and she wept for them in excessive mourning.

She also saw **Paris** miserably treated by **Pyrrhus** <note>Also known as Neoptolemus, the son of Achilles.</note>; **Deiphobus**, whose ears, mouth, and nose were cut off before he was killed; the burning of the entire city of **Ilium** <note>original: "ÿlium"—another name for Troy.</note>; and the death of her son **Polites**, who was killed in the lap of **Priam**, his father. She witnessed her husband Priam being stabbed at the altar, to which he had fled for freedom <note>This refers to the ancient custom of seeking "sanctuary" or "asylum" at a holy altar where one was supposed to be safe from violence.</note>.

She saw the imprisonment of her daughter **Cassandra**, her son Hector’s wife **Andromache**, and her own capture by the enemies. Her daughter **Polyxena** had her head struck off upon the grave of Achilles. **Astyanax**, the child of her son <note>the son of Hector</note>, was dragged out of a hole and crushed with a stone. All of this the miserable woman saw with her own eyes.

And at last, she found her youngest son, **Polydorus**, killed through the treachery of **Polymestor** and buried by the shore of **Thrace**, because of which she mourned in wretched despair. Through such oversized, unspeakable grief and tribulation, some say she lost her mind and ran through the fields of Thrace, raging and howling like a dog until her end. After her death, she was buried on the shore called <term>Cynosema</term> <note>original: "Cinosemia"—from the Greek meaning "The Dog's Tomb."</note> by the **Hellespont** Sea <note>original: "möre helesponticū"—the narrow strait in northwestern Turkey connecting the Aegean Sea to the Sea of Marmara.</note>.

Others say she was led away with the other captive women by the enemies. And so that she would never be freed from misery and sorrow, she finally saw—after **Agamemnon** was killed—her dear daughter Cassandra strangled by order of **Queen Clytemnestra**.

<summary>This page catalogs the devastating personal losses of Queen Hecuba of Troy, detailing the violent deaths of her family members and her subsequent descent into madness or continued suffering in captivity.</summary>
<keywords>Hecuba, Troy, Hector, Achilles, Priam, Cassandra, Polyxena, Cynosema, Hellespont, Greek Mythology, Trojan War</keywords>

Leaf 46

¶ Virgil, in the fifth book of the *Aeneid* <note>original: "Virgilius quinto eni." The quote is actually from Book 2, line 403, of Virgil’s Aeneid.</note>:
Behold, the daughter of Priam, the virgin Cassandra, was dragged with disheveled hair from the temple and the inner sanctums of Minerva. <note>original: "Ecce trahebatur passis priameia virgo Crinibus a templo cassandra aditisq[ue] minerue"</note>

### On Cassandra, the daughter of Priam, the 34th Chapter

[Woodcut illustration depicting the capture of Cassandra. Labels within the image: "Cassandra" <note>original: "casandi"</note> written vertically on the left; "Troy" <note>original: "troya"</note> above the city; a scroll held by a woman on the right contains the words "High Troy shall fall" <note>original: "superum ilium cadet"</note>.]

Cassandra was a daughter of King Priam of Troy. The ancients say of her that she possessed the spirit of prophecy within her. Whether this came from her own diligent study, or through the grace of God, or the deception of the devil <note>The author presents three common medieval explanations for "pagan" prophecy: natural learning, divine gift, or demonic trickery</note>, is uncertain to us. Yet it is said of her how, long before it happened, she prophesied with a loud voice and clear understanding about the audacity of Paris in kidnapping Helen, her arrival in Troy, the long siege of the city, and finally the destruction of the same and the downfall of the king. And because of these prophecies, she was severely beaten by her father and brothers, and her words were not heeded. From this, the poets took the fable of how she was wooed by Apollo, and how she promised to comply with his desires, provided that he [endowed] her with the art of... <note>The text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page; the legend typically concludes with Apollo giving her the gift of prophecy but cursing her so that she would never be believed.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the story of Cassandra of Troy, detailing her gift of prophecy regarding the city's destruction and the disbelief and physical abuse she suffered from her family in response.</summary>
<keywords>Cassandra, Troy, King Priam, prophecy, Trojan War, Virgil, Aeneid, Apollo, mythology</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Cassandra, the Trojan prophetess, following her interactions with the god Apollo and her eventual fate after the fall of Troy.</meta>

->The 47th Leaf<-

...gifted with prophecy. And when she was granted this by him <note>The "him" mentioned is the god Apollo, who gave Cassandra the gift of prophecy in exchange for the promise of her love.</note>, she did not wish to keep the promise she had made to him. And as he could not take the gift back from her, he added a curse to it: that she might indeed prophesy, but no one would believe her words, treating them as if a fool had spoken them. 

She was betrothed to a noble youth named Coroebus <note>Original: "Cozthus chorebus"</note>, who received her kindly. But in sorrow and mourning, he was taken from her in the war before the city <note>The Siege of Troy</note>. And at last, after the loss of her father and other friends, she became a prisoner, allotted to Agamemnon as his portion of the spoils of war. 

And as she was led by him toward the city of Mycenae, she prophesied to him on the way how Clytemnestra <note>Agamemnon's wife</note> had prepared a plot to kill him. But Agamemnon did not believe her prophecy. And after many kinds of worry, fear, and the hardships of the sea, they came to Mycenae; there Agamemnon was killed by his wife, and afterward Cassandra was strangled.

¶ Virgil, 11th Book of the *Aeneid*
By the hand of his wicked wife, he perished upon his very threshold. <note>Original Latin: "Coniugis infande prima inter limina dextra Oppecit" (oppetiit). This refers to Agamemnon's murder upon returning home from the Trojan War.</note>

<summary>This page recounts the tragedy of Cassandra, specifically how Apollo cursed her prophecies to never be believed and her subsequent death at the hands of Clytemnestra alongside Agamemnon.</summary>

The               47th               leaf
¶ Concerning Clytemnestra, the Queen
of Mycenae; the 34th chapter.

**C**lytemnestra was the daughter of Tyndareus, the King of Oebalia, born of Leda, and a sister of Castor, Pollux, and Helen. She was married to Agamemnon, the King of Mycenae. Although she was illustrious due to her high birth, she was nevertheless widely slandered for the audacious crime she committed. For at the time when her husband Agamemnon governed the Greek people at Troy as their commander, and she had already borne him many children, she fell into unseemly lust and wicked love for the idle and cowardly youth Aegisthus. He was a son of Thyestes, born of Pelopeia, who had withdrawn from the life of knighthood out of laziness and joined the priestly office. And through the help and advice of the elder Nauplius <note>Nauplius sought revenge against the Greek leaders because they had wrongly executed his son, Palamedes.</note>—who was once the father of Palamedes—they became unseemly companions to one another. From this evil arose a fear of the King's return because of their misdeed; and through the advice of her lover—and perhaps through the resentment she felt because he had brought Cassandra <note>The daughter of King Priam and a prophetess, whom Agamemnon took as a war prize.</note> back with him to Mycenae—the bold and rapacious <note>original: "freßdig," literally gluttonous or greedy; here used to describe her unrestrained and consuming passions.</note> woman became daringly and murderously enraged against her husband with a weaponized mind. And when he returned victorious from Troy in triumph, having suffered through many great tempests of the sea, he was received by her with a false heart and feigned friendship, under the guise of a wife eagerly awaiting her husband’s arrival. And when they had eaten, and he was sated with good wine, she arranged with her lover Aegisthus—who, as some say, lay secretly hidden in the hall—to kill him. Others say that as he sat at the table, he was dressed in the garments he had won through victorious wars. Because he the Greek... <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page.</note>

He went to a place where the woman brought him the robe; he thrust his arms into the sleeves and threw the garment over his head, searching for the opening. While he was thus wavering to and fro, searching for the head-hole, she signaled to the adulterer Aegisthus to kill him. Because he was entangled, this was easily accomplished. ### Concerning Helen, the 35th Chapter

->[Woodcut illustration: In the center, a man and two women sit in a small boat upon the water. In the background is a fortified city. The figures and places are labeled: **Troy**, **Paris**, **Helen**, **Lacedaemon** <note>Lacedaemon is the ancient name for the city-state of Sparta.</note>.]<-

**H**elen—as many of the ancients believe—was well known to the entire world because of her surrender to <term>unchastity</term> <note>The original term "vnkeüsche" refers to sexual immorality or promiscuity, reflecting the medieval moralizing view of Helen’s flight with Paris.</note> and because of the long and great war raised on her account. She was the daughter of Tyndareus, King of <term>Oebalia</term> <note>The text reads "Cybalie," a likely corruption of Oebalia, an ancient name for the territory of Sparta.</note>, born of the most beautiful woman, Leda, and married to Menelaus, the King of the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note>. The beauty of this same Helen—as all the ancient Greek masters and the Latin writers after them say—was so elegant and extraordinarily radiant that she might easily be placed above the beauty of all other women. Indeed, Homer wearied his spirit—even though he possessed <note>The original German uses "englische vernunfft," which translates literally to "angelic intellect" or "angelic reason," suggesting a mind of superhuman quality.</note> angelic intellect, to say nothing of other writers—before he could fully describe her beauty in his poems based on all he had heard. And this has been confirmed by all the best...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the pursuit of artistic perfection and the creation of ideal beauty, likely in the context of a Renaissance-era treatise on art or history.</meta>

->.48.<-

...diligence, effort, and labor to create a likeness of the highest beauty <note>The original term is "zierd," which can mean adornment, ornament, or a crowning achievement of beauty</note>, so that this same beauty would be known to those who come after. Among the masters, the best and most renowned was commissioned by the people of Croton <note>A prominent Greek colony in Southern Italy, famous in antiquity for its athletes and physicians</note> for a great sum of money; he was named Zeuxis of Heraclea <note>A celebrated Greek painter from the 5th century BCE, famous for his "idealizing" technique</note>. To shape her with the brush, he exercised all his strength, intellect, art, and reason.

However, since this same painter had no other model but the poems of Homer describing her beauty, and the common praise of all people—from which he could not, according to his own judgment, sufficiently form her image in his mind—he thought to gather her beauty from other well-proportioned women and boys <note>The mention of "women and boys" (frawen vnd knaben) reflects the Renaissance artistic practice of seeking the "ideal human form" across different subjects</note>. He did this so that he might better capture and internalize the divine beauty of her facial features and her finest limbs. Through this, he intended to create an image that would show her form in future times.

And five of the most beautifully shaped in gesture and limb that could be found were presented to him. From all of them, he assembled a single figure, taking the best and most beautiful from each. Yet even then, with all his art and power, he could not sufficiently assemble an image that could equal the common praise of her beauty. 

I do not wonder at this, for who could depict with a brush and colors, or with an engraving tool <term>grabmesser|An engraving tool or burin used to carve into metal or wood</term>, the joyful eyes, the lovely face, her pleasing smile <note>The original "schmollen" here suggests a charming, playful, or slightly pouting expression of the mouth that denotes beauty</note>, and the many kinds of graceful gestures that change along with words and deeds? For these are the works of nature alone, and not of the master. Therefore, he made what he could; and that which was made by him, he left to those of the future as a heavenly image. From this, ingenious masters have [taken] a...

->The &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 49th &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; leaf<-

Such poetry was written about her: that she, because of her burning eyes which shone like the morning star; because of such light that had never before been seen by any mortal man; because of the remarkable radiance of her entire face; because of her long, gold-colored hair flying about her shoulders, girt and adorned with sweet-sounding cymbals <note>This likely refers to small decorative bells or metallic ornaments common in high-status medieval dress</note>; because of her sweet voice; because of her praiseworthy conduct and good bearing; because of her rose-colored, sweet-smelling <note>original: "wolſchmeckenden," which can mean both fragrant and pleasant-tasting</note> mouth; because of her glistening forehead; and her white throat like ivory; and because of the unprecedented loveliness of her breasts; [it was said] that she was born of the god Jupiter and Leda. [Jupiter] had transformed himself into a swan, and Leda was changed into an eagle <note>While standard Greek myth usually keeps Leda human, this 15th-century German version describes her turning into an eagle to match Jupiter's bird form</note> and laid two eggs. From the one came Helen; from the other, Castor; both of whom are described as immortal. By this they wish to show that the beauty of this same Helen cannot be depicted by any man with brushes and colors, since the highest god Jupiter himself formed her with all beauty, without flaw.

Because of this wonderful beauty, Theseus was moved to travel from Athens to Laconia <note>The region of Greece where Sparta is located</note>. And during the times of the greatest joy of all the royal knighthood, he boldly abducted the young, tender maiden by force and led her home. And although he could take nothing from her beyond a kiss, her chastity was nonetheless somewhat diminished <note>original: "vermercket," meaning her reputation was stained or her purity was put in doubt by the association</note> to some degree.

And shortly thereafter, while Theseus was away from home, her brothers demanded that she be returned. This was carried out without objection by the mother of Theseus, who was named Aethra <note>original: "Alathia"; a variant of Aethra, the mother of Theseus who looked after Helen during her captivity</note>—although some say that Proteus, the king of Egypt, brought her back to her land. Finally, when she was of marriageable age, she was wedded to Menelaus, the king of the Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans</note>, to whom she bore their only daughter, named Hermione. After these things...

**After several years,** when Paris had returned again to the city of **Ilium** <note>original: "yliū"; another name for Troy</note>, he who—because of the dream his pregnant mother had <note>Hecuba dreamed she gave birth to a burning torch that destroyed Troy; this was interpreted as an omen that her son would cause the city's downfall</note>—was cast out into the forest of **Ida** to the wild animals by the command of his father, **Priam**. And after his unrecognized brother, **Hector**, was overcome by him in wrestling and fighting, and he was recognized as her own son by **Hecuba** by the cradle and other signs and was taken back in, he began to think of the promise of the most beautiful woman made by **Venus** <note>the Roman name for Aphrodite</note> in exchange for the judgment of beauty and the gift of the golden apple. 

And after that, when he had arranged for a great fleet to be built in the forest of **Ida**, he traveled across the sea, accompanied by a royal army, into the land of **Greece** to bring back his sister **Hesione**, who had formerly been carried away by **Telamon** during the first conquest of the city. There, he was magnificently and well received and hosted by **Menelaus**. 

But when he saw **Helen**, shining with heavenly beauty and showing herself alluringly in royal attire—and looking at him somewhat longingly—he was captured in his spirit. He gained hope from her flirtatious manners, her sparkling eyes, and her passionate gestures of love, with which she completely fixed the burning flames of desire in his heart. 

Fortune also granted its favor to these things; for it happened by chance that **Menelaus** had to travel on great business to **Crete**, which we now call **Candia**. He left Paris in his palace, which caused the two of them, passionately ignited in ill-fated love, to depart from **Laconia** <note>The region of Greece where Sparta was located</note> and sail across the sea toward **Troy** at night with a large part of Menelaus’s royal treasure. 

Thus Paris brought into the city of **Ilium**—which we now call **Troy**—the **burning torch** <note>This refers back to the mother's dream mentioned at the start; Helen is the metaphorical "torch" that will burn the city</note> with which the city...

### Of Circe, the daughter of the sun. The 36th chapter.

[Woodcut illustration depicting the sorceress Circe on the right, facing four men on the left. Three of the men have been transformed, having the heads of a boar, a wolf, and a ram. The man in the center retains his human head and is labeled "Ulysses". The woman is labeled "Circe".]

Circe, because of her sorcery, is to this very day a widely renowned woman. According to the poets' tales, she was the daughter of the Sun and Perse, a poetess whose father was Oceanus; and her brother was Aeetes, who was the king in Colchis. However, in truth, she was called the daughter of the Sun because she was uniquely enlightened with beauty, and the power of herbs was known to her above others. Furthermore, in all her actions and behavior, she was extremely rational. The Sun gives all these things to children who are born under its best influence, as the astrologers <note>the text breaks off here at the bottom of the page</note>

<meta>This page continues the biographical accounts of famous women, likely from a translation of Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women). The previous page would have introduced the figure of Circe, though this page begins with her arrival in Italy.</meta>

The 51st Leaf

Regarding how she left the island of Colchis <note>A region on the coast of the Black Sea, famous in Greek mythology as the home of Medea and the Golden Fleece.</note> and came to Italy, I do not recall reading. But all ancient histories testify that her dwelling was on the mountain of the Volscians <note>The Volsci were an ancient Italic tribe in central Italy.</note>, which to this day has retained the name **Mount Circe** <note>Now known as Monte Circeo.</note>. And though one finds nothing but fictional tales about this highly famous lady, they should nevertheless be interpreted according to the truth as far as we are able, so that readers may be informed of the reality <note>original: "irzſal" (error/delusion). The author aims to explain the "truth" behind the myth.</note>.

It is said of her that all sailors who came to that mountain—which once was an island, but is now a coastal shore—either by accidental storms or by their own will and purpose, were transformed by her through sorcery, using spells and potions, into wild, cruel beasts, each according to his nature. Among these were also the companions of **Ulysses** <note>original: "Vlixes", the Latin name for the Greek hero Odysseus.</note>. But Ulysses was protected by the counsel of **Mercury** <note>The messenger god (Hermes), who gave Ulysses the herb "moly" to resist Circe's magic.</note>. He also subdued her with his sword and threats of death, so that she was forced to return his companions to their human forms. He also remained for a whole year in her company and fathered by her a son, **Telegonus** <note>original: "Theologonum"</note>, and then departed from her, gifted with great counsel and wisdom.

The lady was powerful in wealth, in reason, words, and deeds; she resided not far from the city of **Gaeta** in **Campania**. Many were moved to see her because of her beauty and her flattering, lovely manners and gestures, by which she could so present and conduct herself that everyone hoped to win special favor and love from her. There was no one for whom it was not easy to fulfill her commands according to her will. Yet her mind was always set on keeping the purity of her body unsullied <note>The author here provides a "rational" explanation for the myth: that Circe was actually a virtuous or highly selective woman whose "magic" was simply her overwhelming charm and intellect.</note>. Therefore, she intended to send her lovers away <note>original: "geloten", possibly referring to dismissals or specific departures.</note> so that they would not be overcome, and commanded...

# The 52nd leaf.

...some to conduct merchant trade, for which reason they were said to have been turned into dogs. For all merchants learn to chatter, talk a lot, and bark like dogs until they sell off <note>The text uses "vertreÿben," meaning to distribute or sell off inventory.</note> their goods. Therefore, Mercury, the god of merchants, is also painted with a dog’s head. <note>This likely refers to the historical blending of the Roman Mercury with the Egyptian Anubis, known as Hermanubis.</note>

To some, she commanded that they practice knighthood for her sake; these showed themselves to be more combative and stronger than their own nature would have granted them. Therefore, they were said to be transformed into lions. Others she commanded to dwell with her; these were the companions of Ulysses <note>original: "vlixis", the Latin name for the Greek hero Odysseus</note> whom she allowed to live beautifully according to every pleasure, because of the desire she had for Ulysses. Therefore, they were said to be transformed into swine, for they lived in excessive pleasure at all times, just like swine. And she gave many other similar commands to her lovers, who, according to the nature of her commands, might be judged to have been transformed into these or other animals.

¶ O you reader and listener of this story, mark how many Circes there are on earth who even today, with their unchaste gestures and unseemly finery, entice men to their evil love and pleasure, yet still wish to be called pious. This, however, is not entirely pure, for unchaste gestures bring about evil works.

¶ Oh, how many men and youths are daily transformed into lions, dogs, swine, monkeys, and fools <note>original: "gauch," literally "cuckoo," a traditional German term for a fool or someone easily deceived.</note>! They ought rightly to stop up their ears so that they might not hear the flattering song of the sirens, if they wished to remain untransformed. Just as Ulysses did through the wisdom given to him by Mercury, with which his companions were also released by him, according to the instruction of the history. 

It is also said of this same woman that she was the wife of Picus, King of the Latins <note>original: "latinorum pici"</note>, who was born of Saturn; and she taught him to foretell future things from the song of birds. And because he...

## About Camilla, Queen of the Volscians.
### Chapter 37.

[Woodcut illustration depicting a scene from the life of Camilla. On the left, two knights on horseback fight near a city wall labeled "Camilla". In the foreground, a man labeled "Metabus" throws a spear across a river; an infant labeled "Camilla" is tied to the spear.]

**Camilla**, the highly renowned maiden of worthy memory, was the daughter of **Metabus**, the ancient king of the **Volscians**, and **Casmilla** his wife. Her mother ended her life at the birth of this child, and in her memory, Metabus removed the letter "s" from her name, so that she <note>The text cuts off here, explaining the transition from the mother's name, Casmilla, to the daughter's name, Camilla</note>

<summary>This page concludes the myth of King Picus’s transformation into a bird by Circe and introduces Camilla, the famous warrior-queen of the Volscians. It details her naming by her father Metabus following the death of her mother.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the noblewoman Camilla, a character from classical mythology (found in Virgil's Aeneid), following her lineage or the circumstances of her birth mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

->[61]<-

->**The 53rd leaf**<-

...would be named Camilla. For this same maiden, from the day of her birth, fortune was harsh and ill-intentioned. For not long after her mother's death, Metabus was suddenly driven out of his kingdom by an uprising among his most powerful lords and citizens. He set out in flight, taking nothing with him except his small daughter, who was dearer to him than any worldly goods. Because of her, he lamented his misfortune less, provided that he could take her with him into exile.

As the poor man escaped on foot, carrying his only daughter in his arms, he came to the flowing water called Damascenus <note>This likely refers to the River Amasenus in Italy</note>, which at that time had swollen so much from heavy rains that he could not swim across it with his daughter. Through divine inspiration—for God did not wish such a future, world-famous noble maiden to die such a shameful death in the water—he devised a plan: he wrapped her in bark with bast <term>bast: the tough, fibrous inner bark of trees used for binding</term> and tied her to a spear <note>Original: "stange," referring here to the wooden shaft of a spear or pole</note> which he happened to be carrying with him. 

He then vowed the child to the goddess Diana <note>The Roman goddess of the hunt and protector of virgins</note> if she would keep her alive, and he threw the spear with the child with all the strength of his body as far as he could into the water, and quickly swam after her. And when, through God’s help, he reached her again unharmed, he was somewhat comforted <note>Original: "erstrewet," meaning his sorrow was dispersed or he was encouraged</note> in his exile. 

He went with her into a wild forest to live in a cave, where with great effort he nourished her with the milk of wild animals. When this same Camilla had grown older, she began to cover her body with the skins of wild animals that she herself had shot with a bow. She practiced this daily, as well as throwing with a sling and chasing and catching wild animals; and above all things, she cherished her purity <note>This refers to her vow of virginity and dedication to Diana</note>.

<summary>This page recounts the exile of King Metabus and the miraculous rescue of his infant daughter, Camilla, whom he ties to a spear and throws across a flooded river. It describes her upbringing in the wilderness as a huntress dedicated to the goddess Diana.</summary>

Folio 53 [Handwritten: 62]

...expressing <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's discussion on the education of girls.</note> what they should learn there, and what they ought to do in their father’s house; what was expected in the temples, at dances, and other festivities where they are judged and seen by everyone. They should always despise idleness, <term>gluttony</term> <note>Original: "schleckmeüln," referring to a love of sweets or dainty foods, often viewed as a vice of excess.</note>, excessive joy, too much dancing, and the company of young men. They should remember that fulfilling every pleasure serves little toward the preservation of chastity. Therefore, being adorned with commendable chastity through reason, they may happily be entered into holy matrimony with the counsel of their friends.

For this reason, her long and frequently tested, preserved purity remains deeply inscribed in the memory of mankind for all eternity, serving as an instruction for other widows. Although Lycophron <note>A Greek poet from the 3rd century BCE whose work often featured obscure mythological variations</note>, a "modern" Greek poet, levels certain accusations against her in his poem, these marks are unfairly placed upon her; for it is unbelievable that a woman so long proven in honor would easily allow herself to be defiled.

# The 56th Page

[The memory of] this woman has remained among people because of the wars and battles of Aeneas against Turnus on her account, rather than for any other praiseworthy deeds performed by her. This same **Lavinia**, <note>Lavinia was the daughter of King Latinus and became the ancestral mother of the Roman line.</note> because of the outstanding adornment of her beauty and also because she was the expected heir to her father’s kingdom, was passionately desired as a wife by **Turnus**, the fierce <term>king of the Rutuli</term> <note>The Rutuli were an ancient Italian tribe settled in Latium.</note>. Regarding this, **Amata**, the young woman’s mother, gave him great hope and favor because he was the son of her daughter <note>original: "tochter sun"; while the text says "daughter's son," historical tradition often identifies Turnus as Amata’s nephew (her sister’s son).</note> by another man.

But **Latinus**, through his practice of knowing future things for certain, had heard from the response of the gods <note>Referring to an oracle or divine prophecy.</note> that his daughter should be wed to a foreign prince; therefore, he delayed the marriage for many days. And as soon as **Aeneas**, a fugitive from **Troy**, had come into **Italy** and requested friendship and peace from Latinus—which was granted to him—and because of his noble lineage and because [Latinus] recognized this through the aforementioned prophecy, he gave him his only daughter as a wife. 

Because of this, a great war arose between **Aeneas** and **Turnus**. And when, through hard battles with much bloodshed and the killing of the noblest men, the **Trojans** prevailed and were completely victorious, **Lavinia** was given to **Aeneas** with a festive wedding celebration. Because of this, **Amata** was cast into such mourning and sorrow that she ended her life with a rope. 

Yet some say she <note>Lavinia</note> was given to him before the battles. But however that may be, it is known that **Lavinia** was made pregnant by him, and before she gave birth to the child, **Aeneas** drowned in the water called **Numicus**. <note>The Numicus is a small river in ancient Latium where Aeneas was said to have vanished during a battle.</note> And **Ascanius**, the son of Aeneas, took over the kingdom; but **Lavinia** was fearful of him, for he was her stepson, and therefore she fled...

This same high-born lady, acting with a fatherly spirit and the highest wisdom, governed well and laudably until her son Silvius grew in years and wisdom enough to be fit to possess the kingdom. Yet some say that when she was called back from the woods, she was married to Melampus, and Silvius was raised with brotherly loyalty and friendship by Ascanius.

# The 57th leaf

**D**ido, who was previously called Elissa, was a queen and the founder of the city of Carthage. I must extend the praise of this same woman a little further with my writing, so that I might somewhat erase the stains <note>The author is defending Dido's reputation against the story told by the poet Virgil in the *Aeneid*, which portrays her as breaking her vow of chastity; historical accounts often emphasized her faithfulness to her late husband.</note> that have been unjustly cast upon her widowhood <term>widowhood: "wittwenstůl," literally "widow's chair," referring to her social and legal status as a chaste widow</term> by some. To speak from the beginning: the Phoenicians, that highly intelligent and ingenious people, traveled in ancient times from farthest Egypt across the sea into Syria, where they built many magnificent cities. Among them, the supreme ruler was King Agenor, who was illustrious and widely famous not only in his own time but even up to our own; Dido took her origin from his lineage. And when her father Belus had conquered the island of Cyprus, he ended his days and entrusted the young woman and her brother Pygmalion—who was older—to the lords and citizens of the land. Immediately thereafter, they placed him upon his father's royal throne to rule the kingdom. They gave Elissa, his sister, as a wife to the highest-ranking man after the king in Tyre, named Acerbas, or Sychaeus, or Sicharius <note>original: "aterbas oder sicheus oder sicharius"</note>, who was the administrator of the highest office of the priesthood of Hercules. They loved each other holily in their marriage. However, Pygmalion was possessed by greed <term>greed: "geytigkeit," an old term for avarice or extreme stinginess</term> beyond all other mortal men, so much so that he believed he could never be satisfied by gold. Now, Acerbas was exceedingly rich in gold. But when he discovered his brother-in-law’s greed, he kept it as hidden as he could; yet he could not suppress the common rumors of his wealth. Because of this, Pygmalion sought a way to obtain the gold and had him secretly murdered while he was unsuspecting. When Elissa became aware of this, she bore the death of her husband so intolerably that little remained before she were...

<summary>This page introduces Dido (Elissa), tracing her Phoenician lineage and her marriage to the wealthy priest Acerbas. It describes the greed of her brother, King Pygmalion, which leads him to secretly murder Dido's husband for his gold.</summary>

<keywords>Dido, Elissa, Carthage, Phoenicians, Pygmalion, Acerbas, Sychaeus, Tyre, greed, Phoenician history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Elissa (Dido), following the murder of her husband Acerbas (Sychaeus) by her brother, King Pygmalion of Tyre. The text details her transformation from a grieving widow into a cunning leader.</meta>

[Handwritten at top right: 66]

### The 58th leaf

...died because of him. But when she had spent much time occupied with her tears and had often fruitlessly called upon her dearest husband **Acerbas** <note>Also known in classical mythology as Sychaeus; he was a priest of Hercules and very wealthy</note>, and at all times severely cursed her brother—desiring more, she was warned in her sleep. Just as she had also often wisely considered while awake to flee from her brother, so that she would not also be killed on account of his great greed, she therefore put aside womanly weakness and fortified her heart with a manly spirit. For this reason, her name **Elissa** was abandoned, and she was called **Dido**, which in her language <note>Phoenician</note> means as much as a "manly woman."

She began, above all things, to consider how she might win over the princes of the cities who had been burdened and harmed in many ways by her brother, so that they would agree to her undertaking. When this was accomplished, Dido took all the treasures of her husband and as much as she could take away from her brother, and had them secretly carried into the ships which he had prepared to send her into exile. And with well-considered cleverness, she had many of the sacks and small chests filled with sand and made very heavy, to pretend that her treasures were inside them. She had these carried openly into the ships and thus sailed away from there with all the people who were in agreement with her; the greater part of them did not know what her intention was. 

But as soon as they had reached the high sea, she ordered those same weighted sacks and chests to be thrown into the water. Many people who had not been informed of her secret were amazed by this. And upon this, she spoke to her fellow voyagers thus: "Now I will gladly suffer the death that I have often desired, since I was given the opportunity to sink into the sea the treasure for which my dearest husband died at the hands of the greedy **Pygmalion**. But I have compassion and mercy for..."

<summary>This page recounts how Elissa took the name Dido ("manly woman") and fled her murderous brother Pygmalion. She used a clever ruse involving sacks of sand to convince her followers she had cast her wealth into the sea to prevent her brother from ever obtaining it.</summary>

<keywords>Dido, Elissa, Pygmalion, Acerbas, Sychaeus, Tyre, Phoenicia, manly woman, treasure, cleverness, sea voyage</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Dido’s flight from Tyre after the murder of her husband Sychaeus by her brother Pygmalion. She is currently deceiving her followers and the sailors to ensure their loyalty in exile.</meta>

### The 58th page

...to you, for I know well: if we return to my trapped <note>The German "gefangen" suggests her brother is snared by his own greed, or perhaps refers to the sailors being caught in his trap if they return.</note> brother, he will not leave any of you, along with me, unpunished by a cruel death once he perceives that the treasure has been sunk. To prevent this, I have set my mind never to return to my brother again, and I offer myself, to the best of my ability, to be honorable and helpful to all who wish to be with me, wherever we may go, and I shall never desert you. 

When the sailors heard this—although they were reluctant to leave their ancestral homes—nevertheless, out of fear of the death they expected from Pygmalion, they easily consented to travel with her into exile <term>exile: the original German "ellend" literally means 'other land' and refers to the hardship of being forced from one's home</term> and turned their ships. Through the lady's clever deception <note>original: "unördichten," literally 'un-poeting' or a fabricated story</note>, they came to the island of Cyprus. 

There they found young women sitting on the shore according to their custom, offering the guests lovely joy and pastime to honor the goddess Venus <note>original: "veneri"</note> and as a special sacrifice so that they might maintain the human race. <note>This refers to the ancient practice of sacred fertility rites associated with the temple of Aphrodite/Venus on Cyprus.</note> She took all these women with her, and also the high priest with all his household; from him, she learned of many future things that would arise from her flight. 

Thus sailing, they left Candia <note>original: "Candiam," the medieval name for the island of Crete</note> behind them and kept Sicily to their right hand, and arrived at the shores of Africa. There, they intended to find rest from their great labor and safety for their ships, and so they landed there. 

Immediately, there was a great gathering of the people living nearby, out of a desire to see the strange guests. Some brought food, others brought various merchandise as is customary, and through this, they established mutual goodwill and favor on both sides. Through friendly conversation, the travelers realized that it was the residents' good will that the guests should remain among them. To this end, the people of Utica <note>original: "vticenses"</note> also gave them counsel, who themselves had once come from...

...they had traveled from Tyre to that place. And although she learned that her brother was preparing to wage war against her, she remained undaunted in her resolve to stay in that same location. So that she would not be suspected of planning any great undertaking, she requested only as much land for her seat of government as she could encompass with an oxhide. And when that amount of ground was sold to her, she had the hide cut into such small, thin strips <note>This refers to the famous legend of "Dido’s Problem," where she used geometry to maximize the area of her new kingdom</note> that she enclosed far more land with it than the sellers had ever imagined. And when she began to dig there, she found at the very beginning a horse's head <note>original: "roskopff"</note>. According to the interpretation of the soothsayers, this signified that a warlike and powerful city would be built there. She named the city Carthage <note>original: "carthaginem"</note> and the citadel within it Byrsa <term>Byrsa: A Greek-sounding word meaning "oxhide," referring to the strips used to claim the land</term>, named so because of the oxhide.

Then, for the first time, she allowed her companions to see the treasure she had previously kept hidden, and she encouraged them to be joyful and to have good hope for their future fortune. Immediately, through the willing labor of many, the temple, the city walls, the public buildings, and other structures were quickly raised up. Laws were established for the common people, and a social order was granted so that they might live civilly and in harmony. Because of this—as well as her exceeding beauty, noble bearing, high virtue, and supreme constancy—her honor was so elevated that her praise was spread throughout all of Africa <note>original: "affricam"</note>.

On account of this fame—and because all the men of Africa are inclined toward impulsive physical desires—the King of the Musicani <note>The Maxitani or Mauritani, a local North African tribe</note> was fervently moved to seek her hand. He intended to win her through threats and sent word to the leaders of the city: unless they gave him the Queen as a wife, he would destroy the city. When they heard this, they were deeply troubled in their hearts. For...

<summary>This page describes the legendary founding of Carthage, detailing Dido's clever use of an oxhide to claim land and the subsequent growth of the city. Her success attracts the unwanted and threatening attention of a local African king who demands her hand in marriage.</summary>

<keywords>Queen Dido, Carthage, Byrsa, oxhide, founding of cities, African history, omens, ancient law, King of the Musicani</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of a queen (likely Saint Ursula or a similar figure from a legendary history) facing a political and moral dilemma involving a foreign king's proposal.</meta>

# Leaf 59

They knew the holy, immovable resolution regarding the chastity of their lady. Against this, they feared the king; they worried that if his will were not followed, they would be completely destroyed <note>original: "veratcket," likely meaning ruined or perished</note> by him. And when the queen asked the cause of their mourning, they did not dare to tell the truth. Instead, they devised a trick by which they might achieve their purpose.

And they said the king's demand was that she should choose one of two things: either she should send him some of the best people to teach him and his people how to live according to their established laws—because he wished to set aside his terrifying and dishonorable nature—or else they should choose the destruction of the city through war.

Now, they were troubled because they did not know whom they should appoint to this task, for none among them wanted to leave his home and run to that terrifying <term>tyrant</term> <note>original: "wütrich," a term for a cruel or bloodthirsty ruler</note>. The noble blood <note>This refers to the queen herself, personifying her high birth and character</note> noticed what treachery lay hidden in their words, and she turned boldly toward them and spoke thus:

"O, you manly citizens, what levity is in your minds! O, what sluggishness of body! Do you not know that we are born for our father and for the fatherland? No citizen is worthy of the name unless he dares to suffer not only hardship for the <term>common good</term> <note>original: "gemeinen nucz," a central medieval concept regarding the sacrifice of private interests for the public safety</note>, but even death, if it should happen so. Therefore, go forth joyfully and submit yourselves to these small worries so that you may prevent the devastation of the country."

By this speech, they thought they had sufficiently trapped the queen. For if it were her own advice to suffer worry for the sake of the common good, then it would be a very small sacrifice for her to also take a husband to preserve it. And so, they revealed the king's true desire to her.

But when she began to realize how she had ensnared herself by her own advice, she dared [not] <note>The sentence is incomplete as the page ends</note> such treachery...

<summary>The queen’s advisors, fearing a tyrant king, trick her into advocating for self-sacrifice for the "common good," which they then use as a rhetorical trap to force her into a marriage she wishes to avoid.</summary>

## The 60th leaf

...not to resist her people, and she sighed deeply in her heart and thought of how she might strengthen her resolve to maintain her chastity for her husband. She found what she believed to be the most suitable way to do this. And she gave them this answer: if it were their will for her to marry, and if they would give her a set period of time <note>Literally a "goal" or "deadline" for her mourning to end</note>, she would then enter into marriage again. This request was granted to her. In the meantime, Aeneas came from destroyed Troy, a fugitive by chance, wandering to her shores. He was also well received by her. However, to preserve eternal purity for her husband—as was her constant resolve—and to choose death rather than break it, she had a pile of dry wood carried to the highest point of her dwelling. This was done according to her custom, to make it appear as if she intended to observe the anniversary <note>original: "jarzeit" — a traditional commemorative service or anniversary of a death</note> of her husband Sychaeus <note>original: "Sicheo"</note>. For this purpose, she also prepared all sacrifices and offerings <term>hostien</term> <note>Hostien: from the Latin 'hostia,' referring to sacrificial victims or ritual offerings</note> with ceremonial adornment...

...you have shed. They shall look upon it <note>This likely refers to the blood of Christ or a martyr mentioned on the preceding page.</note> pitiably, and especially those women for whom it was easy not only to take a second husband, but even more so a third. What will they say—I beg you to tell me—at the time of the final accounting <term>accounting of all people</term><note>A reference to the Last Judgment, where all souls are held responsible for their earthly lives.</note> when they see the pagan women standing before them? To those ancient women, Christ was unknown, yet they desired to keep their purity for the sake of earthly honor with such a constant mind and such a strong heart that they preferred to go to their deaths by their own hand <note>A common medieval reference to figures like Lucretia, who committed suicide to preserve her honor.</note> rather than enter into another marriage. 

Yet one of our own women, who are quick to seek clever excuses for their conduct, might speak in this way: "It was necessary for me to take another husband. My father, mother, and brothers had died. I was utterly forsaken by everyone; I had neither means nor help from anyone. Moreover, this person and that person promised me so much good..."

<meta>This page continues a moral discourse on widowhood and chastity, specifically addressing the excuses women use to justify remarrying. It follows a tradition that presents Dido, the legendary Queen of Carthage, as a paragon of faithful widowhood.</meta>

->**The 61st Leaf**<-

...towards the earth. Stop up your ears and stand firm against the cloudbursts of flattering suitors, and turn your cloak against the wind; thus you will be preserved. Perhaps another woman comes and says: "Alas, I possessed great riches, much landed property, beautiful houses, royal furnishings, and an abundance of gold and silver; I did not wish these to pass away without an heir. Therefore, I took another husband, so that I might leave heirs behind me." 

O foolish desire! Did Dido <note>In many medieval accounts, such as those by Boccaccio, Dido was praised for committing suicide to remain faithful to her dead husband's memory and avoid a forced remarriage, rather than for grief over Aeneas.</note> not have an entire kingdom without bodily heirs? Did she not have royal riches? Yet she did not wish to become a mother, for she thought in her supreme wisdom that nothing more foolish could happen than to break oneself apart to build up another. How could she then have bound the purity of her heart just so she might provide an heir for her heart? 

Because of this, you have much wealth; do not lose it, but rather give it to the poor children of God, so that you may build yourself a hall in eternity that no enemy can destroy. In this way, you may powerfully protect and keep your purity unassailed. 

Then comes the third woman and says: "I was compelled by my friends <note>In this period, "friends" (German: *Freunde*) usually refers to one's kinsfolk or family members.</note>; my father wished it no other way, and my mother commanded me; it was the common counsel of all my relatives. Otherwise, I never would have taken another husband." 

Such words might be offered to fools, for if one's own desire and <term>lust<gloss>original: *gaile*, referring to carnal desire or animal heat</gloss></term> had not been present, she could have well rejected all the talk of father, mother, and all friends with a single "No." Dido preferred to die rather than live stained. And yet you would rather live in shame than say no. 

A fourth woman might come, who thinks herself quite clever, and says: "I was young, and it is well known how young blood seethes, which I could not withstand." Thus she says...

<meta>This page continues the moralizing discourse regarding widowhood and chastity, likely drawing on Boccaccio’s "Concerning Famous Women." ### Leaf 61

The Apostle Paul, one of the twelve, said: **it is better to marry again than to burn** <note>original: "widerūb gemähelt werden wann brünnen"; a reference to 1 Corinthians 7:9, often cited in the Middle Ages as a concession for those who could not remain celibate.</note>. So I have followed his advice, although this was said because I did not recognize the chastity of our women. Even Dido, in the blooming youth of her warm blood, strengthened her spirit to constantly maintain her purity <note>In the version of the Dido myth used here, she is a model of chastity who died to avoid a second marriage, rather than the lover of Aeneas portrayed by Virgil.</note>. It is a great evil that you prefer to use the holy advice of Paul to justify your unchastity, especially since you have never heeded the actual intention for which that advice was given.

And it would be more praiseworthy to consider how we might diligently restore the lost strength of the body with good food—so much so that the blood often becomes heated in desire—so that we might tame and cool the raging, tempting blood through labor and the reduction of food <note>The author suggests fasting and hard work as a "coolant" for physical lust.</note>. 

The pagan maiden Dido was able to withstand and conquer the raging blood for the sake of temporal honor; why should a Christian woman not be able to do the same to earn eternal honor and glory? But alas, as we seek ways to hide this misdeed from God—to whom all things are manifest—we strip ourselves of temporal honor and eternal joy. We force ourselves to fall into the depths from which no one can rise.

Therefore, all who look upon the dead body of Dido should rightly be ashamed, especially when they consider the cause of her death: that they should be surpassed in praiseworthy chastity by a pagan woman. 

No widow should think that she has done enough for her husband simply by weeping for him and wearing a black garment and a wide veil <term>sturtz: a specific type of stiffened, folded headdress or veil worn by widows in the 15th century</term>. Her shared love should last until death. If she wishes to be promised to her husband in true friendship and love, she should not think of entering into another marriage—as many do, more for the sake of fulfilling their evil physical lust than for the holiness of marriage. And...

->The 62nd page<-

**N**icaula was born in the distant land of the Moors. Because she emerged from such a wild, desolate, and <term>inhuman land</term><note>The author uses "unmenschlich" (inhuman) to describe the harsh environment or the perceived lack of "civilized" customs in distant Africa, common in medieval European perspectives.</note> in terms of the customs and nature of its people, yet attained such great wisdom, skill, and high dignity, she is rightly celebrated far and wide above all others. She lived during the time when the Pharaohs of Egypt were passing away, and she was herself descended from their lineage. It is also said of her that she was a queen in Ethiopia (which is the land of the Moors), in Egypt, and in Arabia. Furthermore, she possessed great wealth on the island of Meroë, where the River Nile flows. For this reason, she had such a great abundance of gold and silver that she was estimated to surpass the whole world in riches.

Yet, despite such great wealth, she was never idle, nor was she found in <term>womanly softness</term><note>The original "weiblicher waichmütigkeit" reflects a period stereotype that women were prone to idleness or emotional fragility; the author highlights Nicaula as an exception to this supposed rule.</note>. Instead, she was always diligent and tireless in the practice of learning wisdom and the arts. Indeed, she is so highly praised in the Holy Scriptures that she is the one named **Sheba**. 

When she heard, through the far-reaching fame of the world, of the supreme wisdom of King Solomon, she was filled with such great wonder that she set out from nearly the ends of the earth. Leaving her kingdom of Egypt and the Red Sea, she traveled through the Arabian desert with a royal retinue at great expense to come to Jerusalem to see him. So impressive was she that even the wealthiest King Solomon marveled at this woman's great power. When she had been received by him with great dignity according to royal honors, and had been well answered and instructed regarding several of her questions, she publicly acknowledged his wisdom. She declared it to be far surpassing all human skill, intellect, and wisdom, and stated that it undoubtedly came not from human learning, but from the divine...

¶ Of Pamphile. The 42nd [Chapter].

**P**amphile was a Greek woman, but from which region she came has been lost to antiquity. Although she is not found to be highly praised for her noble lineage, she nevertheless increased the common good, for which she should rightly receive some praise. For no discovery is so small that it is not a sign of a subtle mind <note>term: "subtle mind" (subtiler sÿnn) — here meaning a clever, inventive, or ingenious intellect.</note> for which a person is to be honored. Since this Pamphile was the first to discover how to peel cotton out of the bolls, and how to card, comb, and spin it, and make cloth from it—all of which was unknown to the world before her discovery—she is rightly placed among other ingenious women and honored with fitting praise according to her merit.

Yet, so that he would not appear to be raging too fiercely against his brother, he forced him into a position where he allowed him to live a life of leisure <term>müssiggeend</term> <note>Meaning a private life without political power or office.</note> dwelling upon his estates.
