### The 64th leaf.

...she was given to the fortress <note>Referring to the Temple of Vesta.</note> of the goddess and placed with other young women in her cloister <note>The author uses the medieval German word *kloster* (cloister/convent) to describe the Roman Temple of the Vestal Virgins.</note> where she was required to vow eternal chastity. But as she grew older, she was overcome by the desires of **Lady Venus** <note>A personification of sexual desire.</note>—though in what manner it is not known. However, her heavy body proved that she had come to a man’s embrace, for she gave birth at once to **Remus** and **Romulus**, the beginners, founders, and fathers of the mighty city of Rome. Because of this guilt, although she was a royal lady, the children were cast away into the wilderness among the beasts, and according to the ancient laws, the mother was buried alive. And although her body was buried, her name is highly honored in eternal memory through the great history of her children, which the tyrant <note>Amulius, the great-uncle of Romulus and Remus, who ordered their abandonment.</note> would have gladly extinguished.

When I consider this Rhea and see how many "works of Venus" those holy clothes cover, I cannot refrain from mocking her folly. There are some greedy people who, in order to save money, force their children into cloisters under the guise of devotion. These parents lead the young ones so strictly in their childhood days that they later enter [the cloister] more willingly, enticed by the hope of a softer life, not knowing what they are doing. They then say they have sacrificed a child to Almighty God, so that through their prayers they might obtain much grace from God for both the dead and the living. With this and similar excuses, they wish to cover their evil intentions.

O unthinking heads! Do they not know what idle women contemplate while in the service of Venus? How eager they are to practice their <term>knighthood</term> <note>The original term *ritterschafft* (knighthood/chivalry) is used here as a metaphor for sexual activity or secular romantic life.</note>, so much so that they often prefer the strict life of common secular "knighthood" to the life of the cloister. And as often as they hear of marriage, or when they [see] worldly...

Page 64

When they look at clothes, golden jewelry, small clasps, belts, and rings—or the like; or when they hear the sound of pipes, or hear of weddings, social gatherings <note>original: "höfflin," referring to courtly or festive social circles</note>, or dances—then they sigh deeply from the bottom of their hearts and complain of their misfortune <note>original: "vngesell," meaning misery or a lack of companionship</note> that they must be robbed of such joys. Then they curse the habit and the scapular <term>scapular: a monastic garment consisting of a long, wide piece of cloth worn over the shoulders</term> along with the veil, as well as their father, mother, and everyone who gave help or advice toward their entering the convent. And their constant thought is how they might decently <note>original: "mit gelimpff," meaning with propriety or through some legitimate excuse</note> leave the cloisters again.

Or otherwise, they think of how they might secretly bring their lovers <term>bulen: a word for sweethearts or lovers, used here to imply illicit romantic interests</term> to them. Because open marriage is closed off to them, they secretly bring the tribute of their service <note>original: "Ritterschafft zeeren," literally 'knightly provisions,' used here metaphorically for devotion or service</note> to Lady Venus <note>the Roman goddess of love and desire</note>. Such is the choral singing of these young nuns, and their devotion and evening prayer—not of all of them, so that I do not overstate the case, but certainly of a great many of them.

Through such "praiseworthy" prayer, their fathers, mothers, and others who helped place them there will climb to heaven just as easily as a cow fits into a mouse-hole <note>a sarcastic folk proverb indicating that such a thing is impossible</note>. ### ¶ About Gaya Cyrilla, the 44th Chapter

**G**aya Cyrilla <note>Also known as Tanaquil, the wife of the fifth King of Rome.</note> was the beloved wife of the Roman king, Tarquinius Priscus. She was so ingenious—even though she was the wife of a Roman king—that she was never found idling in her royal halls. Instead, she dedicated herself to the **labor of wool**, which in those times was considered honorable for noble Roman women. She showed herself so diligent in this work that her name is honored to this day for her domestic industry. 

She was also so highly esteemed during her lifetime that all the Roman people held her in honor and dignity; she was so loved by everyone that they established a public decree: whenever a new bride was led into her husband's house for the first time, she should be asked at the threshold what her name was, and she should immediately answer that her name was **Gaya**. This served as a reminder <gloss>admonition</gloss> that the bride should follow the example of Gaya’s life. Although this custom is little valued by the ignorant, to the wise it is a great sign of the high wisdom and reason of this praiseworthy lady, for which she is rightly not forgotten.

<summary>This page argues that only truly committed women should enter convents and introduces Gaya Cyrilla (Tanaquil) as a model of Roman domestic virtue and industry.</summary>

### The 65th Leaf

Ovid’s *Tristia*: 
What did Sappho teach Lesbia, if not how to love girls? <note>original Latin: "Lesbia quid docuit saphos nisi amare puellas." This quote from the Roman poet Ovid refers to Sappho’s reputation as a teacher of poetry and themes of female affection on the island of Lesbos.</note>

### Chapter 45: Concerning Sappho the Poetess

[Woodcut illustration: A woman labeled "Sappho" sits in a room playing a lute. To her left is a bed and a chest; to her right, a book rests on a lectern and a harp lies on the floor. In the background, a man and a woman embrace in a doorway.]

Sappho the maiden was born in the city of Mytilene <note>Mytilene is the capital of the Greek island of Lesbos.</note>. We find nothing more recorded regarding her birth. However, when we examine her artistry and the books she composed—even though the greater part of them have been lost to the ravages of time—we can easily gather from what remains that she was possessed of high intellect and was descended from noble blood. For no common or peasant lineage could ever have produced such works. She was so diligent that she would not be satisfied merely with reading letters and learning how to pronounce them together, but was driven by a fervent will toward deeper learning.

<summary>This page introduces the Greek poet Sappho, emphasizing her noble origins and her intellectual drive which far exceeded the basic literacy expected of her time.</summary>

<keywords>Sappho, Mytilene, Ovid, women's education, poetry, noble birth, classical antiquity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Greek poet Sappho and begins the account of Tullia, the daughter of the Roman King Servius Tullius. The previous page likely described Sappho's poetic mastery and the high esteem in which she was held by her contemporaries.</meta>

73
[74]

### The 66th Leaf

...it was highly esteemed by them. Because of such high art and intellect <note>The original German "vnuñfft" (Vernunft) here refers to her wisdom or mental capacity</note>, a statue of honor was erected in her praise, named "Sappho's Column" after her name, and she was counted among the best poets. Such honors cannot be compared to the imperial or papal crowns, nor to bishops' miters, nor to the palms of victory.

But if we are to believe ancient writings, just as she learned so well and blessedly, she was also captured by the unhappy love of a young man. She was so bound by this love that she could not endure his absence with any degree of patience. Because of this, she also composed new, masterful, and sorrowful poems in which she lamented her displeasure and misery. She lived during the times of Ezekiel and Daniel <note>Prophets of the Old Testament; the author is synchronizing classical history with biblical chronology</note>, which is 4,630 years from the beginning of the world.

<meta>This page continues the history of the early Roman kings, specifically focusing on the transition from the reign of the fifth king, Tarquinius Priscus, to the sixth, Servius Tullius, and the role of the infamous Tullia.</meta>

Leaf LXVI

**Tullia**, who through her grave and evil deeds provided the reason for the Roman Empire to be transformed from a state of private ownership <note>original: *eygenschafft*; meaning the monarchy's absolute rule</note> into a state of freedom <note>This refers to the transition from the Roman Monarchy to the Roman Republic.</note>, was a daughter of **Servius Tullius**, the sixth king after **Romulus**. She was born of his wife, the daughter of the fifth king, **Tarquinius Priscus**. She was of a high-minded spirit and sharp intellect, worthy of much praise had she not turned those qualities toward murderous evil. 

For at the time when her father, **Servius Tullius**, was elected king through the common favor of the Roman people and the arrangement of King **Tarquinius** and his wife **Tanaquil**—as had been foretold earlier by the burning head of the child **Servius** while he lay in his cradle <note>A famous Roman omen: flames allegedly played around the head of the sleeping boy Servius without harming him, signaling his future royalty.</note>, for which reason the king later gave him his daughter—there were two brothers, **Aruns** and **Lucius**, born of the royal lineage of the **Tarquins**. These two, and especially **Lucius**, bore the rule of **Servius** with great resentment and believed that by hereditary right the kingdom should belong to them. 

They considered how to prevent him from ruling and deliberated whether it would be better to kill **Servius** or the king <note>Tarquinius Priscus</note>. They judged it better for their purpose to execute the king. For they reasoned that if **Servius** died, the king would simply find another son-in-law to whom he would pass the kingdom, thus alienating it from them. 

They arranged for two guards <note>historical accounts identify them as two fierce shepherds</note>, who were well-practiced in swinging an axe <note>original: *bephel*; likely a variant of *Beil* (axe) or *Wurfbeil* (throwing axe)</note>, to go before the king under the guise of a legal dispute, demanding justice. And as the king turned toward one of them to hear his side of the case, the other drove his axe into the king’s head and left it sticking in him. Queen **Tanaquil** immediately ordered everyone to leave who did not belong there and locked the royal hall with great diligence, considering how the— <note>The text cuts off here as the narrative continues on the next page.</note>

He has also now come to himself again; and as we have also cleaned the wounds, all signs are favorable, in the hope that you shall see him soon." With this, their turbulence was stilled. And she meanwhile commanded the people to be subject to Servius by order of the King. 

Thus Servius reigned for some time in the role of a regent <term>regent: original "verweser," an administrator acting on behalf of a ruler who is absent, incapacitated, or—as in this case—secretly deceased</term> for the King, who was nevertheless dead. Leaf 67.

When his death was made known, Servius had so strengthened himself with friends and the favor of the common people that he was proclaimed king by the command of the people and with the consent of the fathers <note>The "fathers" refers to the Roman Senators, or *patres*.</note>. As he now ruled with great power, he did not think to avenge the death of his father-in-law, but rather believed it better to overcome the enemy with kindness than with the zeal of vengeance. And he drew the two fugitive brothers into his friendship and gave them his two daughters, both named Tullia. Yet they were of different disposition and spirit, just as the two brothers were. To Arruns, the kind one, was given the elder, more cunning sister Tullia; and to Lucius, the sharp-witted one of evil schemes, was given as a wife the gentler sister, the younger Tullia. Nevertheless, Lucius constantly considered through various desires how he might come to the kingdom, but his wife was kind. On the other hand, Arruns was kind, but his wife, the elder Tullia, was excessively bold, complaining of her husband’s soft-heartedness—that he had no heart, whereas Lucius was a true man and born of royal blood. She hated her sister because luck had given her such a man, while she herself was burdened with one so soft-hearted and unhelpful to her ambitions. But as evil is naturally drawn to evil, like began to seek out like, as usually happens; and the beginning of all this trouble came from the women. When the bold Tullia had learned all the secrets of her brother-in-law Lucius, she withheld no words from him regarding her own husband and his wife. She told him she had a weakling <note>The original term *postuezler* implies a "cushion-sitter" or a lazy, soft man.</note> who was not worth the name of a man, being of such a soft spirit. Likewise, she said he [Lucius] had a soft-hearted, ignorant wife, and that it would be better for him to be a monk <note>The original text uses *münich* (monk), an anachronism used by the medieval/Renaissance translator to describe someone living a quiet, religious life of retreat.</note> than to waste his days with a woman who was so unlike him in spirit. From that moment, envy against the king began to grow hourly, and Tullia rested neither day nor night, constantly inciting her husband to seize the kingdom. She spoke to him: "You know what I have long lamented—that I did not have a man of strong spirit, just as your wife was discordant in mind to you. For that reason, they have both died. But consider that this did not happen in vain; my lament was that I had a man who did not value himself as worthy of the kingdom. Now I have you, a high-minded and manly man, to whom the kingdom belongs—if you will only recognize that it is yours by right, as you are the closest heir of the <term>Tarquins</term> <note>original: *tarquĩorũ*, referring to the dynasty of Tarquinius Priscus</note>.

Therefore, you should not be idle, if you are indeed the man I intend to be wedded to, and would rather possess the kingdom now than live merely in the hope of future rule. But if your spirit has changed, then all things would become much worse, should you choose to be weak-hearted after the evil we have already committed. Therefore, be manly, considering that the kingdom is destined for you by the gods and belongs to you by inheritance from the Tarquin lineage, so that I may call you my king."

With these and many other rebuking words, she constantly incited the young man so much that he began to think of how to secretly win the people to himself in various ways, reminding them of his royal descent and...

# The 68th Leaf

At times he flattered the king, and at others he bribed the young men; at other times, he promised much with gifts for the future, waiting until a fitting time arrived to carry out his wicked plan. And one day, well-supported by many of his followers, he entered the hall and boldly sat upon the royal throne. He commanded all the fathers <note>original: "vätter"; this refers to the 'Patres' or senators of Rome</note> and the leaders of the city to come immediately to the palace. 

Some, who knew of the plot beforehand, were quick to arrive as they had been expecting it. Others, who were unaware of these matters, came out of fear, while some came simply to witness the strange proceedings. In short, everyone realized that this was a move against King Servius. 

Once the people were assembled, Lucius Tarquinius began to denounce Servius in a long speech, making various accusations to turn the people against him. When the king heard of this, he ran at once into the hall, shouting: "Tarquinius, what is this? With what audacity do you dare, while I still live, to summon the fathers or sit upon my royal throne?" 

Tarquinius answered defiantly that he was occupying his father's royal hereditary throne, from which he—a man born a slave <note>original: "aigen man"; this refers to the legend that Servius Tullius was born to an enslaved woman and rose to power from humble beginnings</note>—had been pushed out by force through a woman's schemes against all right. He claimed that Servius had mocked the royal blood long enough through his various dealings. 

Then a great shout arose from both factions, signaling that the stronger side would now rule. The final moment had come for each side to prove its strength. But Tarquinius was the more powerful; leaping from the throne, he seized Servius, lifted him high, and threw him all the way down from the top steps of the staircase.

### The 69th leaf.

...commanded by his wicked daughter Tullia. Immediately, that same sinful Tullia drove before the palace in a carriage. She called for her husband and addressed him as the first king, just as he had commanded her. But as she was returning to her house and had come to the street called *Vicum Ciprium* <note>original: "vicũ Cipriũ," the Cyprian Way in ancient Rome</note>, the driver pulled back the horses in great terror and showed the woman her slain father, Servius, lying there. 

To establish an eternal memory of such a vile and inhuman sin, that same street was named *Sceleratus Vicus* <note>original: "sceleratus vicus," meaning the Wicked or Accursed Street</note>, which is the "Sinful Street." Because she had already committed evil against her own [previous] husband and sister, she felt no remorse and avoided no further misdeed. It is said of her that she ordered the driver to steer the carriage over the dead body of her father; thus it happened that the carriage, and even her own clothes, were defiled by the blood of her father’s murder.

Driven by even more murderous evil, envious hearts, and ingratitude for the kindness they had received, neither of them wished for their own father and father-in-law to be committed to the earth with well-deserved royal honors. Instead, they commanded that he be left lying shamefully unburied, saying mockingly: "Romulus, the founder of the city of Rome, had no grave, yet he was of better, divine birth. Therefore, this man—born of a servant girl <note>Servius Tullius was legendarily born to a slave woman in the royal household, though he rose to become the sixth king of Rome.</note>—may just as well remain unburied." 

Thus, they would rule through their own power with such arrogance, without any consideration of wise counsel, but rather out of innate evil cunning. For this reason, he was called and named by everyone *Lucius Tarquinius Superbus*, which is "the Haughty" <note>original: "superbus," meaning the Proud or Arrogant</note>. Yet after much evil was committed by them...

### Leaf 69

And because of the deadly evil committed by their son, Sextus Tarquinius <note>Sextus was the son of the last king of Rome, Tarquinius Superbus; his assault on the noblewoman Lucretia was the catalyst for the Roman revolution</note>, against Lucretia, they fled into exile and died miserably. O, deadly evil against nature! How could such a wicked plot against a beast—let alone a father who had always loved his children with great kindness—ever enter a woman’s mind <note>This refers to Tullia the Younger, who allegedly orchestrated the murder of her father, King Servius Tullius, to seize power for her husband</note> without cause? 

But no sin stands alone without being attached to another. Usually, where one evil reaches its intended end, it brings constant provocation toward an even greater evil. Therefore, after the wicked murder of her husband, Aruns, and her sister, she became even more reckless regarding her father’s death. For this reason, the beginning of evil is to be avoided, because the mire of sin is sticky, muddy, and swampy; once one falls in, it is very hard to climb back out. 

So that this evil may be seen as even greater, I will place alongside this betrayal the great loyalty and constancy <term>constancy: the quality of being faithful and dependable</term> of the pure Lucretia, who was harmed in body by that proud lineage but remained unsullied in her spirit.

**Of Lucretia, the 47th Chapter**

**L**ucretia, a leader of Roman feminine virtue and a holy, high ornament of all purity, was the daughter of the noble Roman named Lucretius Spurius Tricipidinus <note>Spurius Lucretius Tricipitinus was a legendary Roman figure and the father of Lucretia; he later became one of the first consuls</note>, and the wife of Collatinus. It is a matter of debate whether she should be praised more for the beauty of her body or for the nobility of her spirit above all other honorable Roman women. At the time when Tarquinius the Proud <note>Tarquinius Superbus, the legendary seventh and final King of Rome, known for his arrogance and tyranny</note> lay before the city of Ardea to conquer it, she moved from the hamlet of Collatia <note>A town near Rome where Lucretia's husband, Collatinus, held property</note> out of the city and into her husband's house. And as the siege of the city was prolonged, it happened that...

### The 70th leaf.

...showed themselves willing. For that reason, she was estimated by many—and rightly so—to be the most honorable. When Collatinus saw how his highly-praised housewife was being lauded, he thought to honor her in return and invited all the royal youths into his house. And in the midst of this, after they had been kindly received and were well and honorably satisfied with sufficient food, Sextus—the son of Tarquinius the arrogant emperor <note>original: "keisers." While the German uses the word for Emperor, Tarquinius Superbus was actually the last King of Rome.</note>—began to cast his unchaste eyes rapaciously upon the honorable beauty of the chaste woman. And he was so ignited by that over-burning, filthy fire that he set his mind on partaking in the desires of her body and defiling her delightful beauty. And if that could not happen with her consent, then it would have to be accomplished by force applied to her.

In a few days thereafter, as the madness began to seize him even more fervently, he rose up secretly by night and rode out from the army <note>original: "hōr" (Heer). At this time, the Roman army was besieging Ardea.</note> toward Collatinus’s house, to Lucretia. By her he was let in and handsomely received without any suspicion of evil, because he was her husband’s closest kinsman.

But when Sextus noticed that the entire household was quiet and now weighed down with sleep, he went into the bedchamber of Lucretia with a drawn sword and spoke thus: "Before all things, Lucretia, I tell you this: if you let a single cry out of your mouth, you shall be stabbed to death. Afterward, know that I am fervently ignited by love for you; therefore, I desire that you kindly fulfill my will. If that cannot happen amicably, you shall be forced to it with the sword."

But since he could not obtain this through threats—for she did not fear death if she could only protect her womanly honor—he devised a damnable, evil trick to stain her purity, and said:

->The 71st leaf<-

"...if you will not submit to my will, then I shall kill you and a servant alongside you, and say: I found you both sinfully together, and out of duty to my kinsmen, I have punished the wickedness and adultery of you both." By such words, the chaste woman was deeply troubled, and she thought in her sorrowful mind: "If I were to be killed in this manner, there would be no one to clear me of such an accusation; therefore, it is better to surrender my body to the adulterer, so that I may afterwards prove my innocence through my own punishment." <note>Lucretia calculates that if she dies now, Sextus will frame her for adultery with a slave, destroying her reputation forever. By surviving the assault, she gains the opportunity to tell the truth before choosing to end her life on her own terms.</note> 

When he had satisfied his foul desire, he departed from there with joy, as if—in his own mind—he had won a great battle. But Lucretia was profoundly saddened and heavily burdened by such a sinful evil. 

As soon as day broke, she sent for her father, Tricipitinus, and for Brutus, the closest friend of her husband Collatinus <note>Lucius Tarquinius Collatinus, Lucretia’s husband.</note>, who always held her in high regard. She also had her other friends summoned, as well as her husband. When they arrived, she recounted to them all, weeping and in great sorrow, what Sextus had committed against her the previous night. 

As her friends, each and all together, began to comfort the weeping woman, she drew forth her sharp knife, which she had hidden and arranged within her garments. She spoke: "Though I have cleared myself of the sin <note>The "sin" here refers to the act of adultery, which she argues was forced and thus not a sin of her will.</note>, I am not yet released from the punishment; thus, no woman shall ever take me as an example to live in shame after such a foul deed." With these words, she plunged the knife into her innocent heart, and falling upon it in the sight of her father and husband, she ended her life. 

O, you ill-fated beauty of this woman! O, the pure clarity of your spirit! Who can praise you enough, or exalt you as highly as you deserve? No one; except those who can truly consider what...

Leaf 71

->### Chapter 48: About Tomyris<-

[Woodcut illustration showing a queen, labeled "Tomyris," placing a severed head into a vessel. A label "Cyrus" is placed near the vessel. To the right, several dead soldiers lie on the ground.]

**Tomyris** was an illustrious queen of the Scythians <term>Scythians: ancient nomadic people of Central Asia and Eastern Europe, often depicted as formidable warriors</term>. Because those people live in an uncultivated, barren, and cold land, situated near the mountains called the Riphean and Hyperborean <note>In ancient geography, these were legendary mountains located in the far north of the world</note>, and because they live largely in isolation and are unknown to almost all other nations, the origins of this queen have been less documented than others. 

However, her name has become widespread because she was able to rule such a rough, untamed, and wild people with such power. At the time when the mighty King of the Persians, **Cyrus**, held the kingdoms of Asia, he was seized by a desire to also conquer the kingdom of Scythia. He sought this more for worldly fame <note>original: "tüm," meaning glory or renown</note> than for the expansion of his empire; for he had...

[Main text:]
->The 67th leaf<-

You have well heard how the Scythians were poor and bestial <note>The Scythians were often depicted in ancient texts as nomadic, rugged, and "uncivilized" by Greek and Roman standards.</note>, and yet they could never be overcome by the greatest kings. Because of a desire for such conquest, Cyrus gathered a military expedition <term>Heerfahrt (expedition): a formal military campaign or call to war</term> against the royal widow **Tomyris**. When she heard of his arrival—even though he was a fearsome emperor of all Asia and nearly the whole world because of his great, victorious battles and strong rule—she did not flee like frightened women do into caves. Nor did she desire any mediator to seek peace; instead, she gathered all her people, commanding them to arm themselves to resist the enemies according to their ability.

And although she could have easily prevented his entry into her land by ship, she nevertheless permitted him to come with all his people into her land of **Araxes** <note>original: "Araye"; refers to the Araxes River region, a common setting for the boundary of Cyrus's empire</note>. For she believed he would be much more easily defeated in her own land, which was familiar to her army, than outside of it.

But when she heard through certain intelligence that he had entered her land, she ordered a youth, her only son <note>Historical sources identify him as Spargapises</note>, to go against him and do battle with a third of her people. Cyrus, when he learned of his approach, considered the nature of the people and the land. He realized that he could overcome them better through cunning treachery than with the sword. And so he had all the campsites sufficiently prepared with the best delicacies and food, and especially with the best wine, which was previously unknown to those people <note>The Scythians were traditionally depicted as drinkers of water or fermented mare's milk, making them highly susceptible to the effects of strong Persian wine</note>. After doing this, they feigned a flight.

And when the youth with his people came to the camp, he found no enemy. Because of this, he was heartily gladdened, like one who had driven the enemy into flight with a powerful victory. And so they abandoned their battle formations and gave themselves over to the food, as if they had been invited to a feast rather than a battle, and they filled...

Tomyris, however, when she heard that her people were slain—though she was greatly moved by the death of her only son—did not behave impetuously with weeping and crying according to womanly custom; for she suppressed that same sorrow with anger and the desire for revenge and prepared herself with the remaining people to fight against Cyrus. ## The 74th leaf
### ¶ Regarding Leena the harlot, the 47th chapter

[Woodcut illustration of a woman labeled "Leena" being tortured on a rack. Two men stand to the left, and an executioner operates the rack on the right.]

->Leena<-

**L**eena was a Greek woman. And although she was of ill repute, yet with the permission of pious women—queens, princesses, and others—I place her also among the number of those who performed deeds more noteworthy than others. For many remarkable stories are often accomplished by both evil and good men. Therefore, although she practiced shameful works, her courageous <note>original: "manlicher," literally "manly," used here to denote strength of spirit or bravery</note> deed should nevertheless not be forgotten. For at the time when Amyntas <note>likely Amyntas I of Macedon</note> reigned in Macedonia, the noble youths Harmodius <note>original: "Armonius"</note> and Aristogeiton <note>original: "ariston"</note> rose up to deliver the land from the oppression of the tyrant Hipparchus <note>original: "hÿspar"</note>. And after they had killed him, an inquiry and interrogation was carried out by his successor to see if it could be discovered who had done this. Because of this, many were imprisoned, and among others this same Leena; for many of the youths frequented her house for pleasure, for which reason she was suspected of having knowledge of such plots.

And when she heard that she was being tormented with manifold torture, so that she would surrender those whom she knew were guilty of the deed—although she unspeakably intended to die rather than to betray these youths—yet out of worry that she might be weakened by pain into confessing, and also considering how honorable and holy the name of true friendship was, after strong denial and the increase of torture and the growth of the body’s frailty, she fell into a stronger, more <term>manly spirit</term> <note>In the medieval and Renaissance period, "manly spirit" (manlichers gemüt) was a high compliment used to describe a woman who displayed exceptional courage or stoicism.</note>. And therefore, so that she might fortify her resolve, she bit off her own tongue and spat it out, so that by this single, highly praised deed, she deprived herself of all speech. Thus, she now knew everyone would remain un-betrayed by her. Who would want to say that Leena <note>original: "leena"; referring to Leaina of Athens, a courtesan and conspirator.</note>, who was of such strong spirit, could be placed into such a shameful life <note>Referring to her profession as a courtesan.</note> except through the great misfortune of ill luck? Without doubt, whoever says that women can only keep secret what they do not know has not known this woman. And truly, this woman fell into <term>frivolity</term> <note>original: "leichtfertikeit"; here referring to her life as a prostitute or courtesan.</note> not out of her nature—for that was good—but out of idleness and laziness, as still happens to many who are not raised by their mothers to appropriate work.

# The 75th Leaf

...lay among dogs, shedding his blood through a thousand wounds. And her mother Jezebel, in royal finery, was thrown down from a high tower and so trampled and crushed by walking, riding, and driving <note>original: "gan/reyten vnd faren"—referring to the movements of pedestrians, horsemen, and chariots</note> that no sign of her wicked corpse remained; instead, nothing but dung and filth was seen. And finally, when these things were fulfilled—so that she and her murderous bloodshed did not remain unpunished—her grandson Joas <note>Jehoash of Judah</note>, whom she did not know was still alive, was raised up as king through the help of the prince Joadam <note>Jehoiada the High Priest</note>. Thus, the evildoer was sent to hell by the same path as she had sent many an innocent person. 

These are the works of God’s justice. And even if the punishment for sin is delayed for a time, it strikes all the more harshly afterward, especially against those who, after long waiting, do not wish to turn toward improvement. And if we do not consider this, if we do not wish to believe it, and if we ignore every warning of punishment but instead choose to burden ourselves with even greater guilt, then—when we least expect it—we shall be swallowed by the raging waves of the bottomless sea, where neither mourning nor crying out for our sins will be of any help. 

Shameful and disgraceful is all desire to rule without a legal entry <note>meaning a legitimate or rightful accession to power</note>; for without right, there are two ways to enter into power, both of which are reckless and grievous: through treachery or by force. To achieve power through treachery, one must use malice, enmity, perjury, and...

### The 86th leaf

[C]hristians killed or sent into misery <note>original: "ellend." In Early New High German, this term specifically refers to being "out of the land," meaning exile or banishment, as well as general suffering.</note> / the rich ruined / the old friends driven away. You cannot eat a mouthful without worry / because you have condemned and driven away the pious and must entrust your life to the wicked. Toward such "honors" you strive, heart; consider whether it would not be better for you to be content with what is yours, which you may possess peacefully, honorably, godly, without interference, and without worry for your life / for just as great things are won with bloodshed, so must they be maintained and protected with great care and anxiety / and it often happens that wealth won through the wicked is lost again through them. And perhaps life perishes along with the wealth, through the actions of those who helped to win it wickedly.

### ¶ Chapter 50: Concerning Cloelia

**C**loelia lived during the time when Tarquin the Proud, the ruler <note>original: "keyser," though historically Tarquin was the last King of Rome, the text uses the contemporary German term for a supreme sovereign</note> of Rome, was driven out. Because of the great evil his son Sextus had committed against the noble Lucretia, great wars broke out between the Romans and him. To aid Tarquin, Porsenna—the King of Clusium of the Etruscans <note>original: "Clusinus Etruscorum"</note>—came to his assistance. However, his crossing was prevented by the bravery of Horatius Cocles, so that he could not cross the Sublician Bridge <note>original: "brucken sublicij," the oldest wooden bridge in ancient Rome</note>. They were also driven back by the boldness of Mucius Scaevola. For this reason, Porsenna reached an agreement with the Romans, stipulating that as a confirmation of the peace, the Romans should give many of the noble maidens of Rome to him as hostages. Among these, Cloelia was also included. 

Afterward, it happened that at one time Cloelia considered deeply that it was not honorable for the public interest <note>original: "common vnd gemeinen nucz," referring to the common good or the state</note> to place such heavy pledges—so many young women—in the hands of a foreign king. She stirred her maidenly spirit, raising it toward courage <note>original: "manheyt," literally "manliness," a term frequently used in this period to denote heroic virtue or fortitude regardless of gender</note>, and planned to escape by night from the guards with several of her companions to...

<summary>This page introduces the story of the Roman heroine Cloelia, detailing how she was taken hostage by the Etruscan King Porsenna during the wars following the expulsion of the Tarquin dynasty.</summary>

...be returned. However, this was on the condition that she would be released again after a time. When King Porsenna saw the young woman, he felt great wonder at her virtue and took high pleasure in her daring <note>The original term "dürstlikeyt" implies a bold, almost thirsty courage.</note>. He permitted her not only to return to her friends but also allowed her to take back with her whomever she wished from the hostages who remained. ->Hippo<-

<keywords>Cloelia, King Porsenna, Sacred Way, Via Sacra, Roman honors, Hippo, Greek women, female virtue</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
The 77th leaf

Though a single swallow makes no summer, she should rightly not be forgotten. Once, when **Hippo** <note>A Greek woman celebrated in antiquity for her chastity, mentioned by authors like Valerius Maximus and Boccaccio.</note> was captured by sea pirates and led away, she noticed how the robbers planned to violate her honor, for she was exceedingly beautiful. But since her delicate chastity could find no way to escape from them, she jumped into the raging sea to prevent their shameful violence. Thus, with the loss of her life, she preserved her purity.

Who could praise the stern resolve of this woman enough? With the perhaps few years of her life, she chose to keep her purity and placed herself in the eternal memory of praiseworthy deeds, so that her praise would never be erased from the books of remembrance. Her body, after being tossed about by the sea for a time, came to the land called **Erythrae** <note>An ancient city in Ionia, on the coast of Asia Minor.</note>. There, she was at first simply buried by the local people <note>original: "ȳmeonern", likely referring to the inhabitants of the region</note> like any other drowned corpse. But when the news arrived of the reason for her death, they had a costly tomb built for her with great dignity upon the shore, as a long-lasting memorial to her chaste spirit; to recognize that the bright light of virtue cannot be darkened by any kind of adversity.

# The 78th leaf
## Titus Livius, Decade 1, Book 2, Folio 10
### ¶ Concerning Veturia, Chapter 52

**V**eturia, the old noble Roman woman, has through her great deeds made the years of her old age green again <note>This metaphor suggests that her actions brought her renewed fame or vitality late in life</note>. She had a son named Gnaeus Marcius, who was rigorous and strong in both body and spirit. Because through his <term>frümkeyt</term> <note>In this context, excellence, valor, or piety</note>, great wisdom, and strong hand, the fortified stronghold of the Volscians called Corioli—which the Romans were then besieging—was won, she gave him the surname **Coriolanus**. This was done with such praise and honor that he became more famous than all others for his deeds of <term>ritterschafft</term> <note>Literally "knighthood"; the author uses this medieval term to describe Roman military service</note>. 

And after a time, when the Romans suffered a great lack of food, much grain was brought there from Sicily through the help of the eldest Romans, whom they call the **Fathers of the Country** <note>original: "vätter des lanndes"; referring to the Roman Senators or *Patres*</note>. But Coriolanus, with haughty speech, forbade that such grain should be distributed to the common people <note>original: "der gemeyn"</note> until they returned to the nobility the honor and dignity they had taken from them. 

And without doubt, because the common folk <term>böuel</term> <note>The "populace" or "masses," often used with a slightly derogatory tone in this period</note> were hungry, they would have laid violent hands upon him if their leaders had not set a day for him to appear before the law to explain the reason for his prohibition. But out of great indignation toward the commoners, he did not appear on the appointed day; for this reason, he was condemned to <term>ellend</term> <note>In Early New High German, this specifically means "exile" or being sent to a "foreign land," though it later evolved to mean "misery"</note>. 

He then retreated to the Volscians, who shortly before had been his enemies, and by whom he was received well and kindly; for valor is still held everywhere in great dignity and value. They were all moved by him and their captain, Attius Tullius, to march in battle against the Romans. And the same Coriolanus was appointed captain of the people, and they marched with great force up to a trench of the city of Rome, and brought the Romans to such a point that their leaders... <note>The text ends mid-sentence as the page concludes</note>

<summary>This page introduces Veturia and her son Coriolanus, detailing his military success against the Volscians, his subsequent conflict with the Roman commoners over grain supplies, and his eventual exile and defection to his former enemies.</summary>
<keywords>Veturia, Coriolanus, Gnaeus Marcius, Rome, Volscians, famine, exile, Titus Livius, Roman history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Gaius Marcius Coriolanus, a Roman general who, after being exiled from Rome, defected to the Volscians and led an army against his home city. The previous context likely detailed his military successes and the mounting panic within Rome.</meta>

### Page 78

The council <note>The Roman Senate</note> ordered an embassy to be sent to the man they had condemned to exile <note>original: "ellend," which in this period meant both misery and banishment from one's homeland</note> to beg for peace from him, according to his will and conditions. But Marcius <note>Coriolanus</note> sent them back with a shameful answer, because of which they were sent to him a second time. And again they were poorly received and sent away with dishonor. On the third time, the clergy went—the high priest with the others—wearing their sacred headbands <note>original: "inseln"; referring to the *infulae*, the woolen fillets or ribbons worn by Roman priests as a sign of their religious status</note> and other clerical robes, humbly desiring peace. But all effort was lost, whereby the entire city was set in despair. 

The honorable women of the city came daily with many kinds of lamentation to Veturia, Coriolanus’s mother, and his wife, named Volumnia. Weeping, they told of their distress and begged for help, eventually persuading them to go. Thus, both women went out with a great multitude of the honorable matrons into the enemy camp with tears and prayers to obtain peace and to appease her son; for indeed, the city could not be protected by the sword. 

As soon as Coriolanus learned of his mother’s arrival—although he was grimly moved against the Romans—nevertheless he restrained his spirit at the coming of his mother. He stood up from his chair and went out of his tent to receive her. Veturia took his wife on one side and his son on the other, and before she reached him, she set aside her gentleness and moved herself to anger against her son. Everything she had intended to beg of him humbly during the journey, she turned instead into harsh words of rebuke as she entered the camp. And when she saw him walking toward her, she took up a strong spirit in a weak body and spoke: 

"Stand still, you ill-bred youth! I wish to know, before you come to embrace me, whether you approach me as..."

->The 79th leaf<-

...to receive a captured enemy—I mean, if that is your intention. Woe is me that the joys for which I desired you to live are those where you were sent into such misery, and that you should be seen by me as an enemy of the **common good** <note>original: "gemainen nuczes"; referring to the public welfare or the Roman Republic</note>. I ask you: do you recognize upon what seat you sit thus armed? Do you recognize what land you have before your eyes? Recognize—if you do not already know it—it is the city in which you were conceived, in which you were born, in which you were nourished and raised with great labor. With what spirit, or by what motivation, could you bring warlike conflict against your **fatherland**? You have not considered what honor was due to your mother at your arrival. The sweetness of your spouse has not reminded you, and you have completely despised kindness toward your children; you would not even receive the petition of the **elders of your fatherland** <note>original: "öbristen deines vatterlād"; likely referring to the Roman Senators</note>. Your hard heart could not yield to any petition so that you might let go of the anger which you—though the guilty one—have taken into yourself. When you looked upon this city, did you not think: "There is my paternal house; there are my **gods** <note>Referring to the *Lares* and *Penates*, the ancestral household gods of Rome</note> whom I should honor; there is my spouse; there are my children; there is my unhappy mother, suffering through many misfortunes"? The best Romans, the eldest fathers, and the highest priesthood have not been able to soften your **stony heart** <note>A common metaphor in Early New High German for stubbornness or lack of empathy</note>, so that you might do through prayer that which you are rightfully obligated to do, and should have done unasked. I must lament my misery, for I recognize that through my child-bearing, I have been an adversary to myself and to the whole land. For where I believed I had given birth to a son and a good citizen, I have instead produced such an unhappy, stone-hard enemy who now allows himself to be moved or overcome in no way. Much better...

¶ Megulia <note>Referring to Megullia Dotata</note>, the 54th chapter, you will find on the 75th leaf.

### ¶ Concerning Thamiris the Painter, the 54th Chapter.

**T**hamiris <note>Also known in classical sources as Timarete</note> was the best female painter in her time. She lived in the ninetieth Olympiad <note>Approximately 420–417 BCE</note>, a daughter of Micon the painter of Athens. She abandoned all womanly tasks and devoted herself to her father's art, in which she became so skilled that, in the time of Archelaus, the king in Macedonia, she held the highest crown and glory of the arts. This was achieved through a panel on which she had painted Diana <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Artemis</note>, which for a long time was kept as a great treasure <term>klainet: a jewel, ornament, or precious object of art</term> for a long and laudable memory of such art by women. It is also well worthy of remembrance when measured against the distaff and spindles <note>Traditional tools for spinning wool, here used to contrast domestic chores with high art</note> of other women.

# Leaf 80

->[Woodcut illustration: On the right, a woman labeled **Arthemesia** stands drinking from a chalice. On the left, a man labeled **Mausolus** is seated. Between them sits a small table with a shallow bowl.]<-

**Artemisia** <note>Artemisia II of Caria (died c. 350 BCE), sister and wife of King Mausolus.</note> was a queen in **Caria** <note>An ancient region in southwestern Asia Minor, in modern-day Turkey.</note> of a high and strong spirit, of holy and surpassing love for her husband, and of such firm chastity in her state of widowhood that her memory is rightfully held up as an eternal example for the widows who follow. She had as her husband the most noble king in Caria, **Mausolus** <note>original: "Mansolum"</note>, whom she loved so dearly during his life that she could not forget him after his death.

Of this, his wonderful tomb <note>This refers to the Mausoleum at Halicarnassus, one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World, from which we get the modern word "mausoleum."</note> bears witness. For when her dearest husband had reached his final day, she had the dead corpse buried with the highest royal honors. And after the burning of the body <note>The practice of cremation was standard for high-status individuals in this period and region.</note> according to their custom, she did not allow the ashes to be buried in a golden urn like other royal ashes, but had them collected with the utmost diligence and kept beautifully; for she believed no other vessel was fit to hold them worthily but her own breast, in which the flames of old love should never be extinguished even after his death. And as an eternal memorial of his past life, she always mixed into every drink a <note>The text likely continues on the next page to explain that she mixed his ashes into her wine to become a living tomb for her husband.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Artemisia of Caria as a paragon of widowhood, detailing her intense grief and her decision to consume the ashes of her husband, Mausolus.</summary>
<keywords>Artemisia, Mausolus, Caria, widowhood, Mausoleum of Halicarnassus, cremation, devotion</keywords>

-># The 81st leaf<-

...a few of his ashes, so much that nothing of them remained unconsumed <note>Artemisia was famously said to have mixed Mausolus's ashes into her daily drink to become a living tomb for him</note>. She spent all the remaining time in weeping and mourning, until her natural vitality <note>original: "ſeychaitkeyt," referring to the "radical moisture" or vital fluids that ancient medicine believed sustained life</note> was consumed, and she went—as she intended—joyfully into death to join her husband. Yet she accomplished great works in her widowhood <note>literally "the widow's chair," a term for her status as a reigning widow</note>. For it was an old custom to erect costly tombs for kings after their death, through which one might recognize the love of the spouse during their lifetime. Therefore, **Artemisia** devised a wonderful and costly building, on which nothing was spared. In this, she would not be satisfied with just one, nor with an ordinary craftsman, but instead she sent for four of the most highly famous masters that could be found in all the Greek lands, named **Scopas**, **Bryaxis**, **Timotheus**, and **Leochares** <note>These were the most renowned sculptors of the 4th century BCE</note>, whose likes were not to be found in the world. To them, she gave the command to build an elaborate <term>Mausoleum|A grand, monumental tomb named after King Mausolus; the word has since become the standard English term for any magnificent burial structure</term> for her husband according to their highest arts, carved from marble with such workmanship that her dear husband’s memory would be made eternal through such a wonder-work. 

And it is not unreasonable—since it surpasses all buildings in the whole world in cost and artistry, and is counted as one of the **Seven Wonders of the World**—that it also be specifically recorded by us in writing. For through common renown, this work shall flourish again <note>literally "turn green again"</note> and the illustrious lady's honorable praise be made clear. 

Thus, the master builders, at **Halicarnassus** <note>the capital of the ancient region of Caria, located in modern-day Bodrum, Turkey</note>, by the queen’s command, formed a four-cornered tomb. And on the side facing south <note>original: "mittag," meaning midday or south</note>, they made it sixty-three feet long; the others were shorter. The height reached a point at one hundred and seventy feet, and it was [adorned] with thirty-six...

->Folio 81<-

The small walls were surrounded by columns, and the eastern side <note>original: "orient"</note> with its adornments was entrusted to Scopas. The northern side <note>original: "mitternacht," literally "midnight," used here to denote the North</note> was given to Bryaxis, the western side <note>original: "occident"</note> to Leochares, and the fourth part to Timotheus. All four of them displayed such artistry and applied such diligence to carve historical scenes, engrave pillars, and other related tasks, that the figures might be valued by everyone as truly living images. This was done with the greatest earnestness and industry of the masters, for each wished to prove his art above the others and be praised for these miraculous works.

Yet Artemisia ended her days <note>An idiom meaning she passed away</note> before the work was completed. Nevertheless, the masters did not abandon the work; instead, for the eternal memory of their ingenious arts and for the benefit of posterity, they finished it entirely. It was completed with such wonderful craftsmanship that the whole work appeared as if it were a single stone. Within it, various vaults and hollows were carved so that no person could speak so softly to another—regardless of which vault they were in—without being heard in all the others. 

This highly and widely famed work was thus completed in honor of King Mausolus, from whose name it was called the "Mausoleum." From this comes the fact that all royal graves are still called <term>mauseola<note>Latin: mauseola; the plural of mausoleum</note></term> in Latin. Because of these things, the loyalty and love of Artemisia are rightly highly praised and recognized as illustrious; even more so and more clearly, the constancy of her state of widowhood and the unceasing shedding of her tears. 

No less praise is given to her pure body, in which the ashes of her husband rested as if in a precious grave, after she had consumed them in a drink <note>Historical accounts suggest Artemisia mixed her husband's ashes with her daily drink to become a "living tomb" for him</note>. This same Artemisia is not only to be elevated in praise for these things, but also for her strong virtue, manly spirit, and boldness. For in knightly deeds <note>The text uses "ritterlichen," referring to martial or chivalric pursuits</note> she was well-practiced and highly capable, and her elegant name...

# The 82nd Leaf

...sea-wall, standing firm and hidden from the sight of the enemies. And as soon as she received the signal from the citizens on the walls that the Rhodians <note>original: "thodiſer"</note> had entered the city and abandoned their ships, and were exercising their power within the city as if it had already been won, she had her own troops move in behind them. With all their might, the hidden citizens of the city also revealed themselves to fight bravely, and the enemy was attacked from all sides and hard-pressed. There was no way for them to flee, and they were all slain. 

Since all the ships of the Rhodians remained there fully equipped, Artemisia immediately conceived a high-minded plan to accomplish further manly deeds. She manned those same ships with her own well-armed soldiers and had them decorated with laurel branches and other signs of victory, and sailed swiftly toward Rhodes <note>original: "thodis"</note>. The guards of the gates and the city were amazed when they saw their ships returning with the tokens of victory; they opened all the gates to the sea and the city, hurrying with such rejoicing that they never realized they were welcoming enemies instead of friends. This continued until she subdued the unsuspecting citizens with a strong hand and seized the city. 

There, she had the lords of the city put to death. As an eternal monument of her victory, she commanded that victory columns be erected in the middle of the marketplace. Two bronze columns were made there, which stood for many years afterward: on one was a joyful, victorious image of Artemisia, and on the other was a sorrowful figure with a downcast face representing Rhodes <note>original: "thodiā"</note>, accompanied by an inscription explaining this remarkable history. Thus, she made the city tributary to her and turned her way back home. 

Furthermore, when Xerxes, the most powerful King of the Persians <note>original: "perſarū"</note>, marched by land and sea against the Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans</note> with the intent to conquer them and all the Greek lands...

¶ Concerning Megulia, the 53rd Chapter.

**M**egulia, as I believe, was a noble Roman woman. And in the holy times of the poverty of this world's existence—before the nobility had fallen into the proud, ostentatious, and costly decoration of clothing and other luxuries—she was called *Dotata* <note>original: *dotata*; a Latin term meaning "endowed with a dowry."</note> by the ancient Romans. This was because she was endowed by the generosity of her friends wonderfully highly above other young women, according to the custom of that time, [to be given] to her husband. For *dotata* is as much as saying "an endowed woman." Therefore, it remained the custom for many years that whenever a maiden was endowed beyond the common rate, she was called "Megulia Dotata." And yet, her wealth was no more than several thousand copper coins. <note>The text refers to "kupfer pfenning," likely representing the Roman *as*. In early Rome, a dowry of 50,000 copper coins was considered massive, though the author views it as modest compared to 15th-century excesses.</note> Oh, good simplicity! Oh, praiseworthy poverty! How has the strange—and perhaps ridiculous—course of things now in these times become so common? For everywhere we have overstepped the right limit so much that now one can hardly find a tanner, shoemaker, carpenter, tailor, merchant, or knight who would want to take a wife home with such a gift as was given to this Megulia, who was called "so highly endowed." And it is no wonder, for common women have <insert>taken to</insert>

# Folio 84

...a father’s [love] toward her and the wicked love of the fervent suitor. At the time when the ten men <note>The Decemvirs, a group of ten men appointed to govern Rome and codify its laws around 451–449 BCE.</note> chosen to rule the city of Rome had already ruled for two years, this same young woman was betrothed by her father to a very stern young man named Lucilius Icilius, a Tribune <note>A Roman official elected to protect the interests and rights of the common people (plebeians).</note>. Because of the demands of military affairs, Virginius <note>The girl's father, a respected centurion.</note> was sent out by the Romans, and thus the wedding was delayed. While matters stood this way, it happened to the misfortune of the young woman that one of the ten men, Appius Claudius—who had remained in the city to rule along with only one of his companions named Spurius Appius—was so moved by her great beauty with a burning passion that he wished to spare no effort, labor, nor expense to possess her. 

However, when he realized that her tender, blessed, and virginal spirit could not be softened by flattering words, prayers, threats, nor by bold displays or great gifts—and considering that force should not be used openly—he was inflamed more and more with love for her and devised a way to have her through wicked cunning. He arranged with one of his accomplices named Marcus Claudius that, as soon as he could manage it, he should accost the young woman on the street as if she were his own slave <note>The original term "eygen menſch" refers to a person held as property or a bondservant.</note> who had been abducted and fled from him, and thus lead her into his house. And if anyone tried to interfere, he should then demand a legal ruling before the court. 

A few days later, the deceitful man seized her and claimed she was his. She cried out and resisted as best she could with the help of the other women who were walking with her. Because of this disturbance, a great crowd of people gathered. Among them came Icilius, her betrothed, and after many words were exchanged, it came to the point that they went before the judge—who was the very man who loved her...

And they stood against Marcus Claudius, who claimed the maiden as his own, asserting she had been taken from him. Immediately, she was awarded to him by the false judge as a fugitive from her master, so that Virginius's side was never heard, nor could it be. 

But when Marcus wished to take her, and Virginius complained bitterly against Appius, he [Virginius] with difficulty restrained his violent anger, asking that he might first speak with the nurse <note><term>amme</term> — a wet-nurse who suckled and raised the child</note> who had nursed and raised the child, to see if she had not been swapped at birth, so that he might more easily allow her to follow Marcus. And as soon as they left the courthouse, coming near the shops <note><term>tabernen</term> — the stalls and shops located in the Roman Forum</note> which could clearly be seen from the building, Virginius drew out his knife and said: "Dearest daughter, I see that I cannot keep you in freedom before this oppressive judge; therefore, I shall deliver you from this state of ownership <note>Virginius chooses to kill his daughter to prevent her from being forced into sexual slavery as the "property" of the conspirators.</note>." 

With these words, he plunged the knife into the maiden's heart, with such great horror, misery, and sorrow of all the people present, that the pure, unfortunate maiden soon fell to the earth, and her soul departed with her chaste blood. In this way, the shameful hope of the unchaste Appius and the false judge was [thwarted] through the

### The [unclear] 85th leaf

Through the spilling of innocent blood—undone by the story and the bringing of the case of Virginia and Icilius <note>Icilius was the fiancé of Virginia; his speeches helped rouse the people against the corrupt government</note> before the common people of Rome—it came to pass that the **Ten Men** <note>The Decemviri, a commission of ten men who had been granted absolute power to write Rome's laws but became tyrants</note> were forced to give up their power over the government of the Roman Empire, and the city and all the people were set back into freedom. 

Not long afterward, a day of trial was set for **Appius Claudius** regarding the matter, through the efforts of Virginius <note>Virginia's father, spelled "virginey" in the text</note>, who now held authority, having been elected by the common people. And when Claudius gave his answer, he was dragged into the dungeon by the command of Virginius and bound with chains in such a way that he could not escape the death he deserved for the falsehood he had committed against that pure heart. 

And soon afterward, he was forced by the rope <note>This likely refers to suicide by hanging in prison or execution by strangulation</note> to drive his soul out of his body into the depths of hell. But Marcus Claudius <note>Appius's subordinate who had falsely claimed Virginia was his runaway slave</note> was not punished according to his guilt, for he was protected by flight <note>He was allowed to go into exile</note>; nevertheless, the property of both men was confiscated into the common treasury. 

What is more evil than an unjust, false judge? For as often as he intends to do wrong, it is inevitable that he perverts the entire order of the law, the power of the statutes is despised, all virtue is wounded, and everything good, along with the common utility, is trampled underfoot. And if this were not already obvious, it is certainly brought to light through the story of the unjust judge Appius. For because he wished to satisfy his evil lust, he attempted to give a free maiden into **servitude** <term>eygenschafft: a state of legal bondage or slavery</term> through his false judgment. Through his actions, he tried to turn a maiden into an adulteress and a bride into a concubine. By his evil ruling, he gave cause for a father to take up arms with his own hands against his dearest daughter, so that fatherly kindness was turned into a terrible deed <note>Referring to Virginius killing his daughter Virginia to save her from being enslaved and raped by Appius</note>.

<summary>This page recounts the downfall of the Roman Decemviri following the tragedy of Virginia, focusing on the imprisonment and death of the corrupt judge Appius Claudius. It concludes with a moral reflection on how judicial corruption destroys the rule of law and social virtue.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the series of biographies of famous women, likely from a translation of Giovanni Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women). The previous sections likely dealt with other female artists or thinkers from antiquity.</meta>

->[Top right corner: 93 / 94]<-

### The 86th leaf

### ¶ About Irene, daughter of Cratinus
**Irene** was a daughter of the painter **Cratinus**. She is praised and honored all the more because she became more skillful than her master <note>original: "meÿster" — referring to her father, who was also her teacher</note>. Indeed, her name is still celebrated by painters today. The great mastery of this same Irene is proven by a painting of a maiden on a panel created by her, located in the city of **Eleusis** in Greece. Likewise, she painted a portrait of the elder **Calypso**, and one of **Absatem** the fighter <note>original: "absatem des fechters" — This likely refers to Alcisthenes the dancer or an athlete, based on Pliny the Elder's accounts of her work</note>. All of these were made with such mastery that it is a wonder to hear they were done by a woman. For many long years, they have been regarded as masterpieces of art, which is why I did not wish to let her pass by without praise.

### ¶ About Leontium, Chapter 59
**Leontium**, if I have considered it rightly, was a Greek woman who lived and was highly esteemed during the time when **Alexander the Great** reigned. However, she would have been much more highly honored for her sharp mind if she had maintained womanly modesty and feminine virtue <note>The author is reflecting the period's bias; Leontium was an Epicurean philosopher and a "hetaira" (a sophisticated courtesan), which led later medieval writers to criticize her character despite her intellect</note>. 

The ancient writers say of her that she was so highly learned in all the arts that, through womanly audacity—or perhaps out of a particular hatred—she dared to write a critical attack against the highest "master of nature" living at that time, **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus was the successor to Aristotle; Leontium’s philosophical critique of him was famous in antiquity for its brilliance, though many men found her boldness "shameful"</note>. She dared to diminish and wound him and his art through her writing. But since her writings have survived through so many years down to our own time, it is well to remember that she must have been a mistress of great arts. Although she was envious, she was not of lowly stock, but born of noble blood. However, her dishonorable lifestyle darkened her bright blood and her enlightened art.

<summary>This page profiles two notable Greek women: Irene, a celebrated painter who surpassed her father's skill, and Leontium, a philosopher who dared to intellectually challenge the famous scholar Theophrastus.</summary>

<keywords>Irene, Cratinus, painting, Eleusis, Calypso, Leontium, Alexander the Great, Theophrastus, female artists, Greek philosophy, womanly virtue</keywords>

**C**laudia must be believed to have been born of the most noble Romans, when one observes her exceedingly great goodness toward her father. **Virginia** was one of the highly honored women among the Romans, yet a different one than the woman described previously. O worthy and holy words of this noble woman! O laudable and faultless desire and good thought, which is rightly to be raised to the heavens with rejoicing! She does not seek to incite rebellion among women against their husbands; she does not seek the beauty of the body or elegant delights through which men are provoked and women become weak-willed. Instead, she desires such unity through which the frivolous desires of young men and their unchaste eyes might be tamed. And the radiance of her own glory in elegant chastity shines even unto the heavens. 

Through this laudable and holy beginning, it came to pass—and lasted for a long time—that no woman could bring an offering to this same temple unless she were completely and beyond all measure highly praised in honor and irreproachable, and had never had more than one husband <note>The Roman ideal of the "univira," a woman who remained faithful to her first and only husband even after his death</note>. And she lived in such a way that, through her very being, she would be judged rightly worthy of the noble temple of Chastity. And without doubt, if one were to drive the slandered women away from the sacrifice of the altar today, many ambitious women would still be found who...

# ¶ Of Flora the harlot, wife of Zephyrus, the 64th chapter.

**F**lora was a Roman woman. And as much as shameful profit took away from her womanly beauty <note>original: "weiplicher zierd," referring here to her virtue or purity</note>, so much did Fortune spread her name further. She was an exceedingly wealthy woman, as all the old masters say. But how she gained that wealth is a matter of disagreement. Some, and the more correct ones <note>The author is indicating his preference for the historical rather than the mythological explanation</note>, say she abandoned all the adornment and beauty of her blooming youth and her pleasant body among common women, procurers <term>ruffian: a historical term for pimps or those who manage prostitutes</term>, and others; she defiled her body with shameful pollution to the defiance of young men. And now this one, then the others, with flattering, affectionate words and impure acts—as is the custom of such harlots—she enticed to her, so that they were inflamed in evil love so strongly that she could easily snatch all their goods from them. And as soon as one fell into poverty, he was abandoned and another taken up, through which she gained such great wealth that few could equal her. But others wished to speak somewhat more honorably of her and set forth a playful story <note>original: "schimpfliche geschicht," a story told for entertainment or as a fable</note>: how in Rome the high keeper of the temple of Hercules gambled by himself in an idle time. He played for Hercules, and the stakes were to be a good dinner and a beautiful mistress to sleep with him that same night; and whatever he threw with his right hand was to count for Hercules, and the throw of the left hand for himself. It so happened that Hercules won, and therefore the keeper brought Flora to him...

<summary>This page introduces the Roman figure Flora, contrasting her immense wealth with her reputation. The text presents two conflicting origins for her fortune: one attributing it to a life of calculated prostitution and another to a legendary gambling match won by the god Hercules.</summary>
<keywords>Flora, Hercules, Rome, prostitution, Zephyrus, wealth, morality, gambling, Roman mythology</keywords>

### Concerning Romana, the young woman. Chapter 64.

[Woodcut illustration: On the left, a man sits on a bench working with a tool. In the center, a man in a hat stands holding a sword or staff. On the right, behind a barred window, a young woman is breastfeeding an older person. Below the window is the label "romana". <note>This illustration depicts the famous "Roman Charity" motif, though the text identifies the mother as the prisoner, whereas many versions of the legend feature a father named Cimon.</note>]

Romana was a young woman during the time of her great and virtuous deeds; for this reason, she is rightfully placed among the highly honored women of worthy memory, even though the name of her <term>lineage</term> <note>original: "geschlächtes"; referring to her family name or clan</note> has been lost to antiquity. She had a mother of honorable lineage, but who was unfortunate; for she was condemned to death before the judge—I know not for what crime—and was handed over by the high captain to the jailer to be imprisoned, with the order that he should <term>execute</term> <note>original: "richten"; while it means "to judge," in this context it specifically refers to carrying out a death sentence</note> her by night because she was of noble birth. But when the jailer was meant to kill her, he felt such pity for the noblewoman that he could not bring himself to kill her with his own hands; instead, he locked her alive in a prison, intending for her to die of hunger there. It so happened that Romana, through persistent pleading, was allowed into the prison to see her; however, she was first thoroughly searched to ensure she brought no food against the orders. There, the young daughter gave her hungry mother her [breast] to suckle... <note>The text ends mid-sentence as the narrative continues onto the next page.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the story of Romana, a young woman who saves her mother from starvation in prison by breastfeeding her. The story is a celebrated example of "Filial Piety," where a child's devotion to a parent transcends social taboos.</summary>
<keywords>Romana, Roman Charity, filial piety, imprisonment, execution, Boccaccio, virtuous women</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues a narrative—likely the classical story of 'Roman Charity'—regarding a woman whose mother was sentenced to death by starvation. On the previous page, the daughter was granted permission to visit her mother in prison.</meta>

->Page 86<-

...milk, of which she had plenty at that time, because she had recently given birth to a child. She did this daily for so long that the guard began to wonder how the condemned woman could live such a long time. And watching what the daughter was doing with the mother, he saw how she nourished her mother from her own breasts. And he told the <term>triumvir</term> <note>Original: "dryman." These were Roman officials (triumviri capitales) who oversaw prisons and executions.</note> about the great kindness of the daughter toward her mother. The triumvir then reported this to the <term>praetor</term> <note>A high-ranking Roman judicial officer, here equated by the author with a "burgermeister" or mayor for the reader's understanding.</note>; this same praetor or mayor told it to the entire council. And it was unanimously agreed that the guilty, condemned mother should be given over to the daughter because of her kindness.

¶ Great is the kindness of this daughter! For if one citizen rescues another in the heat of battle and helps save his life, he is praised by the leaders and crowned with singular honor. But what praise can we find that would be sufficient to crown this woman who nourished her mother with the milk of her breasts? I do not know where to seek it among all the trees <note>This likely refers to the "Civic Crown" usually made of oak leaves; the author is saying no tree provides a branch worthy enough to crown such a woman.</note>. 

This is not only a holy act, but a wonderful kindness. And it should not merely be compared to natural gifts, but placed above them. For nature teaches us to nourish children in their tender youth because of their <term>frailty</term> <note>Original: "plödigkeit," referring here to the physical weakness or vulnerability of an infant.</note>, but this woman rescued her mother from death. Therefore, she is rightly honored as a leader among virtuous children.

<summary>This page recounts the conclusion of the 'Roman Charity' legend, where a daughter saves her mother from starvation in prison through breastfeeding. The authorities are so moved by this act of filial piety that they pardon the mother, and the text argues this devotion surpasses even military valor.</summary>
<keywords>Roman Charity, filial piety, breastfeeding, mercy, Roman law, triumvir, praetor, virtue, motherhood, starvation</keywords>
