# ¶ About Marcia, daughter of Varro: Chapter 65

->Varro’s Marcia<-
<note>This refers to the woodcut illustration depicting Marcia engaged in the arts of sculpture and painting.</note>

**M**arcia, daughter of Varro, the perpetual virgin, is to be exalted so much above other virgins as much as she had devoted herself to constant chastity of her own free will and unforced; not like those others who are thrust into the temple fortresses of the goddess <note>A reference to Diana, the Roman goddess of chastity and the hunt.</note> or come there through vows and thus must maintain purity in blessed garments. This Marcia had overcome with her whole mind the sharp sting <term>angel</term> <note>The German "angel" refers to a fishhook or a sting, metaphorically describing the pull of physical temptation.</note> of the enticing flesh, which even the most eminent men often could not combat; and she had kept her body undefiled by any stain until death. And although this Marcia and such constancy are highly praised, she is no less to be praised for the exercise of her mind <note>original: "spñ", likely an abbreviation for "spiritus" or spirit/intellect.</note> and intellect, as well as her great art and the use of her hands. For whether this came from masters or from nature is not known; because she completely disregarded feminine works of little use <note>This refers to traditional domestic chores like spinning or weaving, which the author contrasts with the "higher" fine arts.</note>, she gave herself so entirely to sculpting and painting, that she with the brush to paint

<summary>This page introduces Marcia, daughter of Varro, praising her for choosing a life of independent chastity and for her exceptional talent in painting and sculpture, which the author values over traditional domestic work.</summary>
<keywords>Marcia, Varro, virginity, chastity, women artists, painting, sculpture, 15th-century printing, Heinrich Steinhöwel, Boccaccio</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
->The 87th leaf<-

...and to carve figures out of ivory <note>original: 'helffenbain'</note>. She far surpassed Sopolis and Dionysius, who were the best painters of that same era; and although other good painters were found from time to time, none was seen who was more skillful in that art than she was. This is all the more worthy of praise. Great evidence of her skill was provided by a painting that was preserved for a long time after her death, in which she had depicted her own likeness with such detail in every limb and color that no one who had ever seen her would fail to recognize her appearance. 

And so that I may also recount some of her particular habits: she made it her custom to paint only portraits of women or to carve them in ivory, and seldom or never made portraits of men. I believe that maidenly modesty <note>original: 'Junckfrawliche scham'</note> was the reason for this. For since ancient times, it was the custom to create figures that were almost entirely naked. It was her opinion that if she did not depict the naked male figures with the correct proportions of their limbs <term>lidmaß: a term referring to the anatomical proportions and joints of the body</term>, she would be criticized for her ignorance. However, if she crafted all their members well, she would then have to abandon her maidenly modesty. Therefore, so that she would not fall into either of these improprieties, she decided it was better to avoid both.

->**Venus**<-
->**Iuphra**<- <note>This name likely refers to the artist known in other historical texts as Iaia of Cyzicus or Marcia.</note>

<summary>This page describes the exceptional skill of a female artist who surpassed her male contemporaries in painting and ivory carving. It highlights her dilemma as a woman in the arts, choosing to avoid depicting male nudes to balance professional technical standards with social expectations of modesty.</summary>

¶ **Chapter 66: About Sulpicia, the wife of Fulvius.**

**S**ulpicia was once considered the most venerable woman by the testimony of all the most noble Romans. She maintained her chastity and honor with no less conviction than Lucretia <note>Lucretia was a legendary Roman figure whose suicide after being raped by the son of the king sparked the revolution that overthrew the monarchy and established the Republic.</note>, who killed herself to give witness to the chastity of her heart. Sulpicia was the daughter of Servius Paterculus and the wife of Fulvius, both of whom were among the most noble Romans. 

And at a time when the Senate, through the <term>Decemviri</term> <note>The *Decemviri Sacris Faciundis* were a college of ten men with priestly duties, specifically the guarding of the Sibylline Books.</note>, had ordered a statue to be dedicated to the goddess Venus "the Turner of Hearts" <note>This refers to *Venus Verticordia*, an aspect of the goddess believed to turn the hearts of women away from lust and toward virtue.</note> according to the instructions of the Sibylline Books <note>A collection of oracular prophecies consulted by the Roman Senate during times of crisis or religious importance.</note>, it was done so that maidens and other wives might withdraw themselves entirely from unchastity. It was also intended that they might more easily acquire and preserve feminine modesty and honor. 

According to the order of the ten men, the Senate desired that the single woman judged the purest and most chaste of all Roman women should dedicate that same statue. Out of the immense multitude of noble and honorable Roman women, one hundred of the most highly praised were chosen. Among those hundred was Sulpicia. From those same hundred, ten of the most honorable were selected; among those ten was Sulpicia. Finally, at the command of the Senate, from those ten, one was elected unanimously by a common vote of the women: that woman was Sulpicia. 

And although it was considered a highly venerable honor at that time to dedicate a statue to Venus, it was nonetheless immeasurably more beautiful that she was judged by such a great multitude of all women to be the most illustrious in all honors. She was placed above all other women in constant chastity and was looked upon by everyone as if she were a goddess come down from heaven. For she possessed, more than all others, everything that belongs to a highly honored woman. For it is not enough for a completely honorable woman to simply guard herself... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence here and likely continues on the following page regarding the requirements of a virtuous life.</note>

<summary>This page describes the selection of Sulpicia, wife of Fulvius, as the most chaste woman in Rome, granting her the honor of dedicating a statue to Venus Verticordia.</summary>
<keywords>Sulpicia, Fulvius, Venus Verticordia, Chastity, Roman Virtue, Senate, Decemviri, Sibylline Books, Lucretia</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the virtues of women, specifically focusing on the preservation of chastity and the conduct of an honorable wife, concluding with the historical example of Sulpicia.</meta>

The 88th leaf

from the embrace of other men or lying with them; she is thus protected so that she cannot do so by compulsion. Therefore, so that a woman may be called entirely honorable, she must tame and cast down her wandering, unchaste eyes, and conceal and protect them with head-cloths <note>This refers to the traditional veils or wimples worn by modest women in the 15th and 16th centuries to signify piety and marital status.</note>. She should also constrain her tongue, not only to honorable words but to few and necessary speeches. Idleness is harmful to chastity; it is the enemy that should be fled above all others. From gluttony <note>original: "schleckmeilung"; literally "tasting" or "dainty-eating," referring to the vice of seeking out delicate or expensive foods.</note> an honorable woman should withdraw, for without the excess and pleasure of food, Venus <note>original: "Venus"; the translator uses the name of the Roman goddess of love to represent physical desire or lust.</note> grows cold. Singing, dancing, and leaping are to be avoided as robbers of modesty. In place of frivolity and unchastity, one should manage the house with temperance and the rejection of pleasure. One should stop up the ears against excessive talk, much chattering, and the telling of tall tales. One should guard against wandering about too much and despise the excessive adornment of clothing. Evil thoughts and harmful desires should be quickly trodden underfoot while keeping watch in holy works and thoughts. And so that I do not recount every single thing: a praiseworthy, highly honored woman should honor her husband with the highest love and place everyone else behind him; yet she should love others with brotherly loyalty. She should also not approach the embrace of her own husband without a blush and a sense of modesty in her spirit, and always be in the hope of becoming pregnant by him, for which purpose marriage was established. And since such circumstances are perhaps not found so purely and completely among all others—even among hundreds or tens—as they were in the unique Sulpicia <note>original: "Supplicia"; likely referring to Sulpicia, the daughter of Vulturnus, who was historically chosen as the most chaste woman in Rome to dedicate a statue to Venus Verticordia (the "Changer of Hearts").</note>, she is rightfully placed before all others in purity.

<summary>This page outlines the behavioral expectations for an honorable wife, emphasizing modesty in speech, dress, and diet as safeguards for chastity. It concludes by praising Sulpicia as the historical ultimate example of feminine purity.</summary>
<keywords>chastity, Sulpicia, marriage, modesty, temperance, Venus, domestic virtue, womanhood</keywords>

# Folio 84.
## Concerning Harmonia, daughter of Gelon of Sicily
### Chapter 67.

[Woodcut illustration depicting two men with swords attacking a woman lying on the ground, while another woman stands to the right. Labels within the woodcut:]
Harmonia
[on scroll:] I am the true one <note>German: *ich bin die recht*. This refers to the lady-in-waiting who identifies herself as the princess to save Harmonia’s life.</note>

**Harmonia of Sicily** was a daughter of Gelon, the King of the Syracusans <note>Original Latin: *Syracusarum*. Gelon II was a co-regent of Syracuse.</note>, whose brother was named Hiero. And although she was born of illustrious nobility, she is remembered much more for her great and faithful goodness. For at that time, the Syracusan people rose up in a riot and, raging blindly and senselessly against the King and all his lineage, had sworn together to bring about their deaths. And when the three royal youths—Hieronymus, Andronodorus, and Themistus—had been killed by them, they also sought to kill the daughters of King Hiero, Damarata and Heraclea, as well as Harmonia, Gelon’s daughter. As they were prepared for her death with naked swords, it happened through the arrangement of the governess <term>zuchtmeisterin: a mistress of the household or female tutor responsible for the conduct of young noblewomen</term> that another lady-in-waiting was dressed in all the finery and garments of Queen Harmonia to die in her place. She offered herself willingly to all...

<summary>This page recounts the beginning of the story of Harmonia of Syracuse, a noblewoman whose life was threatened during a violent democratic uprising against the Syracusan monarchy. It describes a loyal lady-in-waiting's attempt to sacrifice herself by dressing in the princess's clothes to deceive the assassins.</summary>

<keywords>Harmonia of Syracuse, Gelon, Sicily, Hiero, revolution, sacrifice, loyalty, Hieronymus, noblewomen</keywords>

# Leaf 84

...to accomplish this. So much so that she, undaunted by death, would not flee the sharp swords, nor would she say that she was not the one whom they sought to kill. Furthermore, she never revealed the hidden Harmonia <note>Harmonia was the daughter of Gelon of Syracuse; during a revolt, her life was sought by a mob</note>, for whom she was being killed. Instead, remaining silent and unmoved, she accepted the deadly fate and died blessed in great faithfulness—though it was a miserable and evil death <note>original: "vnsälige pöses todes"; referring to the violent nature of the execution</note>. 

But the dear Harmonia, while she was hidden, nevertheless saw the faithful steadfastness of the innocent maiden and her strong spirit even unto death through the wounds of her chaste blood-spilling. Because of this, she was rightfully and deeply struck with grief. And although she might have escaped after the departure of the tyrants <note>original: "wüttrich," meaning bloodthirsty or cruel rulers/oppressors</note>, she nonetheless began to reflect inwardly through the great goodness of her heart. With tear-filled eyes, she marveled at that constant loyalty maintained even unto death, and she felt she could no longer prolong her own life without making a repayment. She resolved in her mind that it would be better to travel to the underworld with such a faithful companion than to live in dread among unfaithful citizens. 

That ancient, kind loyalty, which had escaped the hands of the murderers, then came forward and called back the bloody swords. She recounted the trickery of her mistress <note>Referring to the ruse where the servant dressed in the mistress's clothes to deceive the killers</note>, the innocence of the faithful maiden, and revealed her own true identity. She offered herself up to the blood-spilling and was willingly hacked to pieces by many wounds, so that she might show repayment to the faithful maiden beside her dead body. 

Although antiquity has now almost erased this history, it nonetheless seems right to me that such goodness should not be entirely worn away; therefore, I wished to renew it. And yet, it is not easy to distinguish whether the loyalty of the dying...

### ¶ Of Busa of Canosa, from Naples. The 68th Chapter.

[A woodcut illustration depicting a woman standing on the right, labeled "Busa" in a small box above her head. She is gesturing towards a group of men seated around a circular table, some of whom appear to be eating or drinking. The scene is indoors.]

**Busa**, whom some call Paulina, was born in the city of **Canosa** <note>original: "camisium"; modern Canosa di Puglia</note> in Apulia. She was wealthy and of high nobility, as her eternally great deed clearly demonstrates. For as the ancients say, during the time when **Hannibal** <note>The Carthaginian general who crossed the Alps to fight Rome</note> harassed the Romans with powerful wars and afflicted all of Italy with fire and sword <note>original: "feŵr vnnd eyßen", literally 'fire and iron'</note>, and defiled the entire land with manifold bloodshed, a battle was fought near Naples by the small town of **Cannae**. In this battle, Hannibal not only defeated the Romans but nearly broke the entire power of the whole land known as Italy. It happened that from this battle and the immense slaughter, some escaped by fleeing. About ten thousand of them arrived at Canosa during the night by various winding paths, for that city was still in alliance <note>original: "einigkeit", meaning unity or concord</note> with the Romans. These same miserable, weary, poor, and defenseless <note>original: "waffen", likely truncated for 'waffenlos'</note>...

<summary>This page introduces the story of Busa of Canosa, a noblewoman who provided refuge and aid to Roman soldiers fleeing the disastrous defeat by Hannibal at the Battle of Cannae.</summary>
<keywords>Busa of Canosa, Paulina, Hannibal, Battle of Cannae, Apulia, Roman history, female virtue, Second Punic War</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical accounts of famous women, specifically detailing the Roman noblewoman Busa of Canosa and her actions following the disastrous Roman defeat at the Battle of Cannae.</meta>

### The 85th Page

->**Busa**<- kindly received the abandoned, naked, and severely wounded soldiers into her own house, remaining unafraid of such great misfortune and the power of the victorious enemy. And above all, she comforted the spirits of the wretched with the hope of better fortune. She provided doctors and medicine for the injured according to motherly loyalty and love; she clothed the naked and generally demonstrated her generosity toward all their needs. She gave them daily sustenance from her own property. And when the wretched were well-rested and restored to good hope through her courtly kindness, and now wished to depart from her, they were provided for by her with provisions for the journey, so much so that her generous hands were never drawn back from any necessity.

This is, without doubt, wonderful to say and far more praiseworthy in a woman than in a man. For Alexander the Great, the conqueror of the world, is most highly praised by the ancient writers above all for his generosity, and because he gifted his friends not with small jewels, money, horses, and the like, but honored them with great principalities, powerful kingdoms, and vast empires; he also sometimes honored those who had been conquered by him. 

Oh, how beautiful and how high this is to praise in him! But in my opinion, it is not to be compared to Busa's praiseworthy great deeds. For Alexander was a man, while Busa was a woman—who are generally and by nature more stingy <note>original: *zäher*, literally "tougher" or "clinging," reflecting the author's period-typical view of women and money</note> and more inclined to withhold <note>original: *entpfalten*, meaning to keep or fold away</note> than to give. She was mistress of her own property in her own right, whereas he [Alexander] took what he had from others by force. She had possessed her wealth peacefully through inheritance and, had she wished, could have possessed it longer; he perhaps feared he might not be able to keep what he had won. He gave to friends and those who had served him well; she gave to strangers and un... <note>The text breaks off here, likely continuing "unknown people" on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page highlights the extraordinary charity of the Roman noblewoman Busa, who saved thousands of soldiers after the Battle of Cannae. The author argues her generosity is superior to that of Alexander the Great because she gave from her own inheritance to strangers during a time of crisis.</summary>

<keywords>Busa of Canosa, Alexander the Great, generosity, Battle of Cannae, Roman history, charity, inheritance, gender roles</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues a comparison between the Greek conqueror Alexander the Great and the noblewoman Busa of Canosa, likely from a translation of Giovanni Boccaccio’s 'De Mulieribus Claris' (Of Famous Women).</meta>

[Handwritten notes in top right corner]

->The 96th leaf<-

recognized. He <note>Alexander the Great</note> gave to the fortunate; she <note>Busa of Canosa, a noblewoman who aided Roman survivors after the Battle of Cannae</note> gave to the wretched surrounded by misfortune. He gave foreign things in foreign lands; she gave under her own roof among her own people. He gave for worldly fame and honor; she gave to show help to the needy and wretched. What more shall I say? If we wish to consider the spirit, the womanly nature, and the form of both these deeds, I have no doubt that by an impartial judge, Busa and her generosity would be much more highly praised than Alexander for all the fame and honor he acquired through his great power. But every deed is praised according to the opinion of the judge. In my judgment, Busa used her own property exceptionally well. For nature did not draw and bring forth gold out of the earth so that it should return back into it—as the greedy do who bury it, keeping it with such great care as if it were to be born again—but rather so that it might be useful for the common good and shine in an honorable way, helping us and our friends in the daily needs of living. And if there were anything left over, it should be shared with the lonely, the ruined, the poor, the countless oppressed, the imprisoned, the sick, and all others in need. This should be done generously from a free spirit, not for fame or worldly honor but to provide help; not for gain but out of goodness. And yet, giving should be done with such discretion <note>original: "bescheydenheit" — here meaning prudence or moderation</note> that we do not ourselves fall into need through our giving, which would then force us to require the help of others.

Leaf .86.

[Woodcut illustration showing four figures. Above them are the labels: **Laelius** <note>Gaius Laelius, a Roman general and close friend of Scipio Africanus.</note>, **Masinissa**, and **Sophonisba**. **Sophonisba**, although she was of illustrious birth from the royal lineage of the <term>Numidians</term> <note>The Numidians were an ancient Berber people of North Africa, living in what is now Algeria and Tunisia.</note>, she has nevertheless been remembered much more vividly because of the severe harshness of her death, which she carried out herself. She was the daughter of Hasdrubal <note>Hasdrubal Gisco, a leading Carthaginian general.</note>, whose father was called Gisco, the highest prince at Carthage, at the time when Hannibal afflicted the foreign lands called Italy. When she had grown into her blooming years, she was married by her father to the powerful King of the Numidians, Syphax. And this happened not only out of regard for the kinship of the royal lineage, but also so that her father might withdraw the king and his assistance from the Romans, with whom the king had previously been allied. He intended that through the coaxing of his daughter, she might make him an enemy of the Romans however she could. This indeed came to pass; for when the king had celebrated the wedding days, he was drawn to her love and ensnared so fervently by the lady’s lovely coaxing and great beauty, that he did not think to himself...

<summary>This page introduces Sophonisba of Carthage, describing her noble lineage and her strategic marriage to King Syphax of Numidia. The text explains how her beauty and influence were used as political tools to turn Syphax away from his Roman alliance during the Punic Wars.</summary>
<keywords>Sophonisba, Carthage, Numidia, King Syphax, Hasdrubal Gisco, Second Punic War, Roman History, Marriage Alliances</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the story of Sophonisba, a Carthaginian noblewoman and the daughter of Hasdrubal Gisco, who was famous for her beauty and her political influence during the Second Punic War.</meta>

# The 97th leaf.

...nothing could be dear, pleasant, or joyful to him, except for his wife alone. But as the wretched king was so burning <note>original: "brynnēd"; likely referring to his passionate love or infatuation with Sophonisba</note> with passion, it happened that the Roman commander Cornelius Scipio intended to cross from Sicily into Africa with his fleet. <note>original: "hözē" literally means "woods," a common period reference to wooden ships.</note> Sophonisba was warned of this by her father, and with flattering prayers and caresses, she moved the mind of her husband, Syphax, to her will and desire: that he should not only desert the Romans—to whom he was bound by oaths—and pledge himself to the Carthaginians, but that he himself should become the volunteer commander of this foreign war. And so, in broken faith and vows which he had recently promised to that same Scipio, he forbade him by letter from crossing into Africa.

But Scipio, a youth of high spirit, condemned the wickedness of the unfaithful king and stationed his fleet not far from Carthage, intending first to drive out the faithless king, which indeed happened. Through King Masinissa <note>The text spells this "Masmissa," a common variation of Masinissa, the King of Numidia.</note> and his companion Laelius, who was sent with him by the Romans, Syphax's armies were scattered; he was captured, bound, and led before the city of his royal throne, and there, weighed down with chains, he was shown to his citizens. By them, the city was opened and surrendered to the Romans. 

Before Laelius arrived there in the sudden change and great turmoil, Masinissa entered the royal hall fully armed and saw Queen Sophonisba coming toward him, contemplating her misfortune. And when she saw that he was shaped more nobly and better adorned with weapons and armor than the others, she thought at once that he was the King, and she fell at his feet, thinking how she might move him to mercy through gentle speech, and spoke thus: "It is by the gods' and your own blessedness..."

<summary>This page describes how Sophonisba persuaded her husband, King Syphax, to betray his Roman alliance, leading to his defeat, capture, and the eventual confrontation between Sophonisba and the conqueror Masinissa.</summary>

### The 97th Leaf

...look upon us. You, who shortly before this time were kings like us, may now accomplish all things according to your will. Yet, if it is permitted that a prisoner, before their victorious conqueror and lord, might utter a prayerful voice concerning life and death and touch his knees and victorious hand—then I, who have been cast down, plead with your majesty (in which state I myself existed shortly before). 

I ask by your name, your kingdom, and your entire lineage, and by the fact that you have entered this realm with a better destiny <note>The text refers to Masinissa’s successful invasion of Numidia as an ally of Rome.</note> than that from which Syphax has come: that you would deal with me, an unfortunate woman, in a way that may be called good and merciful in your eyes. However, I ask this only so far as I am not handed over alive to the violent, harsh Romans, who are especially hostile toward the Carthaginians. 

For you can easily understand that I—an enemy of the Romans, a woman of Carthage, the daughter of Hasdrubal <note>Hasdrubal Gisco, a Carthaginian general and statesman.</note>, and the wife of Syphax—have just cause for fear. And if all other ways are closed, then let it be your will that I die by your hand, rather than being led alive into the power of my enemies.

I plead for this with deep, inner supplication. Masinissa was also from Numidia and was quick to physical desire, as are all others from that land. He was moved by the great beauty of the lady; her misfortune had only made her more lovely, which led him to receive the prayers of this unlucky woman more kindly and humanely. 

And before Laelius <note>Gaius Laelius, a Roman general and close friend of Scipio Africanus, who was co-commanding the campaign.</note> arrived, Masinissa offered the lady his right hand and raised her up from the earth, amidst the wailing and weeping of the other women surrounding her. Immediately, he betrothed her to himself as his lawful wife and held the wedding ceremony amidst the clatter and tumult of weapons. By doing so, he believed he had found a path of mercy that satisfied both his own physical desire and the desperate prayers of Sophonisba <note>Sophonisba (c. 248–203 BC) was a Carthaginian noblewoman whose beauty and influence were legendary; she chose death over Roman captivity.</note>.

<summary>Sophonisba, the Carthaginian wife of the defeated King Syphax, pleads with the conqueror Masinissa to save her from Roman captivity, leading him to impulsively marry her on the battlefield.</summary>
<keywords>Sophonisba, Masinissa, Syphax, Carthage, Numidia, Second Punic War, Romans, Hasdrubal, Laelius</keywords>

On the following day, Laelius <note>Gaius Laelius, a Roman general and close friend of Scipio Africanus.</note> arrived, by whose command Masinissa <note>A Numidian prince and Roman ally.</note> marched with him, adorned in all royal splendor. There, they were first well received by Scipio <note>Scipio Africanus, the famous Roman commander.</note>, for they had successfully carried out all that had been commanded of them. 

Afterward, when Masinissa was rebuked with kind words for having married a prisoner of the Romans on his own accord, he went away from them both into his tent, overcome with sadness. And when he was sated with sighing and weeping—to such an extent that his neighbors could clearly hear his lament—he called for one of his servants whom he knew to be faithful. He ordered him to prepare a strong poison, according to the misfortune fated for Sophonisba. He commanded the servant to mix it into a drink, bring it to her, and say: that the loyalty and vow he had willingly given her, he would gladly have kept if he could. But since his own will had been broken by others <note>A reference to the Roman authorities who demanded she be handed over as a prisoner of war.</note>, he wished at least to grant her the "safe-conduct" of ensuring she would not be led alive into the power of the Romans. 

And if she still desired this, he sent her this drink; yet she should be mindful of how she had been lawfully wedded to two kings and had been queen of the land shortly before, and should do as she saw fit. 

As soon as she heard this, she spoke with a manly <note>The original term *männlicher* implies stoic courage or fortitude, a quality highly praised in the classical world even when displayed by women.</note> bearing to the servant: "Thus I receive this wedding gift; and since nothing else could be given to me by my husband, I accept this with gratitude. But tell him: dying would have been easier for me had I remained unmarried <note>original: "unverändert" — referring to her sudden marriage to Masinissa after her previous husband's defeat.</note> at the time I am to become a corpse." She gave no sharper words, but received the drink, and without any trembling, she drank it all. She did not swell for long, but soon fell into the death she had desired. In truth, it would...

### ¶ Concerning Theosena, daughter of Prince Herodicus. Chapter 70.

[Woodcut illustration: Several figures are depicted in a small boat on a choppy sea. One figure, labeled **theosena**, is shown falling headfirst into the water on the right.]

She had a blood sister named Archo, born of the same father, who was also killed through the treachery of this same Philip. Furthermore, through ever-increasing betrayal, he had both of the sisters' husbands miserably stabbed to death; however...

But Theoxena—although she was entreatingly urged by many powerful men—nevertheless maintained her status as a widow for a longer time with a strong spirit. But when Archo passed away in death, Theoxena felt great compassion for her sister’s children and was concerned that they might fall under the power of another stepmother, or be raised by their father with less diligence. Therefore, so that those same children would be held as her own, she took the same Poris as her lawful husband, for at that time there was no law against it. <note>Under some ancient laws or customs, marrying a sister's widower was permitted to ensure the continuity of the family line and the protection of orphaned children.</note> And she began to raise them in the same manner as if they had been born from her own body, so diligently that one could clearly recognize that she had taken the same man more for the service of the children than for her own benefit.

At that same time, it happened that the same King Philip <note>Philip V of Macedon (reigned 221–179 BC), who struggled against the rising power of the Roman Republic.</note>, through his restless mind and spirit, considered how to stir up war against the Romans, at a time when they were flourishing in fortunate prosperity. And to protect himself against the Romans’ power, he had all his citizens driven out of his cities in Thessaly—that is, Macedonia—into the land of <term>Emathia</term> <note>Emathia was a historical region of the Macedonian Kingdom; Philip moved populations to consolidate his defense and reward loyalists.</note>, and he garrisoned his cities with the people of Thrace, whom he considered more warlike and more faithful to him. Because of this, he felt no small ill-will and words of reproach from those who had been driven out. And he considered the boys whose fathers he had killed, and he believed his land could not stand in safety as long as they were not all killed. And he commanded that they be seized and placed in the dungeon, and not killed all at once, but in due time...

<meta>This page continues the tragic story of Theoxena (here called Theosena), a noblewoman fleeing the persecution of King Philip V of Macedon. ->The 100th Leaf<-

[those who] were stationed saw the ship departing from the land. They suspected their flight and intended to detain them, and set out with a well-equipped and armed ship, with such orders and commands that they should not return without the fleeing ship. 

But when Porris saw them coming toward him, he considered the impending distress and the worry in which he stood, and urged the mariners to row with all their strength as fast as each could; then he prayed to the gods to send him help in his needs. 

But when Theoxena saw Porris praying so fervently, she began to consider her distress and anxious worry, and realized the time had now come to carry out her deadly plan. Quickly, she mixed the deadly poison—previously prepared for this—with the wine, and bared the swords and laid them before her own and her sister's children, and spoke thus: 

"Beloved ones, we all have neither comfort nor help, nor is any salvation ordained for us, so we cannot avenge ourselves except through death. For this, you have these two ways: the swords and the drink; each may choose which he prefers, so that we may escape the haughty arrogance of the king. Therefore, most beloved youths, show your noble spirit and manfully take up the weapons or drink the poison, while the raging sea <note>Original: "mōz," an abbreviation for "Meere" (sea).</note> yet denies us life." 

While she spoke thus, the king's servants had now come close to them, and as the youths hesitated to fulfill her command, she—the grim author <note>Original: "ſaffterin" (Schöpferin), meaning creator or instigator.</note> of such deadly evil—began to force and compel them. And when that happened, and the children were now struggling in the throes of death from the drink and the wounds, Theoxena ordered them to be thrown out of the ship into the sea. She, who had raised her children so tenderly, preferred to have killed them rather than have them fall into the king's bondage. <note>Original: "eygenſchafft," referring to the state of being a king's property or slave.</note>

But when the captain of the prisoners (who had been appointed by Manlius) noticed the beauty and blooming youth of the queen, he was ignited with a disordered love—so much so that, forgetting Roman honor, he knew the queen by force <note>The original German "mit notzwangen" indicates a violent sexual assault or rape.</note>. Because of this, she was so greatly burdened and so deeply troubled in her heart that she constantly thought more of avenging this evil than of freeing herself from captivity. ->The 102nd leaf<-

...should kill him and cut off his head, which was all accomplished according to her plan. Immediately she took the head in her lap and went away with good fortune according to her timely deliberation. <note>The story describes Chiomara, who was captured by the Romans. A centurion raped her and then agreed to a ransom; Chiomara used the ransom meeting as an opportunity to have her servants kill him.</note> And when she came home before the face of her husband and recounted to him all the shame that had been inflicted upon her, she threw before his feet what she had brought in her lap and said: "Lord, behold the reward for the shame and the vindication <note>original: 'entschuldigung'—here meaning 'clearing of guilt' or 'justification'</note> of womanly modesty and purity of spirit."

Who would not only praise her among other Roman women, but also place her on par with Lucretia? <note>Lucretia was a famous Roman figure who died by suicide to preserve her honor after being raped. She was considered the ultimate model of female virtue in the Renaissance.</note> The prison still stood before her, as did the chains, and deadly weapons were all around her; yet she thought more of avenging the defilement <term>defilement: 'vermeiligūg', referring to the violation of her body</term> of her body than of her release from captivity. Even though she was greatly concerned that she might be locked in chains again, or thrown back into the dungeon as a prisoner, or even have her own head prepared for the sword, nonetheless this woman set her honorable, manly spirit <note>original: 'manlichs gemüt'. In this period, calling a woman 'manly' was the highest praise for her courage and resolve.</note> in such great strength that she firmly commanded her servants to avenge the crime committed against her by the violator.

Where could you ever find a bolder person, a more manly prince, or a harsher judge over evildoers? She would rather be completely imprisoned and choose death than return to her husband in unavenged shame. She also believed she could not vindicate her defiled body except through great deeds and by demonstrating the purity of her spirit; for through such actions, womanly discipline and honor are preserved, and that which was lost is restored. Thus, witness is given to a pure spirit. This is what women should look to if they wish to strengthen the purity of their spirit. For it is not enough to testify to the purity of the heart with tearful crying and lamenting, or with words about the violence suffered...

### ¶ Concerning Tertia Aemilia, the wife of the first Scipio Africanus. The 74th chapter.

[Woodcut illustration showing several figures. Labels above figures: *Aemilia*, *the maid*, *servant*]

Although Tertia—by her birth into the <term>Aemilian family</term> <note>One of the most ancient and influential patrician houses in Rome</note>, and also by her marriage to the most illustrious Scipio Africanus the Elder—was exceedingly noble, she appeared far more illustrious because of her own great character. For although this same Scipio, when he was younger, famously returned a beautiful captive maiden in the bloom of her youth to her fiancé, the prince Allucius <note>original: "lüceio"</note>, along with the gold her father had sent to ransom her from captivity, he could not withdraw himself from the damned impurity of carnal desire as he grew older. He fell into love and intimacy with his own maidservant. Since it is very difficult to long conceal the results of such improper love within a marriage, it could not remain hidden that Tertia eventually became aware of everything.

<summary>This page introduces the story of Tertia Aemilia, praising her nobility and character while contrasting it with the infidelity of her husband, the famous Roman general Scipio Africanus, in his later years.</summary>

<keywords>Tertia Aemilia, Scipio Africanus, Aemilian family, fidelity, Roman virtue, Allucius, marriage</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of a noble woman, likely Aemilia Tertia, the wife of Scipio Africanus the Elder, focusing on her extraordinary patience and discretion regarding her husband's infidelity.</meta>

[Handwritten at top right: 109 / [111]]

->The .103rd. leaf<-

And who can doubt that she bore these things in her spirit with great difficulty? For some women say truly and without any shame that nothing more shameful, nothing more unbearable, and nothing more sorrowful can happen to a wedded wife than that the justice of her marriage bed <note>The "justice" or "rights" of the marriage bed refers to the exclusive legal and moral claim a wife held over her husband’s fidelity.</note> should be granted to another woman. And by God, I can well believe it; for without doubt there was never a more suspicious creature than a woman. This comes either from the softness of her spirit or from the opinion and worry that they might be held in lower esteem; for as soon as a man begins to dally with another, they believe it all happens to the diminishing of their friendship and love. 

But however difficult this was, the noble woman bore it with such a strong spirit and kept it so secret and hidden that not only did no stranger learn of her husband's guilt through her, but even her own husband could never perceive from her that she held any suspicion of what he had begun. For the wise <note>Original: "vernünfftig"</note> woman believed it would be quite unseemly to reveal that such a man—who had made many kings and powerful lands and peoples subject to him—should be brought low by the love of a serving maid. 

It did not even seem enough to this holy woman that she kept this ugliness hidden as long as her husband Scipio was alive. But even after his death, she considered how the memory of such a misdeed should be removed from so high a man and the sin entirely forgotten. And so that no cause remained to remember such things, she felt it unseemly that a woman <note>referring to the maidservant Scipio had been involved with</note> who had shared in such a man should wrongly live henceforth in bonded property <term>bonded property: "eigenschafft" refers to the legal status of serfdom or slavery</term> and servitude, or be shamed; nor should she serve any other henceforth in an unseemly...

<summary>This page describes the remarkable stoicism of Scipio Africanus's wife, who hid her husband's affair with a maid to protect his reputation, eventually freeing the maid after his death to erase all evidence of his lapse in judgment.</summary>
<keywords>Scipio Africanus, Aemilia Tertia, fidelity, marriage rights, nobility, virtue, servitude, reputation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Tertia Aemilia, the wife of Scipio Africanus, from the previous page. The text describes her remarkable reaction to discovering her husband's affair with a servant girl.</meta>

The 103rd leaf

desire was mixed. Because she <note>The servant girl</note> might be further weakened by it, she betrothed the same companion of hers to a well-to-do man, with whom she was well-provided for according to her honor. O, the great desire and true love of this woman to protect her husband's honor! With a free spirit, she released her own servant, who was her <term>slave-concubine</term> <note>original: "göll," a term for a female servant or slave, often implying a sexual relationship with the master</note>, and gave her away and provided for her. O holy lady, your praise is rightly to be raised up to the heavens! For you have patiently, with a gentle spirit, borne your husband’s fault, and furthermore released your slave from <term>bondage</term> <note>original: "eygenschafft," referring to the legal state of serfdom or slavery</note>. And the more such kindness is rare, the more it is to be considered shining and great. Another woman would have screamed, called all her friends and neighbors to council, and led a shameful complaint: that she was abandoned, she was despised, she was ruined by her husband, and in no way well-treated; but had become a widow during her husband's lifetime and was ranked lower than a shameful personal maid and a vile whore. She would also have treated the man badly and accused him, and would not have cared how she damaged the praise and honor of such a great man, just so she could recount her grievances with her gossip. But this Tertia overcame all adversity with her holy, strong spirit. Through this, she has earned eternal praise.

# The 114th leaf

<margin>110 / [112]</margin>

[A woodcut illustration depicting a woman, Claudia Quinta, pulling a ship with a rope. Several women stand behind her on the shore, and several men are on the ship.]

->**Claudia Quinta**<-

**C**laudia Quinta was a Roman woman. However, from what ancestry she came is not entirely cleared up <note>original: "geleütert," meaning clarified or refined; her lineage was debated by historians</note>; yet she achieved eternal renown through a particular, bold act of reputation. This same woman always applied great diligence, above all else, to the special adornment of her body, appearing too costly and overly dressed compared to all others, even the noblest Roman women. Because of this, she was judged by the most honorable and eldest Roman women to be not only weak-willed and dishonorable, but also shameful in her impurity <note>original: "vnlauterkeit"; this refers to suspicions regarding her chastity</note>.

And during the time when Marcus Cornelius and Publius Sempronius ruled in Rome—which was the fifteenth year of the second Roman war against Carthage <note>The Second Punic War, roughly 204 BCE</note>—it happened that the Mother of the Gods <term>Magna Mater: The goddess Cybele, whose cult was brought from Phrygia to Rome to help win the war</term>, an idol of the Romans, was being brought along the Tiber toward Rome. And to receive her, the "best man"—named Nasica <note>Publius Cornelius Scipio Nasica, chosen as the most virtuous man in Rome</note>—was sent to the shore by the senate of the entire city, along with all the noble Roman women. And when they arrived, the ship carrying the sacred relic <note>original: "heyltumb"; the goddess was represented by a black meteoric stone</note> ran aground on the bottom so that it...

<summary>This page introduces Claudia Quinta, a Roman woman whose reputation for vanity and unchastity was challenged during a religious crisis. It describes the arrival of the goddess Cybele (the Mother of the Gods) in Rome during the Second Punic War.</summary>

<keywords>Claudia Quinta, Magna Mater, Cybele, Second Punic War, Scipio Nasica, Roman virtue, Tiber River, chastity, ancient Rome</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Claudia Quinta, a Roman woman accused of unchastity, who is attempting to prove her innocence during the arrival of the goddess Cybele’s statue in Rome.</meta>

->The 104th Leaf<-

The sailors were in no way able to bring the ship forward. But when all the youths, using all their strength with ropes attached, could not move the ship, Claudia—who was among the other women and knew her own virtuous heart—fell down upon her knees. She humbly prayed to the goddess <note>This refers to the goddess Cybele, also known as the Magna Mater, whose statue was being transported to Rome in 204 BCE.</note> that if the goddess recognized the purity of her heart, she should then allow the ship to follow her girdle <note>original: "gürtel"; here referring to a belt or sash worn around the waist.</note>. 

And immediately, she stood up boldly, hoping to see what she had desired. She had her girdle tied to the ship and let all the young men move away. Then she pulled the ship all by herself to wherever she wished—the same ship that no one could move from the spot before. This happened to the exceeding great wonder of everyone. 

From this great miracle, it came to pass that this Claudia, who before had been seen by everyone as frivolous and shameless, was now conversely and immediately accepted with exceeding great praise as the most worthy and honorable of all Roman women. She, who had come to the shore in a shameful state of suspected weakness <note>original: "waichmütikeyt"; in this context, it implies a lack of moral firmness or unchastity.</note>, returned to her house well-adorned with purity and great honors. 

But because this slander against Claudia had this specific outcome according to her will, no person, however innocent they may be, should ever try such a thing again—to attempt to prove their innocence through things that are supernatural. For such an act is more a way of tempting God than a way of washing away the weight of sin cast upon them. We should perform holy works and live holy lives. And if we are judged to be evil, God does not leave it unrewarded; He allows us to be tested with adversity so that He may prove our patience, pull pride away from us, and so that we may practice our virtues and rejoice within ourselves when we know we are innocent.

<summary>Claudia Quinta proves her chastity by miraculously pulling a stuck ship using only her belt, leading the author to reflect on the nature of divine proof and the merit of enduring slander with patience.</summary>

<keywords>Claudia Quinta, Cybele, Roman history, chastity, miracles, virtue, patience, Magna Mater</keywords>

Therefore, if people do not hold us as such, we should not pay attention to that as long as we live rightly. And it is better that we let them think ill of us, than that we commit evil.

->¶ Concerning Hypsicratea, the Queen of the Great Sea.<-
->The 75th chapter.<-

**H**ypsicratea <note>Hypsicratea (1st century BCE) was the sixth wife of King Mithridates VI of Pontus.</note> was a consort of the great Mithridates and a queen of the sea <note>original: "möres"; likely referring to the Euxine or Black Sea, where the Kingdom of Pontus was located.</note>, exceedingly beautiful and [possessed] of an invincible love for her husband—so great that her name has been immortalized through it. For her husband Mithridates was in constant, great wars against the Romans, and although he had many wives according to pagan custom, she was nevertheless ignited by the torch of love more fervently than his other wives. Because of this, she never wished to leave him, neither on the sea nor on any military expeditions; for she believed no one could attend to him as well as she alone. 

She also knew that the service of the servants was for the most part unfaithful. And although fulfilling such tasks is rightfully difficult for a queen, she nevertheless set her mind firmly upon it. And so that she might do it more appropriately, she had her gold-colored hair cut off, laid aside her rings, brooches, and other feminine adornments, and covered her head with a helmet, and clothed her whole body in knightly fashion with a <term>cuirass</term> <note>A piece of armor consisting of a breastplate and backplate fastened together.</note>, coat of mail, and leg armor down to her feet. 

Thus she exchanged golden rings for the bow, precious clasps for shields, hair-pins <note>original: "kückel"; likely a variation of "Kunkel" (distaff) or related to hair ornaments, contrasting with spears.</note> for lances, and soft pillows for the hard saddle. Thus, at once, a gentle-spirited, softly-living queen was seen transformed into a stern knightly figure. And

### Concerning Sempronia the Roman, the 76th chapter.

**S**empronia was a beauty far surpassing many of the other noble Roman women. And her mind and reason were so great that her equal was not found. For in the Greek and Latin tongues she was so masterfully learned and practiced that she could compose and set artful poems in both languages; and whatever she heard or saw, she grasped nimbly. Additionally, she was so elegantly spoken that through her fine delivery she could move many to jest or earnestness, joy or sorrow—especially toward softheartedness and carnal desire whenever she wished, to which she herself was particularly inclined.

She could also sing laudably to all stringed instruments, which well suits a young woman when used rightly and modestly; but it is more commonly seen that such singing and string-playing become helpful tools for unchastity. And as soon as a stain falls therein and virginal purity is defiled, and the modesty <note>original: "töte," likely a variant of "röte," referring to the "shame-redness" or blushing of the face</note> of the face is laid aside, one no longer needs to woo, but rather such women will run to meet the men, enticing them. 

Furthermore, this same Sempronia was so excessively greedy that to win gold she made herself subject to the lust of any man. On the other hand, she was so [extravagant] <note>original: "geÿtig," here used in the sense of being "greedy to spend" or prodigal</note> that she uselessly consumed in luxury and wickedness all that she had unchastely won, so that no measure was set for her greed or her squandering. It is indeed a deadly evil and wickedness, this greed of women, and a manifest testimony of a defiled...

<summary>This page introduces the biography of Sempronia, a Roman noblewoman praised for her immense intellect and linguistic skills in Greek and Latin, but condemned for using these gifts and her musical talents to pursue a life of vice and greed.</summary>
<keywords>Sempronia, Rome, Boccaccio, women's education, Greek and Latin, music and morality, greed, unchastity</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of female virtues and vices, likely from a translation of Giovanni Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women). The previous sections likely dealt with other Roman women before transitioning here to the cautionary tale of Sempronia and the importance of household management.</meta>

### Leaf 106

In the same way, excessive generosity is almost to be destroyed. For whenever such extravagant generosity possesses a stingy heart—as all women have <note>This reflects a common medieval and early modern stereotype that women were inherently inclined toward greed or lacked the capacity for moderation.</note>—contrary to nature, no hope for well-being can be found there, except for the onset of poverty. Honor and wealth are entirely finished. For they cannot stop until they have reached the greatest disgrace and poverty. 

Women should be thrifty; it is their duty to faithfully preserve what has been won by men. And just as excessive greed and extravagant generosity are blamed in women, so much more is a woman's diligence praised for well-preserving the goods won by her husband. For she ethically increases wealth, she honorably preserves the household goods, she is a true witness of a whole and faithful spirit, a delight to labor, and a strong foundation for those who come after. 

Yet all this happened so that this Sempronia <note>Sempronia was a Roman noblewoman famous for her beauty and intelligence but also for her profligacy. The author uses her as a "negative" example.</note> would be entangled in evil. At the time when the rebellious and malicious Catiline <note>original: "kathelina"; Lucius Sergius Catilina, a Roman senator who led a famous failed conspiracy to overthrow the Republic in 63 BCE.</note>, with many others, had planned destructive discord against the Romans, she involved herself among them in order to better fulfill her unchastity. But when he and his followers were suppressed through the grace of God and the help of Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman orator who exposed the conspiracy.</note>, and some of them were killed, she also fell among them to a shameful death. 

There was another Sempronia, a daughter of Tiberius Sempronius Gracchus and Cornelia (the daughter of the great Scipio Africanus), and a sister of Tiberius and Gaius <note>The Gracchi brothers, famous Roman reformers.</note>. She was of such a steadfast mind that she would rather die than let a judge force her to take a man who was not her equal <note>She was pressured to identify an imposter as her brother; her refusal was seen as a mark of high character.</note>, about which I shall leave off writing for the sake of brevity.

<summary>This page contrasts the domestic virtues of thrift and diligence expected of women with the "shameful" life of Sempronia, who joined the Catiline conspiracy. It concludes by briefly mentioning a second, more virtuous Sempronia of the Gracchus family.</summary>

### ¶ About the women of the Cimbri <note>original: "cimbrorum"</note>, who are the Kurwalhen <note>A term used by early German writers to refer to the Rhaeto-Romanic people of the Alps, whom they believed were descendants of the ancient Cimbri.</note>, or as some would have it, Flemings. **O**f a greater, more constant, praiseworthy, and faithful marriage <note>Refers here to the devotion between spouses.</note>—worthy of being exalted even unto the heavens—nothing has ever been heard than of the great number of women who remained as widows after the slaughter and flight of their husbands, the **Cimbri** (whom we call **Kurwalhen**), which occurred in a hard battle against Gaius Marius the Roman <note>Gaius Marius (157–86 BC) was a Roman general who famously defeated the Cimbri at the Battle of Vercellae in 101 BC.</note>. 

For just as their number was so great, they are all the more worthy of being highly praised. We read of individual women who remained steadfast in the proper protection of their chastity, but we have never, or only rarely, heard of such a vast multitude of them gathered together in such virtue. 

During the times when the Romans were in their most flourishing rule, the Germans—who are called the **Cimbri** in Latin—and several other opposing regions rose up and swore a confederacy against the Romans. It was so strong and powerful that no one believed they could be resisted. They also brought their wives, children, and all their household goods with them on wagons, and suddenly fell upon...

When the women noticed this, they did not follow the flight of their husbands; instead, they made a wagon-fortress <note>a defensive circle of wagons</note> out of the great multitude of wagons upon which they had carried their household goods. For this reason, they requested with one mind not to be reconciled with their fleeing husbands, nor to return home to their houses, nor to receive restitution for their losses, but rather that they all might be placed into convents <note>original: "klōſter" – the author is using anachronistic Christian terms for this pre-Christian era</note> with other chaste virgins. **Gaius Julius** <note>original: "gay julij"</note> the Emperor and **Cornelia**, whose father was **Cinna** <note>The German text reads "prima," but historically refers to Lucius Cornelius Cinna</note>,

stained them with blood. Therefore, he sent the clothes home 
to put on others, and by chance, the pregnant **Julia** 
encountered the servant who was carrying them. As soon 
as she caught sight of her husband's bloody clothes, 
before she could even ask what had happened, she fell into a terrible suspicion 
that some violence had befallen her husband. And immediately 
her sight failed her, as if she could not bear to live after 
the death of her most beloved husband. With clenched hands 
and a stopped heart, she suddenly fell down and 
gave up her spirit, to the great misfortune not only 
of the Romans, but also of the entire world.

->**Porcia, Julius, Cassius, Brutus, Porcia**<-
<note>These labels identify the figures depicted in the accompanying woodcut illustration, showing the key players in the late Roman Republic.</note>

**Porcia** was the daughter of **Cato** <note>Marcus Porcius Cato, known as Cato the Younger (95–46 BCE), a Roman statesman and staunch defender of the Republic against Julius Caesar</note>, who was a follower of **Pompey**. And at the time when he [Cato] led the remnants of Pompey’s army out of Egypt—through the burning heat of the sun across Libya and into Africa—and heard that **Julius Caesar** had contested and overcome Pompey with the strength of his army, he became so intolerant of [Caesar’s] victory that he killed himself at the city of **Utica**. For this reason, he was called *Uticensis* <note>original: "vticensis"; a title meaning "of Utica," signifying his famous death there to escape Caesar's tyranny</note>. 

This same highly noble woman never wavered from her father’s strength and strictness. To say nothing of her many other bright virtues, she held her husband **Brutus**—to whom she had been given by her father—in such great honor and love, so completely and with such purity, that her devotion to her husband far surpassed all other womanly diligence and care. Nor could she hide the fitting flames of love within her heart when she found the proper time and place to show them; she revealed her spirit through her deeds.

<summary>This page introduces Porcia, daughter of Cato the Younger, highlighting her noble lineage, her father's suicide following Caesar's victory, and her profound devotion to her husband, Brutus.</summary>
<keywords>Porcia Catonis, Cato the Younger, Julius Caesar, Pompey the Great, Brutus, Roman Republic, Utica, Roman Civil War, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the Roman history sequence, specifically focusing on the transition from Julius Caesar's consolidation of power to the conspiracy led by Brutus and Cassius.</meta>

-># The 104th Leaf<-

At the time when the deadly fury of the wars and the discord among the Roman citizens was stilled, and the followers of Pompey <note>original: "pöpey"; referring to the political and military faction of Pompey the Great</note> were everywhere suppressed by Emperor Julius, the Emperor then intended to remain the permanent and unchanging ruler of the Roman Empire. However, many of the most powerful Romans conspired <note>original: "schwüren wid jn," literally "swore against him"</note> against him. Among these was Brutus, the husband of Portia. Brutus had no doubt regarding his wife’s steadfastness, and therefore he revealed to her the murderous plan against Emperor Julius.

It happened on the morning when Caesar was to be killed by the co-conspirators that Brutus went out from his bedchamber. Portia then took a razor, pretending that she wished to trim her fingernails, and intentionally let it fall onto her foot, wounding herself severely. When her maid saw the blood, she began to scream. Because of this, Brutus returned to see what the screaming meant and began to scold Portia, saying she should not have attempted to do a barber’s work <term>barber’s work: in the Middle Ages and Renaissance, barbers performed minor surgeries, bloodletting, and nail cutting</term> herself. 

But Portia sent her servant away and spoke to him: "What has happened was not done without premeditation, as you think. I did it because I wanted to test whether I could kill myself with a sword and endure death, should your undertaking against the Emperor meet a different end <note>i.e., should the conspiracy fail and lead to their capture</note>." 

For you have before you—O great strength! O inexhaustible love! O blessed man to have such a marriage! But to continue: the co-conspirators carried out the murder, yet they did not escape unpunished. For although the event went according to their will, the murderers were nevertheless condemned by the remaining part of the Senate. Consequently, they were forced to become fugitives <note>original: "landreümig," meaning to flee one's homeland under threat of law</note> and were scattered across many lands. But Brutus and Cassius traveled with a great army into the East <note>original: "orient"</note> with the intention of opposing the Emperor Octavian.

<summary>This page recounts the legendary story of Portia wounding herself to prove her courage to her husband Brutus before the assassination of Julius Caesar, and the subsequent exile of the conspirators.</summary>
<keywords>Julius Caesar, Brutus, Portia, Pompey, conspiracy, Roman Senate, Octavian, assassination, stoicism</keywords>

...and Anthony, who were the heirs of Julius [Caesar], to set themselves against the leaders Octavian and Anthony <note>original: "anthonium... octauianus vnd anthonius." The text here seems to describe the shifting alliances and civil wars following the death of Julius Caesar, specifically the conflict involving the Second Triumvirate.</note>. They led great armies and fought against them, overcame them, and destroyed their entire host. When Portia <note>Portia Catonis, the daughter of Cato the Younger and wife of Marcus Junius Brutus, one of Caesar’s assassins.</note> heard of this, she resolved to suffer death with a heavy heart, for she had previously suffered wounds from a knife-fall <note>original: "messerual." This refers to an incident where Portia stabbed herself in the thigh to prove to Brutus that she was brave enough to share his secrets.</note>. And she quickly returned to her old purpose; and as she sat by the fire and could not find any kind of weapon to kill herself as quickly as she desired, she took the burning coals in her hands without trembling and threw them into her throat. With these, she so burned her gullet and throat that her heart failed from a lack of breath. Who would doubt that, as strange and unusual as this death was, the greater this love for her husband is to be valued, and the more worthy it is of eternal remembrance.

¶ Concerning Hortensia, daughter of Quintus Hortensius <note>Quintus Hortensius was a famous Roman orator and rival of Cicero. His daughter, Hortensia, became famous for a speech she delivered before the Triumvirs.</note>
The 80th Chapter.

[Woodcut illustration depicting three men—the Triumvirs—seated on a bench on the left, gesturing towards a group of women on the right. One woman in the center of the group, labeled "hortenſia," addresses the men with a commanding gesture.]

[Label within the woodcut]
Hortensia

<summary>This page concludes the tragic story of Portia, who committed suicide by swallowing live coals after the defeat of Brutus, and introduces Hortensia, a woman celebrated for her oratorical skills in Rome.</summary>
<keywords>Portia, suicide, burning coals, Brutus, Octavian, Mark Antony, Hortensia, female orators, Roman history, loyalty</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographies of famous women, specifically moving into the Roman era. The previous section likely concluded the story of another virtuous woman of antiquity. The text is from a late 15th-century German edition of Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris."</meta>

->Leaf 110.<-

**H**ortensia was the daughter of the eloquent <term>Hortensius</term><note>Quintus Hortensius Hortalus (114–50 BCE), a famous Roman orator and rival of Cicero.</note>, and she is rightly to be highly exalted with worthy praise. For she did not only keep her father’s arts within her own mind, but she also knew how to express them with a clear voice, so gracefully and masterfully according to true art, and so lovably and movingly, that it was often too difficult even for many of the most highly learned in that same art.

It happened during the time when the Roman government was placed in the hands of <term>three men</term><note>The Second Triumvirate, consisting of Octavian (later Augustus), Mark Antony, and Lepidus, around 42 BCE.</note> that, due to a state of emergency concerning the common good, a large sum of money was ordered to be paid by the noble Roman women. Because of this, they were so burdened that their clothing and jewelry would not have been enough to pay such a tax. They could find no one who would entreat the men on their behalf, or who even dared to speak a word against the order of the three men to mitigate it.

Only **Hortensia** was so daring that she undertook to act as an advocate for the women, to protect them before the three men with a strong spirit. And she spoke so well, so lovably, and so excellently, that everyone marveled at her; they thought her father Hortensius had risen again from the grave and had been transformed into a woman. And this was not unbefitting: for just as her speech was in no way deficient, so her request was in no way diminished by the three men. Instead, a much larger part of the tax was remitted, after they considered what superior wisdom this woman possessed—wisdom which is usually praised only in secret and silence—and how graceful she appeared when her talents were brought to the light of day. Because of this matter, Hortensia was rightly celebrated and highly exalted by all Romans with great praise and honor. For the remaining money, which was then only a small amount, the women were easily able to pay.

### ¶ Of Sulpicia, wife of Truscellio. The 81st chapter.

<summary>This page recounts the story of Hortensia, a Roman woman who used her inherited oratorical skills to successfully protest an unfair tax levied against women by the Second Triumvirate. It highlights her rare public role as a female advocate in ancient Rome.</summary>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographies of famous women, following the previous accounts of Roman virtue and nobility. The text focuses on Sulpicia, daughter of Julia, during the period of the Second Triumvirate.</meta>

The 111th leaf
<margin>115 / [119]</margin>

**Sulpicia**, the noble Roman woman, is justly placed among other illustrious women because of the great love she bore for her husband. During the time when the <term>Three Men ruled at Rome</term><note>The Second Triumvirate: Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus (43–32 BCE).</note>, her husband, **Trusellio**<note>The original text refers to him as "Trustellio," though historical sources usually identify him as Cornelius Lentulus Cruscellio.</note>, was also <term>proscribed</term><note>Proscription was a state-sanctioned condemnation to death or exile, where a person’s name was posted on a public list and their property confiscated.</note> and condemned by them. However, he escaped to Sicily through a swift flight, where he remained in <term>exile and poverty</term><note>original: "ellend vñ armüt." In this period of German, "Ellend" specifically meant being "out of the land"—the state of being an exile—which resulted in misery.</note>.

When Sulpicia became certain of this, she declared that she intended to suffer toil and labor alongside her spouse. For it seemed quite unseemly to her to sit in great honors, live in luxury, and maintain a happy existence while her husband wasted away in exile and hunger. However, it was not easy for her to carry out her plan, for she was guarded with the greatest diligence by her mother, **Julia**, to ensure she did not follow her husband into his wretched state.

But there was never any guard so great that true love could not deceive them. Therefore, she waited for a suitable time for these matters, put on the clothes of a servant, and took with her no more than two maids and as many manservants. She deceived the watchfulness of her mother and the other guards, leaving her father's house and all her possessions to follow the misery of her spouse.

This she did even though, according to the established laws, she could have been divorced from that same man, held a wedding with a new husband, and lived in high honors. But the illustrious lady preferred, out of her singular love for her husband, to despise the storms of the sea and endure the heat of the burning sun. She was not frightened by the wilderness of the desert, mountains, or valleys, traveling through many unknown regions and suffering thousandfold care and anxiety to find her husband, who had been cast out by fortune. Whereas she might have spent her time in her house in luxury, softness, and a gentle life, she chose otherwise. This was not the act of a womanly spirit, but rather of a manly, thorough...

<summary>This page recounts the devotion of the Roman noblewoman Sulpicia, who defied her mother and the state to follow her proscribed husband into exile in Sicily. It highlights her choice of hardship and loyalty over a life of luxury and the possibility of a second, more prestigious marriage.</summary>
<keywords>Sulpicia, Trusellio, Julia, Roman history, Second Triumvirate, proscription, exile, female virtue, loyalty</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the conclusion of a previous biography (likely regarding a virtuous queen or female warrior) before beginning Chapter 82, the life of Mariamne, the Hasmonean princess and wife of Herod the Great.</meta>

->The 114th Page<-

The strong significance of shining wisdom is this: it is not always to appear in gold, precious stones, and other finery; nor always to behave in a courtly manner in a life of ease; nor always to flee the heat of the sun, or the snow and rain of winter. It is not always to indulge in desirable lusts or the bedchamber, but also to suffer labor and poverty with a strong spirit alongside those men whom the Wheel of Fortune <note>The *Rota Fortunae*, a medieval concept representing the unpredictable nature of fate, where kings are brought low and the poor are raised up.</note> has driven into misery. This is the distinguished knighthood <note>The author uses the term "ritterschafft" (knighthood) to describe female virtue, framing moral endurance as a form of chivalry.</note> of women; these are the struggles; these are the highest victories over their defeated enemy, which is weakness of spirit. Because of this, they shall be illustriously praised and honored, for they overcome physical lusts, an easy life, and pride with constancy, honor, and purity; from this comes high fame and eternal glory to them.

### ¶ About Mariamne. The 82nd Chapter.

**M**ariamne the Jewess was a daughter of Aristobulus, the Jewish king, born of Alexandra, the daughter of King Hyrcanus. She was of such great and unseen beauty that she not only surpassed all the most beautiful women of that time in beauty, but was also judged by many to be a heavenly image rather than a mortal one—as Mark Antony, one of the three men <note>The Triumvirate, the political alliance of Mark Antony, Octavian, and Lepidus that ruled Rome.</note>, well-testified. For Mariamne had a brother by the same father and mother named Aristobulus, who was of equal beauty and age to her. And when Alexandra desired from King Herod that he should grant the same Aristobulus the high office of the Priesthood, she sought to achieve this through the advice of her friend Dellius <note>original: "gellij"; referring to Quintus Dellius, a Roman politician and friend of Mark Antony.</note>, by having a master painter capture both of their likenesses...

# The 113th leaf

**C**leopatra was an Egyptian woman, and whether she traced her origin through many kings from Ptolemy—the son of Lagos, the King of the Moors <note>original: "moren künig"; likely a historical confusion between the Macedonian "Lagid" dynasty and the Moors of North Africa</note>—or, as others wish to have it, was the daughter of King Dionysius Ptolemy <note>referring to Ptolemy XII Auletes, often called Neos Dionysos</note> and Mineus; she nevertheless came to the rule of the kingdom through rogueish malice. Despite this lineage and the beauty of her body, she possessed no true radiance of character. For she was, on the contrary, seen shamelessly by the whole world because of her greed, unchaste deeds, and murderous evils. 

For Dionysius (or Mineus), her father, was a chief friend of the Romans and of Julius the Emperor <note>The text refers to Julius Caesar as "the Emperor," a common anachronism in medieval and early modern texts</note> in the first year of his reign. And when he was about to die, he left a testament behind him: that the eldest son, called Lisania <note>This name is likely a corruption or variation of Ptolemy XIII</note>, should take his sister Cleopatra as his wife (who was also the eldest daughter), as they were well able to do according to their laws; and the two of them should rule after his death. For shameful excess was common in Egypt, so that no physical kinship should prevent marriage—except only that of fathers and mothers toward their children—and so this was carried out. 

But when, in those days, a fervent desire to rule the kingdom alone began to grow, she fell into such deadly evil that she had that same youth—only fifteen years old, her brother and her husband—killed with a poisoned drink, so that she might rule the kingdom alone. But shortly thereafter it happened that the great Pompey won nearly all of Asia and marched with his army into Egypt; and there he made the younger son <note>Ptolemy XIV</note> the king and ruler of the realm of Egypt. Because of this, Cleopatra was so aggrieved that she prepared her weapons to fight against him. During these events, Julius the Emperor marched...

After so many nights <note>A reference to Cleopatra’s legendary sexual liaison with Julius Caesar</note> and because she had stood helpfully by him, he made her the powerful queen of all **Egypt**. Since she desired nothing else, he also took her sister away with him, so that she could not interfere with Cleopatra's reign. Thus Cleopatra, with seductive agility, kept the kingdom and took over such a government that she lived always according to the desires of her body. Furthermore, she was so greedy for gold and jewels that, in order to obtain them, she was seen as a common woman <note>original: "ein gemeine fraw"; a polite period term for a prostitute</note> by the kings of the East. Through this, she brought many lords to poverty. And not only did she rob those men, but she also plundered the temples, taking from them all ornaments of gold and precious stones and leaving them standing empty.

Some time later, after the Emperor **Julius [Caesar]** was killed, and **Brutus** and **Cassius** (who had killed him) were also defeated, **Antony** <note>Mark Antony, the Roman general</note> traveled into Syria. As soon as Cleopatra heard of this, she set out to meet him so delightfully and so well-adorned that through her beauty and her unchaste eyes, combined with seductive gestures, she might easily draw that impure man into her love.

In this love, he was ignited with such passion that, for the sake of her prayers—she who had previously killed her own brother and husband with poison—he strangled her sister **Arsinoe** with his own hand in the **Temple of Diana** <note>The Great Temple of Artemis at Ephesus, which served as a sanctuary for political refugees</note> in the city of the **Ephesians**, where the poor woman had fled in hope of safety. This was the first reward Cleopatra received from her new lover <note>original: "bülen"</note> for her adultery: that she would have less cause to fear any interference in her rule. And when that evil woman Cleopatra recognized Antony's character, she was not ashamed to demand from him the kingdoms of **Syria** and **Arabia**. However, when this seemed to him to be too much and excessively great—that he should satisfy every desire of the woman he loved—
