To satisfy her, he granted her fine portions of land, people, markets, and cities in both realms, and everything that lies between the great river **Eleutherus** <note>The Eleutherus is the modern Nahr al-Kabir river, forming the border between Lebanon and Syria</note> and Egypt at the edge of Syria, except only for the kingdoms of **Sidon** and **Tyre**, which he kept for himself. When she had taken possession of all these and Antony wished to march into Armenia, she accompanied him in the guise of a devoted lover as far as the river **Euphrates**. 

And as she intended to travel back home to Egypt through Syria, she was very well and honorably received by **Herod**, son of Antipater, who was the King of the Jews at that time. She was not ashamed to solicit him through go-betweens to have sexual relations with him, testing whether—if that occurred—she might strip him of his kingdom, which he had acquired through Antony’s help and had not possessed for long. 

But when Herod became aware of this, he refused such an affair, not only out of respect for Antony, but also so that he would remain untainted by such an impure woman. He would have had her killed if his advisors had not argued against it. And yet, to honor Antony, he gave her the revenues of **Jericho** <note>original: "jerethante"</note>, where the **balsam** <note>Jericho was famous in antiquity for its precious balsam groves, used for perfume and medicine</note> grew; she took some with her to **Babylon in Egypt** <note>This refers to the Roman fortress of Babylon in the Nile Delta, located in what is now Old Cairo, not the city in Mesopotamia</note> where it still grows to this day. 

Thus she traveled with these and other great gifts from Herod back to Egypt. Afterward, however, when Antony had put the Armenians to flight and summoned her to meet him—so that she might receive him all the more desirably with her embrace—he presented her with **Artavasdes**, the King of Armenia, along with his sons, all his advisors, and all his treasure, all of whom he had captured through treachery and wicked cunning. 

The greedy woman was exceedingly pleased by this and received her passionate lover so desirably, so tenderly, and with such enticing flattery, that he [neglected] his wife **Octavia**, who—

...abandoned the sister of the Emperor Octavian and wished to separate from her, because he loved Cleopatra so passionately and desired to take her as his wife. And at a time when they sat together at the table and lived according to every luxury, in such superfluous food and drink—so great that nothing for the body’s pleasure and desire could be imagined that was not sought out and found—Antony said to her, "What could ever equal our daily splendor and costly hospitality?" as if he were saying that no more costly meals could be prepared than those readied for them daily. 

The unchaste, impure woman <note>The author uses moralizing language common in 15th-century German chronicles to describe Cleopatra, reflecting contemporary views on her reputation.</note> answered that she well knew how to prepare a single course such that she alone would consume a thousand times the cost of his entire meal in one supper. Antony thought that this could not be possible, yet he wished to see it and be witness to such luxury. And he appointed Lucius Plancus <note>Lucius Munatius Plancus, a Roman senator and ally of Antony.</note> as a judge to decide the matter. 

And when, on the following day, the King's <note>original: "künigs." Antony is often referred to with royal titles in these texts despite being a Roman triumvir.</note> food was not increased beyond the usual custom, Cleopatra laughed and commanded her servants to bring her meal. The servants brought nothing but a vessel of extremely sour vinegar, as they had been instructed by her beforehand. 

Immediately, she took a pearl <term>berlin</term> <note>The text uses "berlin," an Early New High German variant of "perlin" (pearl).</note> which she wore in her ear as an ornament according to her custom—the cost of which was unspeakable—and placed it in the vinegar. And as soon as it dissolved therein, she drank it off. And immediately she wished to have the other pearl, equally costly, from her other ear consumed in the same way; however, Lucius Plancus stepped in and gave the judgment that Antony was defeated. 

Thus the other pearl was saved, and the Queen won. That same pearl was afterwards divided in two, taken to Rome, and placed in the highest temple, the Pantheon.

And there [the remaining pearl] was hung in the ears of the goddess **Venus** as a lasting testament to the half-consumed banquet of **Cleopatra**. <note>According to legend, Cleopatra dissolved one of two massive pearls in vinegar to win a bet. The second pearl was saved and later cut in two to adorn a statue of Venus in the Pantheon in Rome.</note> But as insatiable greed grew even more from day to day in that unchaste woman, and because she sought to bring all things under her control after this costly banquet, she begged the drunken **Antony** that he should make the Roman Empire subject to her—as if it were in his power to simply give the Roman Empire away. 

He promised to give it to her, giving little forethought to his own power or that of the Romans, or what he would be giving away, for his reason was clouded by wine. O good God! What great audacity to ask, and the folly of the giver was likewise of no small, unspeakable "generosity." <note>The author uses 'miltigkeit' (generosity/clemency) ironically here to describe Antony's reckless promise of a whole empire to his lover.</note> 

Such a prize—which over so long a time, with such great toil and labor, with so much bloodshed, with the deaths of so many high-born men, with the subjection of so many peoples and lands, with so many great events and hard battles, had barely been brought together—was promised to such a woman at her simple request, as carelessly and thoughtlessly as if it were a shepherd's hut. Yet it remained unfulfilled, for this power was not his alone to give. 

For with the separation <note>Referring to Antony's legal divorce from Octavia to be with Cleopatra.</note> that **Antony** had forced upon **Octavia**, the sister of **Octavian**, discord was sown and the beginning of a deadly war. But **Antony** and **Cleopatra** arrived with many well-equipped ships, whose sails were made of purple and whose hulls shimmered with gold, and they sailed into **Epirus**. There they landed and fought on land against **Octavian’s** army. And **Antony’s** army was so divided that they sought help through...

### Concerning Antonia, the daughter of Antony. Chapter 84

**A**ntonia the Younger—so named because she had an older sister who was also called by that name—was an elegant example for all future women who wish to maintain their state of widowhood in pure integrity. She was the daughter of the aforementioned Mark Antony, the Triumvir <note>original: "drey manes", literally "three-man," referring to the Second Triumvirate of Rome</note>, yet born of Octavia, the sister of Octavian. And she was given as a wife to <insert>Drusus</insert>...

### Concerning Agrippina, the wife of Germanicus. Chapter 85.

[Woodcut illustration: On the left stands the Emperor Tiberius, identified by a label, wearing a crown and holding a scepter. In the center, guards restrain a woman labeled Agrippina.]

**A**grippina was a daughter of Marcus Agrippa, born of Julia, the daughter of the Emperor Octavian <note>Better known as Augustus, the first Roman Emperor.</note>. He devised a dishonorable birth for his mother and claimed she was born of Julia, but not by Agrippa, but by Octavian <note>Caligula spread a rumor that his mother was the result of incest between Augustus and his daughter Julia, preferring a "divine" but scandalous lineage over a common one.</note>.

Through this death, while Agrippina earned great praise, honor, and glory among her own people, she left behind even more dishonor and words of shame for Tiberius.

### ¶ Regarding Paulina the Roman. Chapter 86.

**P**aulina was a Roman woman. Through her simple and <note>The original term *spötlichen* suggests her devotion was of a kind that left her open to being mocked or led to a ridiculous outcome.</note> gullible devotion, she established an eternal memory for her name. This occurred during the times when the Emperor Tiberius reigned in Rome. Just as this same Paulina surpassed other young women in the beauty of her body and her fine manners, so too—when she was given as a wife to Saturnius—did she become a particular ornament of feminine honor. 

She concerned herself with nothing more than how she might please her husband in all things. To achieve this, she sought the help of the <term>idol Anubis</term> <note>Anubis was an ancient Egyptian god, usually depicted with the head of a jackal. In Roman times, Egyptian cults were popular but often viewed with suspicion by traditionalists.</note>, whom she praised and honored every day, just as all the Egyptians did. She did this because she believed he had the power to create goodwill and harmony between married couples. 

However, as is common everywhere, the most beautiful women are most loved by young men—especially those women who are notably diligent in their adornment, good conduct, discipline, and honor, as Paulina then was. Because of this, a youth named Mundus was so moved by her beauty and became so passionate that he could not contain himself. He showed his desire first with his eyes, then with gestures; now with flattering words, then with gifts; then with pleas and many kinds of offers, to see if he might in any way obtain that which he so fervently desired. 

But all of this was in vain, for that most <term>chaste</term> <note>The original *künest* literally means "boldest" or "bravest," but in this context, it refers to her strength of character and "bold" refusal to break her marriage vows.</note> woman, who had set her heart entirely upon the love of her husband, blew all other flattering words into the air. When Mundus realized that the lady's constancy could be weakened neither by wearying words nor by gifts, he began to turn his mind toward evil cunning and trickery. And when he noticed how this woman went every day into the temple to keep company with the <gloss>false god</gloss> Anubis through her constant offerings, there occurred to him the...

The simple man <note>original: *grob*; here meaning naive or unsophisticated rather than rude</note> believed everything his wife said and rejoiced with her that she was to give birth to a god. And without doubt, they both would have eagerly awaited the time of such a birth, if Mundus could have kept his <term>wicked trick: *böſliſt*, a clever but malicious deception</term> hidden.

But burning love would not allow him to keep it secret. And as he considered how friendly, how eager, and how lovely she had behaved toward him, he thought that if she learned it was he to whom she had acted so, she would willingly give herself to other nights, since the first shame was now gone; thus he might often come to her embrace according to his will.

And when she went once into the temple, Mundus met her and spoke secretly to her: "Paulina, you are blessed that you have received from me the god Anubis." But from these words, something very different sprang forth than he had hoped; for immediately Paulina was terrified. This was to such an extent that while he held the title alone, she possessed the actual order and rule. Thus, all Roman power was manifested in a woman. Yet that great splendor was stained with a shameful mark. For she raged for a long time with killings and by condemning citizens into exile.

For she became troublesome and contrary to her son Nero in many things, because of which she fell into such great hatred of his that he completely stripped her of all honors and imperial majesty. <note>Agrippina the Younger had been instrumental in Nero's rise to power, but their relationship soured as she attempted to maintain control over his administration.</note> And she became resentful and often said, out of womanly agitation, that she had raised up her son; with her cunning she could also well ensure that he would be brought low. From this Nero became frightened, for he knew her to be malicious. Furthermore, she held much favor for the sake of her father Germanicus <note>Germanicus was a beloved Roman general and hero; his popularity among the people and the military made Nero hesitant to move against Agrippina publicly.</note>, so that he did not want to kill her openly. 

But three times he had poison served to her, which however brought her no harm; because through her foresight she tamed the malice of the poison with corresponding medicines <note>Agrippina was rumored to have built up an immunity to various poisons by taking daily mithridates (small, non-lethal doses of antidotes).</note> so that she was not harmed. Finally, when Nero noticed that she was not to be caught in any snares which he had manifoldly laid for her, he thought it necessary to seek a craftier way. And he summoned Anicetus, the <term>chief commander over all shipping</term> <note>original: "öbristen patron über alle schiffung"; this refers to the commander of the imperial fleet at Misenum.</note>, who was very knowledgeable of the king's secrets, for he had raised him from childhood. The same man told him how a "frail" ship could be made that would be deadly to Agrippina and yet look unsuspicious. 

This advice pleased Nero. And one time, when she had come from the city of <term>Antium</term> <note>original: "ancio"; the modern Anzio, an ancient coastal resort where Nero was born.</note>, he feigned every friendship toward her and received her well and beautifully, embracing her with his arms in every way, as if he had let go of all envy and ill-will toward her. And he escorted her to her house. Afterward, when the ship was prepared for her death, she sailed with several of her servants one evening out toward a city located not far away. And as they came into the sea, the lookouts gave the signal for the roof of the ship to fall; in it much lead was hidden, which fell and struck dead several of her servants, but Agrippina—

# The 123rd Leaf

**E**pitharis was distinguished neither by noble birth nor by any other outward brilliance; for she was not from Rome, but was a foreigner, born of a slave who was later <term>freed</term><note>The text uses "gefreyet," referring to manumission, the process by which a slave was granted legal freedom.</note>. She also had no desire for virtuous and refined works, which is indeed to be reproached. But at the end of her life, she demonstrated that she possessed a noble, strong, masculine spirit <note>In the 15th century, bravery and political resolve were often described as "masculine" traits, even when displayed by women.</note>.

For in the times when the tyranny of <term>Nero</term><note>Nero (reigned 54–68 AD) was infamous for his cruelty and the persecution of his political rivals.</note> against the Romans and the whole land began to grow, it came to pass that many from the Senate conspired together against him. And while they were considering what ways the matter might be accomplished, I know not by whom all things were made known to Epitharis, including all the names of the conspirators.

When, however, in her opinion, the matter was being delayed too long, she traveled into <term>Campania</term><note>A region in southern Italy.</note>, as if she were annoyed that the plot against Nero was not being carried out. And while she was at <term>Puteoli</term><note>original: "puteol"; a major Roman port city near Naples.</note>, so that the time would not pass in vain, she once summoned Volusius Proculus, who was the supreme captain of the entire imperial fleet and one of those who had helped kill <term>Agrippina</term><note>Nero’s mother, whom he had assassinated in 59 AD.</note>, the emperor's mother.

And she intended to be very helpful to the conspirators if she could persuade him to join their company. And she began to tell him at great length of the evils and misdeeds, as well as the murderous works and wicked customs of Nero, and also his ingratitude—that he had never gifted him nor rewarded him with any advancement according to his merit for the great deed committed against Agrippina. With this, she revealed and disclosed to him the plot of the co-conspirators and exerted all her strength to bring him into that same company.

But it turned out quite differently than her plan intended; for when Volusius...

The 124th leaf

[He] <note>This likely refers to Volusius Proculus, an admiral whom the conspirator Epicharis had attempted to recruit, but who instead betrayed her to Nero.</note> wanted to try whether he might earn the Emperor’s grace through his services; and as soon as he came to him, he revealed everything that Epicharis had told him, although he could not sufficiently explain the details to him, for the woman was so clever that she would not name any of the conspirators to him. She was captured and questioned as to who had sworn to bring about the death of the Emperor; but she could not be moved to point any of them out. At last, while she lay imprisoned, it happened that the matters were revealed by the conspirators <note>original: "sehern"</note> themselves. And now that the Emperor knew their names, he ordered the woman to be led to torture once again, to see if she would agree to testify against the others. But however severely she was tormented, even beyond the usual torture, they could never shake her steadfast spirit so that she would name any of the conspirators; they had to remain locked secretly in her heart. She was then locked away to be interrogated again on the following day. And when she had been so tortured that she could no longer walk on her feet and her body had become weak, she feared the coming torture—that her spirit might follow the weakness of her body and that she would speak. She untied the bindings <note>likely her girdle or breast-band</note> with which she was girded, and made of them a noose, and hanged herself from the chair on which she sat imprisoned, so that she might not reveal her secrets. This stands against the common proverb with which we are taught: that no secret can be hidden behind a woman; for what they do not know, that is kept silent by them. <note>The author is using irony here to flip the misogynistic trope that women are incapable of keeping secrets.</note> Thus, the powerful Emperor could not prevail against the woman. And although the constancy of this woman is to be valued greatly and highly, she is also rightly exalted and praised above the spirit of all men, when one considers that this conspiracy against Nero...

# The 125th Page

...yet Nero rejoiced that he had found a reason to put this virtuous and most venerable man to death. For he held an old hatred toward him on account of his wisdom, teachings, discipline, honor, and virtue—things to which Nero, by his inborn wickedness, was an enemy. Therefore, he sent word to Seneca, ordering that he choose for himself in what manner he wished to die. 

As soon as Pompeia <note>Pompeia Paulina, the wife of the philosopher Seneca</note> perceived that he was preparing to fulfill the Emperor’s command, she set aside all flattering and comforting words regarding the hope for his life. Through the compulsion of burning love for her husband, she steeled her spirit to drive out great sorrow and dismay by a shared death, wishing to die willingly with her husband, just as they had lived together in such harmony. And in the same manner as her husband, she—undaunted and with a stout heart—allowed her veins to be opened in lukewarm water, letting the blood run out until death. 

But when Nero heard of this, he immediately commanded that she should be kept alive; for he bore no particular ill-will toward her, and also because he wished that such an example of his tyranny <term>tyranny: the original German "wütterey" implies a savage, unrestrained rage or cruelty</term> should be less widely known. Yet the servants could not arrive quickly enough to restore her blood. Thus she faded away, so that her paleness bore eternal witness that she had shed much blood and the spirit of life along with her husband. She mourned him thereafter, remaining for a few years in a state of pure widowhood with honorable memory of her husband, and so closed her days. 

O God, what is more noble or greater than the sweet love of holy matrimony? This woman followed the ancient, holy custom of married women, who shunned not only third, fourth, or perhaps sixth marriages, but even a second one. For after the death of their first husband, they were quick to embrace holy chastity and worthy, honorable, eternal memory. Not as many... <note>The text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page.</note>

### Chapter 90: Concerning Poppaea Sabina

[Woodcut illustration depicting a man kicking a pregnant woman, with another couple standing to the right. **Poppaea Sabina** was one of the most illustrious Roman women, named thus after her grandfather on her mother’s side, Sabinus Poppaeus <note>original: *sabino popeo*. Poppaea took the name of her prestigious grandfather, a consul, rather than her father, who was implicated in a conspiracy.</note>. She was also not lacking in any womanly gifts, had she only possessed a nobler spirit; for she was of such extraordinary beauty as to surpass all other women. She possessed soft words, a sweet voice, flattering speech, and a lovely demeanor. She was of exceedingly sharp and nimble intellect <note>original: *behenden ſinnen*</note>, had she only used it well. It was her constant habit to display a modest behavior in public, while in secret practicing unchastity <note>original: *vnkewſcheyt*</note>—as, alas, many do who nonetheless wish to be held in high honor. And although she rarely went out, she nevertheless employed her seductive cunning; for when she noticed that many men, and especially those of the highest rank...

### The 127th leaf

...moreover, there was no resistance. She began to lay many snares and nets <note>The text uses "strick" and "netz," metaphors for Poppaea's seductive traps</note> in which she might catch Nero, so that he would take her as his wife. He received her with exceedingly great joy and named her **Augusta Poppaea** <note>original: "augustam pompeam"</note> after her mother. 

Then Poppaea first began to speak boldly with him: "I recall that I have been with you for two nights, during which I was waiting for marriage. I am not of low birth, I am fertile, and I am also gifted with beauty; I believe I am well worthy of imperial marriage." By this, she moved the Emperor so that he began to eagerly seek a way to properly wed her. 

First, he sent away his wife **Octavia**—who was the daughter of Emperor Claudius—though she was innocent, to an island. In the twentieth year of her age, he had her put to death at the incitement of Poppaea. After this, he took Poppaea as his wife. 

But she did not long enjoy this high status, which she had nevertheless pursued with great labor and many evil schemes. For shortly thereafter, while she was pregnant again, it happened that Nero, moved by anger, kicked her in the stomach, from which she met her final day <note>This occurred in 65 AD; historians debate if it was an accident or murder</note>. 

Then Nero did not want her to be burned, as was the custom in Rome, but according to the custom of other kings, he had her carried to the grave with a great procession of the people, sumptuously embalmed, and there interred according to imperial dignity. Then Nero praised her with a long speech, and most of all for her beauty and her enticing practice of unchastity <note>original: "vnkeüsch"</note>, at which she was very skilled. He valued this as a great brilliance of virtue, even though it is a poisonous injury to a man's soul; she possessed no great goodness within her.

# The 137th Leaf

## ¶ Concerning Triaria, the wife of Lucius Vitellius. Chapter 91.

**T**riaria was the wife of the brother of the Roman prince Aulus Vitellius. She is widely celebrated solely for her exceptional love and manly fighting for and on behalf of her husband. For at the time when Vitellius the Emperor and Vespasian strove against each other for the rule of the Empire, and a great multitude from both parties had gathered in the city of Terracina <note>original: "taracinam"</note> and were prepared with weapons of war, Triaria armed herself so boldly, so chivalrously, so manfully, and so defiantly to protect her husband and make him victorious, that much blood was shed by her hand above others. Therefore, her memory is highly praised and established for eternity; and since nothing more is written of her other deeds, I have also shortened the account of this unwomanly fighting.

## ¶ Concerning Proba, the wife of Alypius or Adelphus. Chapter 92.

**P**roba was "Proba" by name and in her works, for "proba" means "pious" or "virtuous" in German <note>The author is highlighting a Latin pun: the name Proba is derived from the Latin "probus," meaning virtuous or upright</note>; thus the lady was virtuous in all her deeds. And above others, she is most worthy of eternal memory because of her great skill. From whom she may have learned, I do not know; however, she was a great mistress of the seven liberal arts <term>The Seven Liberal Arts: the medieval curriculum of grammar, logic, rhetoric, arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy</term>, as is well noted from her poems. And above all, she was so thoroughly and completely learned in the books of Virgil that they could never leave her memory, as her book called *Centona* <note>original: "centona"; likely referring to the *Cento Vergilianus*, a poem composed of lines recycled from Virgil's works</note> clearly proves. In this book, she describes the Old and New Testaments <note>original: "ee," meaning law or covenant, here referring to the Bible</note> so clearly, so lovably, so sweetly, and so gracefully, that it would be a marvel for a woman even to conceive of such a thing, let alone to accomplish it. And so it recounts from the creation of the world and the first man, up to the holy...

<summary>This page provides biographies of two Roman women: Triaria, noted for her martial bravery during a civil war, and Proba, a Christian poet praised for her mastery of Virgil and the liberal arts.</summary>
<keywords>Triaria, Aulus Vitellius, Terracina, women warriors, Faltonia Betitia Proba, Virgil, Cento, Seven Liberal Arts, Bible in poetry</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of a remarkable woman of letters, likely Faltonia Betitia Proba, known for her "Cento," a poem constructed entirely from the works of Virgil to tell biblical stories.</meta>

->Page 128<-
<margin>132, [136]</margin>

**Pentecost, and the sending of the Holy Spirit to his disciples.** And [this was composed] solely from the verses of the master Virgil <note>The Roman poet Publius Vergilius Maro (70–19 BCE)</note>, sometimes taking them whole, then half, now from the beginning, then the middle, or perhaps taken from the end, according to how they served her purpose. And she did this so cleverly that anyone ignorant of such things might easily mistake her for a prophet or an evangelist—or even mistake Virgil himself, from whose works she gathered this book, for one.

She also investigated other books of the Holy Scripture as thoroughly as many of the most highly learned doctors of the church. Because of this, it is right to mourn that manhood <note>The author is expressing a common medieval sentiment that men should be ashamed to be surpassed in learning by a woman.</note> is so indifferent toward learning that it should be overcome by a woman. The more I read this little book of hers, called a *Cento* <term>Cento: a literary work made up of quotations from other authors; the word literally means "patchwork quilt."</term>, the more I find that she and her book are worthy of eternal memory.

I cannot help but think that, since she lived at a time when she was so productive, she must have written many other books as well, which perhaps—to our misfortune—were despised and have perished without protection. Yet some claim she also used the same skills to create a *Homerocento* <note>original: "homericentonam"</note> from the books of Homer, just as she made the *Cento* from Virgil. If this is true, then her praise should rightly be expanded, for it would be certain proof that she was as learned in the Greek arts as she was in the Latin.

It is a wonderful thing to say that a female figure <note>original: "frawen bild"</note> should possess such high intellect that she could create such original books on the life of Christ from all the Christian texts, so orderly, so elegant, and without any error in the meter of the verses, in both syllables and words. It is even greater and more to be valued that she took these from pagan poems. O, how diligently she cast aside all the sluggishness of her spirit so that she might search out the eternal light! O, that the women of our time, who are servants to all evil pleasures, would only look upon this example!

## Chapter 93: Concerning Faustina, wife of Marcus Antoninus the Good

[A woodcut illustration depicts a woman in a bed surrounded by several figures. Although the text describes ancient Rome, the figures are dressed in the clothing of the 15th century, including a figure resembling a king and another a bishop <note>It was common for illustrators in the 1400s to depict historical figures in contemporary medieval clothing to make them more relatable to the readers of the time.</note>. Labels within the woodcut identify the figures as **Faustina**, **Faustina**, and **Antoninus**.]

**Faustina Augusta** <note>A title of honor granted to the wives or female relatives of Roman Emperors.</note>, who was afterwards deified <note>original: "geheyliget" - literally "made holy," referring to the Roman practice of Consecratio, where an emperor or empress was officially declared a god after death.</note> by the Romans, was highly honored and respected during her lifetime and after her death. This was due more to the goodness of her husband than to her own personal merit. She was the daughter of the Emperor Antoninus the Pious <note>original: "Anthonini des gütigen" - Antoninus Pius, who reigned from 138–161 AD.</note> and his wife Faustina, and she was given as a wife to Marcus Antoninus the Good <note>Marcus Aurelius.</note>, whom the Emperor had adopted as a son. As soon as her father died, they both reigned together, and by the common decree of the **Senate**—that is, the highest authority <term>the Senate: the governing council of ancient Rome</term> of the Romans—she was named **Augusta**.

...named [Augusta] <note>Empress Faustina the Younger was given the title Augusta in 147 AD</note>. However, before her, this title had been given to no one by the senate. Although other empresses had also held the name **Augusta** from their husbands, it was not by recognition of the supreme power. And "Augusta" is as much as to say an "increaser" <note>original: "merezin"; a feminine form of the Latin *auctrix*, meaning one who increases or nourishes</note>. Furthermore, she was of such boundless beauty that it was estimated by everyone to be a kind of divine adornment mixed with the mortal; and that the same beauty would not decay or pass away through age or death. It happened that her likeness, in the times of her blooming youth, was struck upon **gold**, **silver**, and **copper** coins, which can still be seen to this very day, from which one may well observe her beauty. 

But as far as her beauty was spread throughout the whole world, so much was she stained, in shameful measure, by unchastity. For it is held of her that she was not content with one lover in addition to her husband, but that she came to many a man's unchaste embrace. Among those who were slandered by name for adultery with her were **Serulus**, **Orphicus**, and **Modemcius**. But preferred above the others was **Tuculus**, whom the Emperor even once caught with her during dinner. 

To these was also counted **Marcus Verus**, although he was her son-in-law <note>Marcus Verus here likely refers to Lucius Verus, who was married to Faustina’s daughter, Lucilla</note>. And what is worse than all that, it is said of her that she became so inflamed with love for a knight <note>In the original Roman accounts, this "knight" is typically identified as a gladiator</note> that she fell into a mortal illness from excessive desire for him. And so that she might recover her health, she revealed to the Emperor, her husband, the intensity of her love for the knight. He, on the advice of a physician, had her anointed with the warm blood of that same knight, drawn from his heart, to quench the violent heat of his wife; yet [it] says...

...that some said she was pregnant at that time and gave birth to a son named Commodus Antoninus, whom everyone judged to be the son of that same knight <note>Historical rumors suggested that the Emperor's son Commodus was actually fathered by a gladiator, whom the text refers to here as a "ritter" or knight.</note> and not the Emperor’s. When Faustina’s shameful conduct became so widely known, his friends advised Antoninus that he should put her to death, or if he wished to act more humanely, he should divorce her. 

But Antoninus was kind-hearted; and although he bore the adultery of his wife with a heavy heart, he nonetheless rejected the advice of his friends, solely so that no greater scandal might arise from it. He preferred to endure this and gave no other answer to his friends who had advised him to separate from her than to say that one would have to return the "dowry" to the divorced woman. With these words, he gave them to understand that he held the Empire through Faustina <note>Marcus Aurelius gained his right to the throne largely through his marriage to Faustina, the daughter of the previous Emperor, Antoninus Pius. To "return the dowry" meant to give up the Empire itself.</note>. For this reason, many such things must often be endured in the hope of improvement, for the honorable are often seen in heavy falls, yet they emerge from the darkness of the clouds back into the light.

<margin>†</margin> When Antoninus had traveled out to the Eastern kings to manage many affairs for the common good, it happened that Faustina reached her final day through a great illness at the foot of the mountain called Taurus. Through the prayers of Antoninus, she was consecrated by the Romans and henceforth always called the **divine Faustina** <note>original: "diua Faustina"</note>, which means the "divine" or "holy" Faustina—an honor which had never been granted to any woman by the Romans before her. Then Antoninus had her named **Mother of the Camps** <note>original: "matrem castrorum"</note>, which means a mother of the fortresses or castles, and he built her a temple at the place where she had passed away. He had beautiful statues carved in her likeness to honor her and established a society of maidens called the **Faustinians** <note>original: "faustinianas"</note>; and so, for a time, she was regarded as a saint.

### ¶ Chapter 94: Concerning Semiamira

->**Heliogabalus** [and] **Semiamira**<-
<note>This label identifies the figures in the accompanying woodcut illustration, which shows the Empress and her son among their court.</note>

**S**emiamira, born in Messana in Greece <note>While the text says "Greece," the historical Julia Soaemias (Semiamira) was actually from Emesa in Syria.</note>, daughter of the Empress Julia—who was once the wife of Septimius Severus <note>original Latin: "Severi pertinacis"</note>—was for a long time a disreputable woman. But afterward, she was held in high esteem because of the brilliance and supreme power of her son over the Romans. She was the mother of **Varius Heliogabalus**, who was for a time a priest of the god Phoebus <note>The god Phoebus (Apollo) represents the sun; Heliogabalus was famously the high priest of the Syrian sun god Elagabal.</note>. He was later elected Emperor. She claimed she had conceived him by the Emperor Antoninus Caracalla, with whom she had previously had an affair. 

In truth, she made her body common to many men; therefore, in his childhood, he was called "Varius Heliogabalus" by other children because he was born of manifold fathers with whom she practiced constant unchastity, and not from any certain one. Indeed, <term>Varius</term> <note>The author provides a folk etymology here, suggesting the name "Varius" refers to the "various" men his mother slept with.</note> is as much as to say "manifold." Because he was said to be the son of Emperor Antoninus Caracalla <note>The text refers to Caracalla as "Caratella," the emperor known for the Antonine Constitution</note>, he was well-regarded by the military. And this came to pass through the great wealth of his grandmother, named Varia Maesa <note>original: "Varia messana"</note>, who stood behind Empress Julia <note>Julia Domna</note>, and through many types of cunning. Thus the military deserted Emperor Macrinus and elevated Elagabalus. It was also an easy thing to do, for at that time, the House of the Antonines <note>original: "anthoninorum"</note> was so great and well-loved in Rome that the common people desired nothing else than that one of their lineage should hold the government of the empire. And it soon happened that he became emperor and was named Antoninus, near Antioch. 

But when Emperor Macrinus, in the city of Antioch, heard of this, he was greatly amazed at the cunning of the woman; and he sent out Julianus with a large force to fight against him. This Julianus was slain, and all his people deserted Macrinus for the new emperor Elagabalus. Macrinus marched against him and was defeated, and while in flight, he was stabbed to death along with his son Diadumenianus. Thus was Caracalla avenged, whom Macrinus had previously murdered. 

Following such a victory, Elagabalus came to Rome and was eagerly and highly received by the entire Senate with great praise. Through the sudden elevation of Elagabalus, his mother Julia Soaemias <note>original: "semiamira"</note> was raised almost to the heavens from her shameful, common life as a woman, and was named **Augusta** <note>A title of honor for the wives and mothers of emperors</note> like other empresses. She was established as a powerful lady in the Roman imperial hall, where she was made illustrious for this reason alone, and for no other good deed. ### The 131st Page

...[he] allowed nothing to be ordered or arranged except by her command. And on the day when he had come to Rome and the Senate was assembled, he requested that his mother come into the Senate. She followed this request and was seated with such honorable adornment as the others among the high counselors to help govern the Roman Empire and to pass judgment, as was the custom of the Senate. This was something that, before her, had never been granted to any woman among the Romans.

O shameful Carnival mockery! <note>The author uses "faſnacht ſchimpff" to describe the situation as a ridiculous, topsy-turvy farce, like a Carnival play.</note> That among the wisest, most serious, and most prominent men, a woman—one who has only just emerged from a public brothel <note>original: "frawenhauß" – a common term for a brothel in the 15th/16th century; the author is using derogatory language to emphasize her perceived unfitness for office.</note>—should be seen giving advice and passing judgment. O ancient Roman freedom! O former holiness! O praiseworthy severity <note>original: "vnwürdſcheÿt" – here likely meaning the strictness or gravity with which the ancestors rejected anything unworthy.</note> of our ancestors, who drove all frivolity and lives stained with shameful vices out of their assembly of honorable, wise men.

Now you see a dishonorable woman shamefully defiling the place of the noblest Romans: the Curii, the Fabricii, the Scipios, and the Catos <note>The author lists the genitive forms of famous Roman family names (Curionum, Fabriciorum, Scipionum, Cathonum) who represented traditional Roman virtue.</note>. But why should I complain about a woman? For we see now that the well-known enemies of the common good—and sometimes the youngest, those most foolish compared to others, the strangest and most unknown—are found in many positions of power under the Roman emperors, princes, lords, and cities as the highest and best-maintained officials.

But furthermore, what else happened? Heliogabalus <note>Roman Emperor r. 218–222 AD, notorious in medieval and Renaissance historiography for his eccentric and "oriental" behaviors.</note> never went into the council without his "holy" mother going with him. And blind fortune <note>original: "blind ſäligkeÿt" – referring to the fickle nature of luck that favors the undeserving.</note> so gifted her that she was held in such high regard and honored by the common rabble that even the wise Sibyls <note>Ancient prophetesses famous for their wisdom.</note> were valued less than she was by the flighty people. Although these things are vexing to hear, there follows something even more ridiculous: this woman was held in such high esteem by her unwise son that he [placed] her in the position of the noblest...

### ¶ Chapter 95: Of Zenobia, Queen of the Palmyrenes.

**Z**enobia, Queen of the Palmyrenes <note>original: "Palmirenorum"</note>, was of such high virtue that she far surpassed other pagan women. Her origin was the royal lineage of Egypt, who were all called Ptolemies <note>Zenobia claimed descent from the Ptolemaic line, including Cleopatra, though modern historians debate this</note>. From childhood, she despised all feminine works. As soon as she had gained some physical strength, she spent her time in the woods, well-equipped with a girt-on quiver and all kinds of archery gear. With these, she hunted harts, roebucks, and all kinds of game; she became so masterful and sure in this that few could be found to equal her.

And in a short time she became so manly <note>In the 15th century, "manly" (*männlich*) was a high compliment for a woman, signifying she possessed the "masculine" virtues of courage and physical strength</note> that she ventured to fell, catch, and kill bears, leopards, wild boars, and even lions. Moreover, she was bold enough to ride, run, climb, and slip through mountains and valleys, stone walls, and all marshes. Without any fear, she slept in the open fields at night, enduring heat, cold, rain, wind, and snow. 

She despised the common company of many people and honored the purity of her body above all other things. Yet she was so hardened in manly works that in wrestling, fencing, and other knightly and courageous deeds, she far surpassed other youths of her age. But when she had reached marriageable years, by the advice of her friends, she was given as a wife to the noblest youth, Odaenathus <note>The text reads "Adamato," a Germanic variation of the name of the ruler of Palmyra</note>, who was likewise practiced in knighthood.

<summary>This page concludes a previous biography with a moralizing reflection on death before introducing Zenobia of Palmyra, highlighting her royal Egyptian lineage and her youthful rejection of traditional gender roles in favor of hunting and martial training.</summary>

<keywords>Zenobia, Palmyra, Egypt, Ptolemy, hunting, gender roles, Odaenathus, virtue, 15th-century biography</keywords>

She was exceptionally beautiful, though brown or dark-skinned—as all those people are due to the excessive heat of the sun—with black eyes and snow-white teeth, and well-adorned. At one time, her husband <term>Odenathus<note>original: "adanatus"</note></term> was captured by <term>Shapur<note>original: "sapore"</note></term>, the King of Persia, and held in harsh servitude. As soon as she heard this—never forgetting the old hardiness she had practiced in her youth—she set aside her feminine finery and went out well-armed with the strength of an army, with the help of her stepson <term>Herodes<note>Herodianus, the eldest son of Odenathus</note></term>, to fight against Shapur. And she did not cease until she had captured him, along with all his wives and children, freed her husband, and made all of <term>Mesopotamia</term> subject to her command. She also won many of the Romans' lands during this time, which she held and ruled so powerfully that the Emperor <term>Gallienus</term> and, after him, <term>Claudius</term> did not easily dare to attempt to take them back from her. Likewise, the Eastern kings of Egypt, Armenia, and Arabia feared her power; everyone feared her might and desired nothing more than to keep what they still possessed. 

Her husband Odenathus was eventually murdered along with his son Herodes by the queen’s cousin; some believe this was done so that her own children would rule the realm after their father’s death, as it would otherwise have fallen to Herodes—I shall leave that matter as it is. Zenobia was such a protector of purity that she was not only guarded against foreign men, but she was never intimate<note>original: "gemein," literally "common," used here to mean sexual intimacy</note> with her husband Odenathus except solely for the hope of offspring. And once he had slept with her one time, she would not be joined with him again until she was certain that she had not already conceived. And when she had conceived, she would not be touched by him again until...

# Leaf 134

### After the usual time of purification after her childbirth
<note>In the Middle Ages and Early Modern period, "purification" referred to the ritual "churching of women," a period of rest and religious ceremony following childbirth based on biblical tradition.</note>

O, the praiseworthy wisdom of this woman, who knew so well that nature instilled desire in us mortal humans for the sole purpose that the human race might remain in existence and not perish; and that whatever occurs beyond that is superfluous, useless, unclean, and shameful. Where are there more such women? Not here. She maintained a magnificent and costly court in the royal fashion, yet she did not have daily servants unless they were old and very withered <note>original: "verschmitten"; likely referring to aged or eunuch attendants to ensure the Queen's reputation for chastity remained beyond reproach</note>, so that frivolous temptation might be better avoided by everyone. She also desired to be worshiped by her people, as was the custom of the queens of Persia. She also managed her household in the manner of the Roman emperors, using golden vessels adorned with many kinds of precious stones after the custom of Cleopatra, as she had heard of it. And although she was a great keeper of the treasury, none was ever found more generous in spending for the sake of honor. 

And although in her youth she was diligently devoted to the hunt, she nevertheless learned Greek, the Egyptian tongue <note>original: "heydnisch" (heathen); likely referring to the Egyptian or Syriac/Aramaic languages spoken in her region</note>, and Latin so well that she could read and understand all ancient histories in every language. She also desired that the common language of her children should be Latin. What more shall I say? She reached the point where she intended to crush the power of the Romans. 

However, after the emperors Gallienus, Aurelian, and Claudius had died <note>The text contains a historical error here; the Emperor Aurelian was actually the one who defeated Zenobia, not someone who died before her downfall</note>, Marcus Manicus <note>Likely a corruption of a name from the writer's source material</note>, who had been elected emperor there, prepared himself against her and sent out Aurelian with the strength of an army. He came to Emesa <note>original: "semessam"; a city in modern-day Syria</note> to besiege her. Zenobia marched out against him undaunted with the help of Zabdas <note>original: "zebonis"; her famous lead general</note>, who stood by her. However, she was turned to flight, and after long warring, she was captured with her sons by Aurelian.

Aurelian <note>Lucius Domitius Aurelianus, Roman Emperor from 270–275 AD, known for reuniting the Roman Empire after it had started to fragment.</note> was no less glorified and honored than if he had captured the most powerful prince and the greatest enemy of the Roman power. There, near the palace of the Divine Hadrian <note>original: "diui adriani"</note>, she ended her life.

### The 134th leaf
...such a fire of **disordered love** <note>original: "vnordenlicher liebe"; a historical term for lust or passion that falls outside the "orderly" bounds of marriage or religious law</note> was ignited in both of them that she set aside maidenly discipline and shame and fled with him out of her father’s house with changed clothes and names; for in a youth’s garment, she kept the name **Johannes**. Thus, she was held by everyone in England to be a student alongside her lover, and there she diligently devoted herself to her lover and to all the learning of the arts. Afterward, when her companion had passed away in death, she recognized her own aptitude for learning and felt the sweetness of the arts; and she wished henceforth to have no more **improper intimacy** <note>original: "vnzimliche gemeinsame"</note> with any other, but rather to remain in constant practice of the arts in man’s clothing and not be recognized as a woman. 

But she practiced herself so diligently day and night in learning that in a short time, she was held in wondrously high esteem above all others in the <term>seven liberal arts</term> <note>The standard medieval curriculum consisting of the Trivium (grammar, logic, rhetoric) and the Quadrivium (arithmetic, geometry, music, astronomy).</note> and Holy Scripture. Then she moved from England to Rome; there she spent several years lecturing in public schools, attracting many of the most honorable disciples ahead of other doctors. Beyond her skill, she always appeared to lead a good, honorable, and holy life and was regarded by everyone as a man, and was so well-regarded that at the time when **Leo, the fifth pope of that name**, had **paid the debt of the flesh** <note>An idiom for dying.</note>, she was unanimously elected Pope by the most worthy assembly of all the cardinals and was called **John the Eighth**. 

She was of such a bold spirit that she did not fear to occupy the **Chair of the Fisherman** <note>A reference to the Papal throne, as St. Peter was a fisherman.</note> and to exercise and distribute all holiness from it, which has never been permitted to any woman according to Christian order. **from above.** [God] had mercy on his people, that such a highly venerable position <note>Referring to the Papacy</note> should be held in such a way, and that the people should be so ruled and deceived in such great error by a woman. He no longer wished to leave such power in her hands. Therefore, through the counsel of the devil—who had previously poured such stubbornness into her—she was fervently incited to unchastity <note>Original: *vnkeüſcheyt*</note>. It was so much that all her skills and arts, which had been sufficient for her to acquire the papacy, were of no help to her in extinguishing the heat of that fire, until one was found who could tame that fervor and "bless" <note>The text uses *beyliget*, a euphemism here for sexual intercourse</note> the occupant of Saint Peter’s chair until the Pope was made pregnant. 

¶ O unworthy sin! O great patience of God! What happened? This woman, who for a long time was able to darken the eyes of men, could not hide her labor with all her arts. For once, after she had held Mass and a public procession <term>church-going: *kirchgang*, a formal religious procession</term> was underway between the Colosseum and the old hall of Pope Clement <note>Likely referring to the Basilica of Saint Clement or the route to the Lateran Palace</note>, she gave birth to a child in front of all the people, without the help of midwives. Therefore, she was cast into the outer darkness by the authorities, and she perished with the child on the island <note>This may refer to a specific secluded location or a tradition of her being buried or exiled</note>. 

¶ And to keep such shameful impurity in memory, during the times of the public processions <term>Rogation days: *kreüczgenge*, literally "cross-walks" or litanies performed while walking</term> of the Pope and all the people, when they come to the place of the birth, they shy away from it and turn from it into other paths and streets to curse the sinful place, and then return to the path from which they came.

# The 135th leaf

<margin>139, [143]</margin>

->**Charlemagne** <note>original: "Karolus magnus"</note>              **Irene**<-

**Irene** was the most noble woman of Athens, shining with a singular grace. Once, the Emperor Constantine <note>Constantine V, reigned 741–775</note> summoned her and betrothed her to his son, named Leo <note>Leo IV, reigned 775–780</note>. And after the death of Constantine, she became Empress and bore a son by her husband Leo, whom she named Constantine <note>Constantine VI, reigned 780–797</note> after her father-in-law. Finally, when Leo had finished his days, she reigned with her young son Constantine for ten years, heartily and well. 

But when he had grown into the age of a young man, he believed the empire should belong to him alone, and he cast his mother out from his company for eight years. At last, as this high-spirited woman was eager to rule, and having come into conflict with her son, she devised through <term>womanly cunning</term> <note>The original German "weiplich list" is a common historical trope used to describe the political strategies of powerful women.</note> a way to return to the empire. She planned how she might capture him and keep him locked in prison, deposed from power. All this came to pass, and she came to occupy the throne alone—the same throne from which, shortly before, the whole world had received its laws—and she reigned.

# The 136th leaf

Afterward, Constantine fell into a fatal illness and ended his days. But after she <note>Empress Irene</note> had reigned for five years, Nikephoros set himself against her and besieged her palace, the Eleutherios <note>The Eleutherios was a palace in Constantinople built by Irene</note>. Thus, for the first time, imperial power came from the hands of the Romans, and Irene was betrothed to Charles. Yet in the end, she was forced by Aetios <note>original: "enticio", a powerful eunuch and advisor</note> and Nikephoros into a nunnery.

### ¶ Of Engeldruda the Virgin, Chapter 98.

**E**ngeldruda, of the most noble lineage of the Lords of Ravenna, who formerly came from Florence, is also not without reason numbered among the virtuous women because of the greatness of her spirit, through which she manfully <note>In this context, "manfully" implies displaying courage or strength typically associated with men in the medieval view</note> and firmly protected her purity before the Roman Prince. <meta>This page appears to be a translation of Giovanni Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (Concerning Famous Women), specifically the story of Gualdrada (here called Engeldruda), a 12th-century Florentine noblewoman known for her modesty. # Page 136

...where once a temple had been built to the idol Mars <note>original: "abgot marci"; the text refers to the ancient Roman belief that the Baptistery of Florence was originally a temple dedicated to the god Mars.</note>. Because the Emperor wished to make that same festival more grand and dignified by his presence, he entered it accompanied by a vast multitude of his princes and lords. 

Once he was seated in the place of highest honor according to his rank, he observed the decorations of the temple, the gathering of the citizens, and especially the women seated around him. However, he fixed his eyes upon Engeldruda <note>Historically known as Gualdrada Berti.</note> longer than any of the others. 

When he had spent some time observing her beauty—alongside her simple and modest clothing, honorable bearing, and maidenly gravity <term>gravity: the original "schwärmütikeyt" implies a serious, modest, and steadfast temperament rather than modern sadness</term>—he began to praise her with great admiration. 

He then questioned one of the citizens of the city named Bilicio—an honorable man of knightly rank who had been assigned to attend the Emperor on behalf of the city. The Emperor asked who the maiden sitting across from him was, remarking that in his opinion, Bilicio likely knew her well, and that she was the most beautiful of all the women he had ever seen.

Bilicio answered him with a smile and a boastful, clever tone: "Most illustrious Prince, regardless of who she may be, she is of such a character that she would grant you a friendly kiss if I were to command it."

As soon as the maiden heard these words, she became indignant. She was deeply distressed <note>original: "schwärmütigklich"; she took the matter heavily to heart.</note> that her father would represent her constancy and maidenly modesty in such a light. 

She could no longer endure it; before the Prince could respond to her father's words, she stood up. Her face was flushed with the deep red of a rose. She lifted her eyes slightly toward her father and then lowered them toward the ground, speaking in a full yet soft voice: 

"Stop, my father, do not speak so! Unless violence is done to me, I swear by God that no man—save for the one to whom you holily betroth me—shall ever receive from me that which you have so boldly promised."

It highlights the theme of female honor and the maiden's defense of her own bodily autonomy against her father's social posturing.</summary>

**C**onstance appeared as an empress. But since this same dignity has been granted to so many women that many people might look down on it somewhat unless other notable deeds are also present, I say that she has been placed in lasting memory because of one single birth. <note>Constance of Sicily (1154–1198) was the heiress of the Norman kings of Sicily and wife of Holy Roman Emperor Henry VI. Her "single birth" refers to her son, Frederick II, born when she was 40.</note> She was the daughter of William, formerly the best king in Sicily. <note>Historical records usually identify her as the posthumous daughter of Roger II of Sicily, though some chronicles confuse the lineage with the Williams who followed him.</note> At her birth, it happened by chance that Abbot Joachim of Calabria <note>original: "abbt Joachim von Calabria"; referring to Joachim of Fiore, a famous mystic and theologian known for his prophetic visions</note> had arrived, a blessed servant of God. This happened, not without great wonder of all those who heard it: that the old woman, in the fifty-fifth year of her age, brought forth a child. And since this was for some time unbelievable to everyone, and was estimated by some to be a fraud <note>original: "lisakeyt" (Listigkeit), meaning a trick or deception</note>, it was prudently considered how the suspicion might be removed from the people. And as the time of the birth approached, it was ordained by the command of the Emperor that all the most noble women of the Kingdom of Sicily should be summoned, who wished to be present at this strange birth. Thus, many of the women were gathered there, even from afar, before whom the old Empress was delivered of the child, named Frederick, who afterwards grew into a wondrous man, and was seen to be not only a destructive rogue <note>The author likely views Frederick II as a "Stupor Mundi" who clashed with the Church, hence "verderblicher schelm" (destructive scoundrel)</note> of the Kingdom of Sicily, but of Italy and Calabria, according to the manifestation of the prophecies of the abbot <note>Likely referring to the prophecies of Joachim of Fiore</note>. Who is he now who would not deem the pregnancy and the birth of Constance <note>Constance of Sicily (1154–1198), mother of Frederick II; while history suggests she was 40, this text claims 55 to emphasize the miracle</note> to be miraculous? For, besides this, such has never been seen in our times. Also, since Aeneas of Troy came into Italy, the like has never been heard, except for Elizabeth, the wife of Zechariah, from whom by special divine action John was born, the equal of whom—or even greater—will never again come among all the children of women.

¶ What the marks signify and how one should 
read according to them. The 100th chapter.

**F**or a better understanding of this little book and others that I have translated from Latin into German, it is to be noted that various distinctions of marks <note>The author is referring to punctuation marks, which were not yet standardized in early printing</note> are placed by various people. Now it is to be known that three kinds of marks are commonly placed in all speech, giving different understandings; they are such as a virgule <term>virgule: a diagonal slash (/) used in early typography as a comma or pause</term>, a little...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of grammar or orthography, specifically focusing on the system of punctuation marks and their functions in early modern printing.</meta>

[Top right corner]
143
[147]

# The 139th Leaf

...hanging such a little point or dot with a stroke drawn beside it like this / and a little point or dot like this . The first little stroke is called a <term>virgule</term> <note>original: *virgula*, a "little rod." This was the precursor to the modern comma.</note> like this /; it signifies that certain words are placed together correctly and in order, but they do not yet conclude a complete or understandable sense. The other little point is called a <term>comma</term> <note>original: *coma*. In this period, a "comma" often functioned more like our modern semicolon or colon.</note> like this ·; it signifies that an understandable sense is concluded, but more hangs upon it, which increases the meaning and provides something further to be understood, as is indicated in this speech. The third little point is called a <term>period</term>, or <term>finisher</term> <note>original: *finitivus*</note>, or <term>lowest</term> <note>original: *infimus*</note> like this . and it signifies that the sense of the speech is finished and complete, and nothing more hangs upon it for greater understanding. 

But there are still three other types of points which are commonly maintained by all the ancients. These are **two little moons** <note>original: *mönlin*, referring to crescent-shaped marks.</note> facing one another like this ( ) between which a speech is enclosed. They are called the sign of **parenthesis** and they signify that the speech enclosed between them is an **interjected speech**; without it, the sense of the rest of the speech in which it is enclosed is not changed. 

The second is a little point with a crooked stroke drawn upward and forward like this ? and it signifies that the preceding speech is a **question**. 

The third point is **two strokes** drawn forward and a little upward like this // and they are not placed except at the very end of the lines where a word is divided, where one part of a word ends the line and the other part begins the next line. 

Although some now place a small point in the middle of the line for the virgule like this · and for the comma they place two small dots like this : and for the period a little point drawn downward like this . or thus . I have nevertheless strictly kept the first system in this little book, wherever it has not been changed by the **printers**. I have now also...

<meta>This page contains the colophon and concluding remarks for the 1479 Augsburg edition of Boccaccio's "De mulieribus claris" (Concerning Famous Women), translated by Heinrich Steinhöwel.</meta>

The 139th leaf

Several words have been set down without distinction for the better understanding of various dialects; for instance, I treat "sy" <note>original: "sy" — a word meaning she or they</note> as one syllable, and "sy" as two syllables or more—a short one where it is not meaningful, and a long one where it carries significance. For example, in this sentence: "I wish that you would accept my little book with gratitude; that would bring me the highest joy." The book is full of examples of this instruction. Therefore, no more shall be written of it further, in order to avoid lengthiness, but rather to give praise, thanks, and honor to God, who has granted us the grace to finish this little book.

# Front Cover of the Binding

This volume is bound in a **reddish-brown leather**, featuring a complex series of **blind-tooled** <note>Blind-tooling is a decorative technique where a heated metal tool is pressed into leather to create a darkened, indented design without the use of gold leaf or ink.</note> patterns that reflect the meticulous craftsmanship of the period.

The decoration is arranged in several layers:

*   **Outer Borders:** The cover is framed by multiple rectangular borders. The outermost border is decorated with repetitive **circular floral stamps**, followed by inner bands featuring **scrolling vine patterns**.
*   **Central Panel:** The middle of the cover contains a rectangular panel with a **diamond-shaped lattice** <note>This "lozenge" or diamond grid was a popular motif in Renaissance and early modern bookbinding.</note> design. 
*   **Floral Motifs:** Within each diamond of the lattice, a small, stylized **flower** has been stamped.

->**No title or author name is visible on the front cover.**<- 

<note>It was common for historical books to be bound without titles on the front or spine; owners would often recognize the work by its unique tooling or by a handwritten label added later.</note>

<summary>This page describes the ornate, blind-tooled leather front cover of the manuscript, featuring geometric lattice work and floral decorative stamps.</summary>
<keywords>bookbinding, blind-tooling, leather, floral motifs, diamond lattice, historical binding</keywords>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page serves as the table of contents and introduction for the German translation of Giovanni Boccaccio's "De mulieribus claris" (Concerning Famous Women), a highly influential 14th-century collection of biographies of historical and mythological women. This specific edition features the translation by Heinrich Steinhöwel, a prominent figure in the development of German humanism.</meta>

->1<-

¶ Here follows the brief summary of several women
about whom Giovanni Boccaccio has written in Latin
and Doctor Heinrich Steinhöwel has translated into German. <note>original: "geteütschet"</note>
¶ Ch. 1. Concerning Eve on page 1.
¶ Eve transgressed the single commandment of God. And some
believe she was the first to discover spinning. <note>Spinning was considered the foundational domestic art for women in the medieval view.</note>
¶ Ch. 2. Concerning Semiramis on page 2.
¶ Semiramis ruled in the likeness of her son. She con-
quered the land of the Moors and India. She built Babylon and
subdued those same rebels. She took many men
in impurity, including her own son. She permitted un-
chastity to everyone. She devised the wearing of low-hanging 
clothing for women. <note>This refers to the invention of long, flowing garments or gowns.</note>
¶ Ch. 3. Concerning Ops on page 4.
¶ Ops saved three sons from the death which their father Sa-
turn had commanded to be inflicted upon them. <note>In Roman mythology, Ops (identified with Rhea) saved Jupiter, Neptune, and Pluto from being eaten by Saturn.</note>
¶ Ch. 4. Concerning Juno on page 5.
¶ Juno was Jupiter’s sister and wife, the wealthiest,
and was called a goddess of heaven and honored above all god-
desses; the number of her followers lasted until long after the birth of Christ.
¶ Ch. 5. Concerning Ceres on page 6.
¶ Ceres discovered agriculture in Sicily, with all its ap-
purtenances: milling and baking.
¶ Ch. 6. Concerning Minerva on page 8.
¶ Minerva or Pallas maintained her eternal chastity. She
found the way to make olive oil. She found how to use sheep's wool.
She discovered chariot travel; she discovered weap-
ons, armor, and the ordering of battle. She discovered 
counting. She discovered flutes; a beautiful description of her
form, signifying wisdom. She was honored as a goddess.
¶ Ch. 7. Concerning Venus on page 9.
¶ Venus had many men. She devised the brothels <note>original: "frauenhewser," literally 'women-houses'</note>
and forced honorable women into them, and arranged for 
young girls to be available for guests.

<margin>Boccaccio</margin>

<meta>This page appears to be a table of contents or a summarized index of chapters from a historical or mythological work, specifically Giovanni Boccaccio’s 'De Mulieribus Claris' (Of Famous Women), which was widely translated into Early New High German in the late 15th century. It continues the list of influential women and their deeds.</meta>

¶ **Chapter 8 regarding Isis, page 11**
¶ Isis was violated by King Jupiter and sent to Egypt. There she was elected queen because she found the people to be wild and beast-like, and she taught them how to live in a human manner. She discovered the first Egyptian letters <note>Isis is credited here with the invention of the Egyptian alphabet or hieroglyphs, a common medieval attribution.</note>.

¶ **Chapter 9 regarding Europa, page 13**
¶ Europa was seduced by Jupiter. This is a lesson for young women to guard themselves against many who wander about <note>original: "hin vnnd her webern"—literally "weaving to and fro," implying predatory or restless men</note> according to their own desires.

¶ **Chapter 10 regarding Libya, page 13**
¶ Libya ruled so well that the entire kingdom of Libya kept its name from her.

¶ **Chapter 11 regarding Marsepia, page 14**
¶ Marsepia and Lampedo were valiant <note>original: "streitbar"—meaning warlike or capable in combat</note> queens in Amazonia, showing how the rule of women had its origin.

¶ **Chapter 12 regarding Thisbe, page 15**
¶ Thisbe fell upon a sword with her beloved Pyramus <note>The text reads "Priamo," likely a transcription error or variant for Pyramus, the tragic lover in the Babylonian tale.</note>.

¶ **Chapter 13 regarding Hypermestra, page 18**
¶ Hypermestra kept her husband Lynceus <note>original: "Liniū"</note> alive when her forty-nine sisters murdered all their husbands <note>Referring to the myth of the Danaïdes, the fifty daughters of Danaus who were ordered to kill their husbands on their wedding night.</note>.

¶ **Chapter 14 regarding Niobe, page 20**
¶ In her pride <note>original: "hochfart"—hubris or excessive arrogance</note>, Niobe wanted to be honored above the gods; because of this, her thirteen children died, and her husband Amphion killed himself out of sorrow.

¶ **Chapter 15 regarding Hypsipyle, page 21**
¶ Hypsipyle <note>original: "Isiphile"</note> saved her father when all the men were killed by the women <note>Referring to the women of Lemnos who rose up against their husbands.</note>. Because of this, she was driven into exile <note>original: "ellend"—in this period, the word meant both misery and the state of living in a foreign land/exile.</note>. This is a lesson on the kindness of children toward their fathers.

¶ **Chapter 16 regarding Medea, page 23**
¶ Medea was the greatest sorceress. She helped Jason against... <note>The text breaks off here mid-sentence.</note>

...to win over her father for the golden fleece <note>Original: "guldin schepfer." While "schepfer" literally means "creator," in the context of the Jason and Medea myth, this refers to the Golden Fleece or the ram that bore it</note>. She had her brother hacked into many pieces. She killed Jason’s wife and child. This is a good lesson for women to govern their eyes <note>A moralizing warning to control one's desires or the objects of one's gaze</note>.

### Chapter 17: Concerning Arachne, page 25

Arachne invented the weaving of linen and the knitting of nets. She was a master weaver. She hanged herself in excessive pride because she was defeated by Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom and craft, who competed with Arachne in a weaving contest</note>.

### Chapter 18: Concerning Orithia, page 26

Orithia, on account of her warlike hands <note>Original: "streitbarer hendhalb," referring to her prowess in battle</note>, was compared to Hercules.

### Chapter 19: Concerning Erythraea, page 27

Erythraea, or Eriphila, was a Sibyl <term>Sibyl: A woman in ancient times believed to act as an oracle or prophetess</term> and prophesied many various things.

### Chapter 20: Concerning Medusa, page 27

Medusa became wealthy through her beauty and wisdom; however, Perseus carried away her riches.

### Chapter 21: Concerning Iole, page 29

Iole tamed and feminized <note>Original: "weibet," literally "woman-ed." This refers to the legend where the hero Hercules became so infatuated with Iole (or Omphale, with whom she is often confused in this period) that he wore women's clothing and spun wool for her</note> the strongest Her[cules]...

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents for a work likely based on Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women), a collection of biographies of historical and mythological women.</meta>

¶ Chapter 26. About **Procris**, page 37.
¶ Procris’s supreme constancy was weakened by gold, and she was accidentally shot by her loving husband. <note>Procris was the wife of Cephalus; in the myth, she was killed by her husband with a magical spear when he mistook her for a wild animal while she was spying on him out of jealousy.</note>

¶ Chapter 27. About **Argia**, page 29.
¶ Argia showed exceptional love for her deceased husband. <note>Argia risked her life to give her husband, Polynices, a proper burial after he fell in the war of the Seven Against Thebes.</note>

¶ Chapter 28. About **Manto**, page 40.
¶ Manto was a great prophetess through God. The city of Mantua takes its name from her. <note>original: "Mantho hat den namen von jr"</note>

¶ Chapter 29. About the **wives of the Minyans**, page 41.
¶ The wives of the Minyans took the place of their husbands who were sentenced to death. A beautiful lesson in true love. <note>These women visited their husbands in prison, exchanged clothes with them to let them escape, and remained behind to face the consequences.</note>

¶ Chapter 30. About **Penthesilea**, page 43.
¶ Penthesilea, queen of the women <note>The Amazons</note>, invented the throwing axe and fought manfully against the Greeks to please Hector.

¶ Chapter 31. About **Polyxena**, page 44.
¶ Polyxena was beheaded by Neoptolemus upon the grave of his father, Achilles.

¶ Chapter 32. About **Hecuba**, page 45.
¶ Hecuba was driven to madness by her misfortune: the loss of her husband, her children, and the city of Troy. <note>Hecuba was the Queen of Troy; her "madness" or "fury" refers to her grief-stricken transformation after the fall of the city.</note>

¶ Chapter 33. About **Cassandra**, page 46.
¶ Cassandra was strangled by Clytemnestra because of her prophecy. <note>Cassandra was the daughter of Priam, cursed to speak true prophecies that no one would believe.</note>

¶ Chapter 34. About **Clytemnestra**, page 47.
¶ Clytemnestra had her husband murdered, for which she and the murderer were killed by her son. <note>The husband was Agamemnon, and the son was Orestes.</note>

¶ Chapter 35. About **Helen**, page 48.
¶ Helen was abducted from the Greeks by Paris, and because of this, Troy was destroyed.

3

¶ Chapter 36. Concerning Circe, page 51.
¶ Circe, as the fable tells us, transformed men into irrational animals through the use of sorcery.

¶ Chapter 37. Concerning Camilla, page 52.
¶ Camilla was driven into exile with her father and later reinstated. She preserved her purity and fought against the Trojans. A lesson on the virtuous conduct of young women. <note>Camilla was a legendary warrior-maiden of the Volsci who appears in Virgil’s Aeneid.</note>

¶ Chapter 38. Concerning Penelope, page 54.
¶ Penelope maintained her holy state of widowhood in steadfast loyalty to her husband Ulysses, waiting for him for twenty years.

¶ Chapter 39. Concerning Lavinia, page 56.
¶ Lavinia fled into a forest with her stepson and there gave birth to Aeneas Silvius. <note>Lavinia was the daughter of King Latinus and the final wife of the Trojan hero Aeneas.</note>

¶ Chapter 40. Concerning Dido, page 57.
¶ Dido founded the city of Carthage. She remained in a state of pure widowhood until her death. A beautiful lesson in praise of the state of widowhood. <note>In this version of the story, Dido remains faithful to her deceased first husband rather than dying for love of Aeneas.</note>

¶ Chapter 41. Concerning Nicaula, page 63.
¶ Nicaula was the wisest in the arts and the wealthiest; she was a queen from the most distant <term>land of the Moors</term> <note>original: "morenland"; likely referring to Ethiopia or the Queen of Sheba.</note>.

¶ Chapter 42. Concerning Pamphile, page 63.
¶ Pamphile was the first to discover the use of cotton. <note>original: "baumwol"; Pamphile of Kos is traditionally credited with discovering how to weave silk or fine linens.</note>

¶ Chapter 43. Concerning Rhea Ilia, page 63.
¶ Rhea Ilia was a <term>nun</term> <note>original: "kloster fraw"; referring to her status as a Vestal Virgin, though the translator uses contemporary religious terminology.</note> and gave birth to Remus and Romulus, the founders of the city of Rome, for which she was put to death. A beautiful lesson concerning nuns.

¶ Chapter 44. Concerning Gaia Cyrilla, page 65.
¶ Gaia Cyrilla was the most domestic of queens. <note>Also known as Tanaquil, she was a Roman queen famed for her skill at the spinning wheel and her domestic virtues.</note>

¶ Chapter 45. Concerning Sappho, page 65.
¶ Sappho was the most learned in <term>Latin poetry</term> <note>original: "lateinischen gedichten"; though Sappho was a Greek poet, medieval and Renaissance texts often used "Latin" as a general term for classical scholarship or referenced her through Latin translations.</note> and all manner of stringed instruments.

¶ Chapter 46. Concerning Tullia, page 66.
¶ Tullia had her husband, sister, and father killed, and she drove a wagon over her father so that she was splattered with his blood. <note>This refers to the "Wicked" Tullia, daughter of the Roman King Servius Tullius.</note>

Each entry provides a moralizing summary of the woman's life, highlighting virtues like widowhood and domesticity or vices like sorcery and murder.</summary>

<keywords>Circe, Camilla, Penelope, Dido, Nicaula, Rhea Ilia, Sappho, Tullia, Roman history, Greek mythology, widowhood, female virtue</keywords>
