<meta>This page continues the table of contents and summaries for a 15th-century German edition of Giovanni Boccaccio's "De mulieribus claris" (Of Famous Women), a collection of biographies of historical and mythological women.</meta>

¶ **Chapter 47. About Lucretia, page 69.**
¶ Lucretia kills herself in the presence of her father, husband, and friends, so that she might excuse herself from the forced adultery <note>Lucretia was a legendary Roman noblewoman whose suicide after being raped by the son of the king led to the revolution that established the Roman Republic.</note>.

¶ **Chapter 48. About Tomyris, page 71.**
¶ Tomyris <note>original: "Thamiri"</note> fells the most powerful emperor, Cyrus, in battle <note>Tomyris was the queen of the Massagetae who defeated Cyrus the Great.</note>.

¶ **Chapter 49. About Leaena, page 74.**
¶ Leaena <note>original: "Leena"</note>, the great courtesan <term>harlot: the original German "hůr" refers here to a professional companion or courtesan of high status</term>, bit off her own tongue so that she could not reveal the secrets of her lovers <note>Leaena was a Greek hetaira who supposedly bit off her tongue to avoid betraying a conspiracy against the tyrants of Athens.</note>.

¶ **Chapter 50. About Athaliah, page 74.**
¶ Athaliah <note>original: "Atalia"</note>, out of greed to rule without interference, had the entire royal line of David killed; a lesson <note>original: "lee" (Lehre)</note> to despise usurped power, and a warning against the rapacious, biting rule of masters.

      ¶ **Chapter—again—50, though it should be 51.** This occurred by mistake; therefore, take note of it, for the count of women is still one hundred. This is the second "50": **Cloelia, page 76**, and the count will be restored at Irene.

¶ Cloelia led her companions on a farm horse across the fearsome river Tiber to release them from captivity <note>original: "geſellſchaft" here referring to their status as hostages; Cloelia was a legendary Roman maiden who escaped Etruscan captivity.</note>.

      ¶ **Chapter 51. About Hippo, page 77.**
¶ Hippo leaped into the sea to preserve her chastity from the kidnappers <note>According to legend, Hippo committed suicide after being abducted by sailors to protect her honor.</note>.

      ¶ **Chapter 52. About Veturia, page 78.**
¶ Veturia saved Rome, for which reason all women were endowed with great freedoms, who before were little honored by the men <note>Veturia was the mother of the Roman general Coriolanus; she successfully pleaded with him to stop his attack on Rome.</note>.

      ¶ **Chapter 53. About Megulia; it is misplaced.**
      **You will find it on page 84.**
¶ Megulia was called *Dotata* <note>Latin: "the endowed one"</note>, which means "well-endowed," because of her large dowry <note>original: "heymſteŵr"</note>, which, however, in these current times would be considered small.

<meta>This page continues the table of contents for a German edition of Giovanni Boccaccio's *De mulieribus claris* (Of Famous Women). It lists chapters 54 through 66, detailing various historical and mythological women, their deeds, and their moral significance.</meta>

[Page number in top right corner: 4]

¶ Chapter 54. Concerning Thamyris, page 80.
¶ Thamyris, a nun <note>original: "kloster fraw"; the translator uses contemporary religious terms like "nun" to describe ancient women who lived in seclusion or remained virgins</note>, was the best painter.

¶ Chapter 55. Concerning Artemisia, page 80.
Artemisia drank the ashes of her dead husband and had a tomb built for him, which is one of the seven wonders of the world. <note>This refers to the Mausoleum at Halicarnassus, built for King Mausolus.</note>

¶ Chapter 56. Concerning Virginia, page 84; and you will find the correct image belonging to this on page 88.
¶ Virginia was killed by her father because of the false judgment of the wicked judges. <note>A reference to the story of Verginia, whose father killed her to protect her from the corrupt official Appius Claudius.</note>

¶ Chapter 58. Concerning Irene, page 86.
¶ Irene was the best painter.

Chapter 59. Concerning Leontium, page 86.
¶ Leontium was the most learned woman; she would have been even greater had she not stained her art with unchastity.

¶ Chapter 60. Concerning Olympias, page 86.
¶ Olympias gave birth to Alexander through adultery. She was driven out and reinstated, but was then captured and killed. <note>Olympias was the mother of Alexander the Great.</note>

¶ Chapter 61. Concerning Claudia, page 88.
¶ Claudia, a nun <note>likely referring to a Vestal Virgin</note>, killed <note>original: "töttet"; historical accounts usually state she "saved" (rettet) her father from being pulled from his chariot, suggesting a potential printer's error in the source text</note> her father.

¶ Chapter 62. Concerning Virginia, daughter of Aulus <note>original: "vgine alucij"</note>, page 88; and you will find the correct image belonging to this on page 84.
¶ Virginia established a temple to womanly modesty and honor. <term>Modesty: the original "schā" (shame/modesty) refers to the Roman virtue of *Pudicitia*.</term>

¶ Chapter 63. Concerning Flora, page 89.
¶ Flora, the wealthy harlot, established a shameful festival. <note>The Floralia, an ancient Roman festival.</note>

¶ Chapter 64. Concerning the Roman Woman, page 91.
¶ The Roman woman breastfed her mother, who had been sentenced to die of hunger. <note>A variation of the "Roman Charity" legend, which more commonly features a daughter nursing her father in prison.</note>

Chapter 65. Concerning Marcia, page 92.
¶ Marcia, daughter of Varro, the eternally pure nun, was the best sculptor and painter.

Chapter 66. Concerning Sulpicia, page 93.
¶ Sulpicia was recognized as the most venerable of all Roman women; a lesson in the virtue of women.

<summary>This page provides a table of contents for chapters 54 to 66 of a collection of biographies of famous women, featuring figures such as Artemisia, Olympias, and various female artists of antiquity.</summary>
<keywords>Boccaccio, Thamyris, Artemisia, Virginia, Olympias, Flora, Roman Charity, female artists, virtue, Roman history, Early New High German</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents or summary of chapters from a biographical collection of famous women, likely a German translation of Giovanni Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women).</meta>

### Chapter 67. About Harmonia. Page 94.
Harmonia offered herself to death in loyalty, just as her maiden had done for her. <note>Harmonia was a Sicilian princess who was saved by a servant who took her place during an assassination attempt; Harmonia then revealed herself so as not to outlive her loyal servant.</note>

### Chapter 68. About Busa of Canosa. Page 95.
Busa out of generosity gave ten thousand men food and provisions. <note>Busa was an Italian noblewoman who provided for the survivors of the Battle of Cannae.</note>

### Chapter 69. About Sophonisbe. Page 96.
Sophonisbe's morning gift <term>morning gift: a traditional legal gift from a husband to his wife the morning after the wedding</term> was a poisoned drink. She drank it willingly and without fear. <note>She took her own life to avoid being paraded in a Roman triumph.</note>

### Chapter 70. About Theoxena. Page 98.
Theoxena killed her children, herself, and her husband before she would enter into servitude. <note>original: "eygenschafft" referring to slavery or capture</note>

### Chapter 71. About Berenice. Page 100.
Berenice killed the murderers of her children with her own hand.

### Chapter 72. About the wife of Orgiago. Page 101.
The woman Drigiagontis <note>original: "drigiagōtis", referring to Chiomara, the wife of Orgiago</note> cleansed her body and mind with the head of the man who forced himself upon her. <note>After being raped by a Roman centurion, she had him killed and brought his head to her husband to prove her honor was restored.</note>

### Chapter 73. About Tertia Aemilia. Page 102.
Tertia Aemilia was patient regarding the adultery of her husband.

### Chapter 74. About Claudia Quinta. Page 104.
Claudia Quinta went out with all the noblest Roman women as the most slandered, and returned home as the most celebrated. <note>She was accused of unchastity but proved her innocence by miraculously pulling a grounded ship carrying the statue of the goddess Cybele.</note>

### Chapter 75. About Hypsicratia. Page 105.
Hypsicratia was killed by her husband, to whom she had previously shown great loyalty. <note>The German text implies she was killed by him, though classical sources emphasize her loyalty to Mithridates VI during his defeats.</note>

### Chapter 76. About Sempronia. Page 106.
Sempronia, the most learned, the most eloquent, and the unchaste, was killed.

### Chapter 77. About the women of the Cimbrians, who are the Celts. Page 107.
<margin>original: "kurwalhen" — an archaic German term for Celtic or Romanic peoples</margin>
The women of the Cimbrians stabbed their children to death and hanged themselves before they would enter into servitude.

### Chapter 78. About Julia. Page 108.
Julia died of shock because of her husband's bloody clothes. <note>Julia, the daughter of Julius Caesar, suffered a miscarriage and died after seeing the blood-stained robes of her husband Pompey, which she mistook for a sign of his death.</note>

<summary>This page serves as a table of contents or summary for chapters 67 through 78 of a work on famous women, highlighting themes of loyalty, suicide to avoid slavery, and vengeance for family honor.</summary>
<keywords>Harmonia, Busa of Canosa, Sophonisba, Theoxena, Berenice, Chiomara, Tertia Aemilia, Claudia Quinta, Hypsicratia, Sempronia, Cimbrians, Julia, feminine virtue, martyrdom, historical women</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as a table of contents or register for a biographical collection of famous women, following the tradition of Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (On Famous Women). It continues the numbering from previous chapters.</meta>

¶ **Chapter 79. On Porcia, daughter of Cato.** Page 109.
¶ Porcia, daughter of Cato, burned her throat so that she died. <note>Porcia Catonis, wife of Brutus, famously committed suicide by swallowing hot coals after learning of her husband's death.</note>

¶ **Chapter 80. On Hortensia.** Page 110.
¶ Hortensia was the advocate for the women before the three men. <note>Hortensia famously delivered a speech before the Triumvirs (Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus) in 42 BCE to protest the taxation of wealthy Roman women.</note>

¶ **Chapter 81. On Sulpicia.** Page 111.
¶ Sulpicia followed her husband into exile. <note>Original: *ellende* (misery/exile). Sulpicia defied her mother to join her husband, Lentulus Cruscellio, while he was proscribed and in hiding.</note>

¶ **Chapter 82. On Mariamne.** Page 111.
¶ Mariamne, the most beautiful, was innocently killed by her husband Herod. <note>Mariamne I was the second wife of Herod the Great; she was executed by him on charges of infidelity, though she is historically regarded as a victim of his paranoia.</note>

¶ **Chapter 83. On Cleopatra.** Page 113.
¶ Cleopatra murdered her husband; she gained great lands through impurity <note>Original: *unlautterkeit* (unchastity/impurity).</note>; she killed herself.

¶ **Chapter 84. On Antonia.** Page 117.
¶ Antonia maintained her chaste state of widowhood and [provided] an example against the unchaste through [her love for] Drusus. <note>Antonia Minor, daughter of Mark Antony, remained a widow for decades after the death of her husband Nero Claudius Drusus, becoming a symbol of Roman virtue.</note>

¶ **Chapter 85. On Agrippina.** Page 117.
¶ Agrippina died of hunger; Tiberius could not be moved against his resolve. <note>Agrippina the Elder was the granddaughter of Augustus; she was starved to death while exiled by the Emperor Tiberius.</note>

¶ **Chapter 86. On Paulina.** Page 119.
¶ Paulina lay with a youth, believing he was a god. <note>A reference to a story from Josephus where a virtuous woman named Paulina was deceived by a suitor, Mundus, who bribed priests to tell her the god Anubis wished to lie with her.</note>

¶ **Chapter 87. On Agrippina, mother of Nero.** Page 120.
¶ Agrippina, mother of Nero, killed her cousin and husband and took Claudius; she brought her son to the empire; she committed many murders; she was killed by her son. <note>Agrippina the Younger was the mother of Nero and wife of the Emperor Claudius, whom she was accused of poisoning.</note>

¶ **Chapter 88. On Epicharis.** Page 123.
¶ Epicharis, after much torture, hanged herself before she would betray the conspirators against Nero. <note>Original: *die geschwornen* (those who had sworn an oath/conspirators). Epicharis was a key figure in the Pisonian conspiracy.</note>

¶ **Chapter 89. On Pompeia Paulina.** Page 124.
¶ Pompeia Paulina wanted to die with her husband by bloodletting when Nero ordered his death. <note>She was the wife of the philosopher Seneca; she attempted suicide alongside him, but survived.</note>

<meta>This page begins the dedication of Heinrich Steinhöwel’s 1473 German translation of Giovanni Boccaccio's *De mulieribus claris*. It follows the title or frontispiece of the manuscript/incunable.</meta>

[Handwritten page number: 6]

**To the** most illustrious Princess and lady, Lady Eleanor, Duchess of Austria, etc., his most gracious lady, Heinrich Steinhöwel of Weil an der Würm <note>A town now known as Weil der Stadt, near Stuttgart.</note>, doctor of medicine, offers himself for all humble services.

Most illustrious Princess, as I was not at home this past year for notable reasons and was nearly entirely unburdened by the cares of medical practice, I thought not to spend my time in complete idleness. And I took it upon myself, for the praise and honor of your Grace and all women, to translate into German <note>original: "zeteütſchende"</note> the little book of Giovanni Boccaccio regarding the wise and illustrious women who were recorded in eternal memory by the ancient chroniclers for their singular undertakings.

Yet they are recorded differently: some for their wise and good works and stories, so that womankind might wish to follow them; others are set down for their shameful misdeeds, so that their deeds might be shunned. 

As I had previously considered in my own mind to dedicate this little book to your Grace—so that it would not lie idle with me, but with the help of a favorable escort <note>A metaphor for the protection and prestige provided by a powerful patron's name.</note>, might travel more safely into the world and become more widely known, since good things (as Aristotle writes) become better the more common <note>Steinhöwel refers to the Aristotelian principle that a good is more "divine" when it serves a community rather than an individual.</note> they are—and since I also found that it should be sent neither to princes nor lords, but (since it speaks only of women) to a discerning, God-fearing, and just female judge of all things, I have recognized you, most illustrious Princess, through common renown as such a person, and a lover of all the good arts and artists.

Therefore, I wished to commend this diligent work of mine—concerning the ninety-nine <note>While Boccaccio’s original Latin work contained 106 biographies, Steinhöwel’s German adaptation traditionally included 99.</note> illustrious women—to your Grace’s higher wisdom, as to a true and strict judge...

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of Heinrich Steinhöwel’s German translation of Boccaccio’s "Of Famous Women" to Eleanor of Austria, a known literary patron. Steinhöwel explains that he used a period of professional leisure to translate the work to provide moral examples for women to follow or avoid.</summary>

<keywords>Heinrich Steinhöwel, Giovanni Boccaccio, Eleanor of Austria, patronage, Of Famous Women, translation, Early New High German, ethics, medical doctor</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the translator's preface or dedication to a collection of biographies of famous women, likely a translation of Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris." The author is addressing a noble patroness, explaining his editorial choices and the moral utility of the book.</meta>

to recognize and judge whether they are worthy to walk abroad in the
world, for the exercise of human minds, rather 
than for great profit. Humbly praying, by the 
holy marriage in which you are highly 
praised above other women, to graciously 
receive this my little book and to hear it before passing judgment. 
For I do not doubt that the virtuous, laudable, and strong deeds 
of many women described therein will 
bring joy to your mind. The impure [deeds] will cause wonder and 
provide a warning to shun such things and cast them aside, 
just as when one wants to pick roses: 
although the thorns stand around them, they are nonetheless 
separated from them and the roses are picked. The bees 
also do not suck every flower to which they fly. 
They do not consume any completely, but when they have sucked 
from them what is useful for the honey, the rest 
they leave unharmed. In such a manner, 
Saint Basil <note>original: "Sanctus Basilius"; referring to Basil the Great (c. 330–379 AD), whose famous treatise advised Christians on how to derive moral benefit from "pagan" classical literature.</note> teaches us to read such books, 
mixed with good and shameful <note>original: "schantzlichen" (schändlichen)</note> deeds, 
so that we pick the roses from the thorns and the honey from the flowers. 
That is: that we pick out what serves us for 
virtuous deeds, leaving the rest lying in contempt. 
If I should also occasionally write coarser words than 
befits your Grace's purity to speak or hear, 
I beg you to take it in the best way. For those same 
words, without changing the true meaning, could not 
be replaced with circumlocutions <term>circumlocutions: "vmbreden"—the act of using indirect or polite language to avoid blunt or "improper" terms.</term>. 
Therefore, and especially so that the words are not counted as impure 
where the evil deeds are not present [in the reader], 
so I have occasionally, with the permission 
of pure women, set [them] down here, so that the correct sense 
remains unchanged, as is written of the women Flora, Leena, 
and several others, whose unchaste 
deeds were so impure that it would be improper to gloss them 
with circumlocutions, but rather [to speak plainly] to the disgrace 
of their worthless deeds.

<summary>The author justifies including stories of both virtuous and "impure" women by using the metaphors of picking roses from thorns and bees gathering honey. He explains that he uses plain, sometimes "coarse" language to properly condemn vice rather than masking it with polite speech.</summary>

<keywords>St. Basil, moral education, Flora, Leena, virtue and vice, literary theory, metaphors of reading</keywords>

Therefore, I have waited to conclude my little book with the described life of such a virtuous woman of our own time. But since I have been deprived of my hope until now <note>The author likely intended to include a specific biography of his patroness but did not receive the necessary details or permission in time</note>, I did not want to let this little book of mine lie idle any longer. Instead, I send it to your **Princely Grace**, as was my first intention, to serve as a crown to these one hundred <note>original: "hundredtisten"; likely referring to the total number of famous women in the collection</note> illustrious women. I have waited for your further praise because I do not doubt that, even though you cannot be present in many places at once, this little book will spread your virtuous works far and wide. It will remind other women to follow your example and the other praiseworthy deeds described herein. 

And I pray your high-born Grace to receive this in good intention, as I have offered it, in the hope of being placed under your Grace’s protection and favor for all time. May God protect you in holiness for a long time to come.

The First Leaf

The serpent loses its venom through the power of a maiden bringing forth. <note>original: "Vipera vim perdit vi pariente puella." This Latin verse is a common medieval wordplay. It contrasts Eve, through whom the serpent (the devil) gained power, with the Virgin Mary, whose child-bearing took that power away.</note>

## Concerning Eve, Chapter One

[Woodcut illustration of Adam and Eve in the Garden of Eden. They stand on either side of the Tree of Knowledge, around which a serpent with a human head is coiled. The serpent hands an apple to Eve, who in turn hands one to Adam. They are surrounded by a low brick wall.]

**S**ince I intend to write of the illustrious deeds through which women are enlightened and henceforth remarkably ennobled, it is fitting that the beginning be taken from the mother of the human race. For just as Eve is the noblest mother, so was she ennobled beyond all appearance; for she was not, like other mortal humans, born into this fragile vale of tears <term>vale of tears: a traditional Christian phrase (Latin: "lacrimarum valle") referring to the hardships of earthly life</term> for a life of toil. She was also not forged with that same hammer and anvil <note>This is a metaphorical way of saying she was not conceived and born through the usual biological process of human reproduction.</note>, nor did she come into life weeping for her sins, nor was she as weak as others. Rather, as has never happened to any other, the best Craftsman of all things <note>A reference to God as the "Great Architect" or "Workman."</note> had formed Adam with His own hand from a clod of earth—

<summary>This opening page introduces a history of famous women, beginning with the creation of Eve. It highlights her unique status as the first woman, created directly by God rather than born into the hardships of the world like her descendants.</summary>

<keywords>Eve, Creation, Adam, Garden of Eden, Paradise, Nobility of Women, Origins of Humanity</keywords>

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>This is the first page (foliated as leaf 1) of the manuscript. The text begins mid-sentence, likely continuing from a preface or the very beginning of the Genesis narrative as interpreted by the author.</meta>

->The 1st leaf<-

...led by delight, and having fallen into a sweet sleep <note>This refers to the "deep sleep" God cast upon Adam (Genesis 2:21) to facilitate the creation of Eve.</note>. Through the art known to Him alone, He drew from Adam’s side a compliant wife for the man, joyful in the pleasure of that place and the sight of her Creator—an immortal woman over all things, and now the waking man’s spouse, by whom she was also named Eve. What greater or more glorious thing could ever happen to any born being? Beyond this, we may well consider that she was of wondrous beauty. For what was ever made by the finger of God <note>original: "finger gottes"; a common theological metaphor for God's direct creative power.</note> that did not surpass all other things in beauty? 

And although such beauty is transitory over time, and often diminishes in the midst of blooming youth from a small sickness, it is nonetheless numbered among the foremost gifts of women. Through beauty, many a woman has received great honor from the unconsidered judgment of mortal men; thus, it is not superfluous that beauty is now established as a primary ornament for the praise and glorious works of women, and shall often be mentioned hereafter. 

Afterward, when she—by right of her origin and residence—became a citizen of Paradise and was surrounded by a radiance unknown to us, eagerly enjoying the delights of that place with her husband, the envious enemy of her salvation <note>A reference to the Serpent or Satan, often described in medieval literature as "envious" of humanity's place in Eden.</note> poured a wicked counsel into her mind: that if she transgressed the single commandment set for them by God, they might attain even greater glory. And since she, out of womanly fickleness <note>original: "weiplicher leichtfertikeÿt"; the translator uses the contemporary Renaissance perspective that women were naturally more prone to persuasion or levity.</note>, believed him more than was beneficial for her, and foolishly intended to climb higher, she—before all else—made her husband swayable to her opinion through flattering caresses. And so, in contempt of the commandment, they tasted the apples of the tree of the knowledge of good...

and evil/ they deceived themselves and all their descendants, falling from future rest and eternity into anxious labor and miserable death, and out of a delightful fatherland into the midst of brambles, clods, and crags. <note>The "delightful fatherland" refers to the Garden of Eden; the "brambles, clods, and crags" represent the harsh, uncultivated earth after the Fall.</note> For when the visible light with which they were surrounded departed, they were punished by their creator and covered with skins <note>original: "kosten," likely referring to the coats of skins mentioned in Genesis 3:21</note>, driven out of the place of delight, and came miserably into the level fields. And even if I remain silent about the heat and the frost and other hardships, she nonetheless arrived, weary from labor, at a great and mortal old age.

### The second leaf

**S**emiramis, noble and most ancient, was a queen
of Assyria. But from what origins she may
have come, antiquity has carried away. <note>The author suggests that her true historical origins are lost to time.</note>
Therefore, it pleases the ancients to say in the
manner of a myth that she was a daughter of Neptune <note>original: "Neptuni"</note>,
whom they wish to be a son of Saturn, and they say
from an erring faith that he is a god of the sea.
And if that is not to be believed, it is yet well to
think that she was born of noble lineage.
She was wedded to King Ninus of Assyria
and bore him an only son named Ninyas.
But after Ninus had made all of Asia and
finally the Bactrians <note>original: "bacharos"; likely referring to the Bactrian campaign</note> subject to him,
and had died from the shot of an arrow while she
was still young and the son a child, she thought she
could not safely entrust so great and mighty an
Oriental realm to the tender age of her son.
And she was so high-spirited that she dared
to receive and rule through art and wit the
lands which the bold man had made subject
with weapons and conquered with strength.
For through feminine cunning and exceedingly
great devised cleverness, she deceived the army
of her dead husband.
It was also no wonder, for Semiramis was
very similar in appearance to her son;
likewise, due to age, her feminine voice
did not differ from the boy's voice. <note>The text explains how her physical resemblance to her son allowed her to impersonate him.</note>
There was also no or little difference in body,
except that she was a little taller. Through
such help, and also so that nothing else
might happen in the future to make her
cunning obvious, she covered her head with
a wrap <note>original: "überschlag"</note>, with hidden hands and
with hidden feet. Since such clothing was
unusual for the Assyrians, so that the
innovation would not bring wonder to
the inhabitants, she commanded all the people
to wear such clothing. Thus

# The 3rd leaf

The spouse of the late Ninus was received into royal majesty in the guise of her son, and the woman, in the form of the child, with wonderful diligence. And under a feigned masculinity, she maintained knightly teachings and wisdom, and performed such deeds that were highly esteemed by even the strongest of men. Since she spared herself no labor and took no fright at any worrisome things, and through her unheard-of deeds <note>original: "ge-schichten," here meaning her exploits or history</note> had overcome everyone’s envy, she was not ashamed to reveal to everyone who she was and how she had feigned such things through womanly cunning. 

It was as if she wanted to show that it is not a necessity for a man <note>The text suggests that biological sex is not a requirement for leadership</note> to rule, but rather a manly spirit; and as much as everyone felt greater wonder at this, so much further was the majesty of this same woman illuminated. And to further relate her stories: she not only protected the empire won by her husband with weapons through her adopted manly spirit, but she also added Ethiopia <note>original: "moren land" (Moor-land), a common term for African territories in this period</note>, which she had previously completely devastated through war. 

Afterward, she turned her powerful weapons toward India, where no one had ever gone before except her husband. Afterward, she rebuilt the ancient work of Nimrod <note>A biblical figure traditionally credited with building the Tower of Babel</note>, Babylon. That same city was powerful at that time in the region of Shinar <note>The biblical name for the plains of Mesopotamia</note>. She surrounded it with walls made of baked stones, sand, resin, and other bitumens <note>original: "gummi," referring here to the bonding agents and pitch used in ancient construction</note>, so high, thick, and exceedingly long that it was a marvel to behold. 

And so that we do not withhold from her many other stories one that is well worthy of memory: it is truly said of her that when she had settled her affairs in peace and was now resting, and one day was having her hair combed with womanly diligence by her maidservants and having braids woven from it according to the custom of the land, it happened that she...

**Juno**, a daughter of Saturn and Ops, according to the poems of the poets and the fables <note>original: "ire sal," referring to the mythological stories or "errors" of the ancients</note> of the heathens <note>a historical term used by Christian authors to describe the ancient Greeks and Romans</note>, is elevated high in dignity above all other heathen women, so much so that over a very long time her name could not be forgotten.

At last, she was brought from that same city to Rome and into the Capitol, placed into the chamber of Jupiter the Greatest as his wife, and it was named the Temple of Juno the Queen. There she was highly honored for a long time by the powerful Romans of all the world with many kinds of dignity—even after the time when God had appeared on earth as a man.

# The Sixth Leaf

Ceres is also known as Ops, Cybele, Rhea <note>original: "thea"</note>, Proserpina, Vesta, the Nourishing Virgin, the Great Mother, and the Good Goddess by various and different people. Juvenal also says that they appease the Good Goddess with the belly of a sow <note>original: "abdomine porcae"; this refers to traditional Roman sacrifices to the goddess Bona Dea, often identified with Ceres</note>.

->### Concerning Ceres, the goddess of fruits and a queen of Sicily; the fifth chapter.<-

[Woodcut illustration showing agricultural scenes: a man carrying a sack into a barn, oxen pulling a cart with a person harvesting grain, and a crowned woman holding a scepter labeled "Ceres" with a man working at her feet.]

Ceres, as some say, was the most illustrious queen of Sicily. Because of her extraordinary intellect and reason, she was the very first among her people to devise <term>agriculture|the cultivation of fields</term> and to tame oxen to the yoke. She also invented the plow and the <term>plowshare|the "sech" or cutting blade of a plow</term> with which, through the aforementioned help of the oxen, she cut through and turned over the earth, and hid the seed within it. When the growth reached full harvest, she taught them how to thresh, grind, and bake, and how to make food for the people who previously had only sustained themselves on acorns and wild apples <note>original: "holczöpfel"</note>. Because of this merit—even though she was a mortal woman—the people believed she was a goddess of grain, sent down to them from heaven, and they elevated her with divine...

...Ceres, and they believed she was a daughter of Saturn and Cybele. They further say how Orcus <note>A name for Pluto, the god of the underworld.</note>, the king of Molossia <note>A region in ancient Epirus; the author here euhemerizes (treats as historical) the myth of the King of the Underworld.</note>, during a time of turbulence at sea, kidnapped her daughter Proserpina, whom she had borne from her brother Jupiter. And because they both possessed similar merits, they have been placed together in one chapter.

However, whether their ingenious discovery is to be praised or scolded, I do not know. For who would scold the fact that the wandering, wild people were drawn out of the forests and into the cities? Who would also not praise that those who previously lived irrationally <note>original: "vnuernünfftlichē," meaning without the use of human reason.</note> have been called to better habits? Or that acorns were transformed into grain <term>Grain (Korn): Specifically referring to cereal crops like wheat or rye, which replaced the primitive diet of foraging.</term>, through which the body becomes more presentable and the limbs stronger, and a consistent nourishment is given to mankind? Who would scold that the rough and ignorant times were transformed into orderly statutes and life? Who does not praise that sluggish minds and reason were turned toward investigation and practice? To whom would it not be pleasing that the coarse louts were drawn out of the caves and brought into building and rational practice, through which so many cities were expanded, so many newly built, so many kingdoms increased, and so many praiseworthy customs were invented and maintained? All this because the art of grain was discovered. And as this art is good in itself, I believe—according to the judgment of many people—that whoever would scold it would be called insane. 

On the other hand, who can praise that such a multitude of people, who had been here and there in the forests, eating acorns and crab apples...

He who was accustomed to the milk of wild beasts, to small herbs, and to water; spirits free of all anxiety <note>The German "sorgfältigkeit" here implies heavy worry or the burdensome cares of civilization</note>, content with the law of nature, moderate, chaste, and ignorant of treachery—enemies only to the wild beasts and birds—is called to gentle and unacknowledged food.

# Chapter 6

## Concerning Minerva

-> **Minerva, who was also named Pallas by the Greeks** <-

**Minerva**, whom the Greeks called **Pallas**, was a maiden of such ancient origin that the poets of old—who did not know her true lineage—created fables <note>The translator uses "fables" to signal that these are pagan myths rather than historical or biblical facts.</note> claiming she was born from the skull of Jove <note>original: "Iovis"; the Roman name for the king of the gods, Jupiter.</note>. For this reason, she was believed to be a goddess of divine nature. However, others write that she was born in Libya near the lake called Triton, from which she also received the name "Tritonia."

She was a woman of sharp intellect and great industry. She was the first to discover and teach the craft of working with wool, showing how it should be cleaned and combed with cards <term>cards: tools with wire teeth used to untangle and align wool fibers before spinning</term>, how it should be spun upon the spindle, and finally how it should be woven into cloth. Because of this discovery, she is rightfully honored by all who practice these arts.

<margin>The invention of armor and war-craft</margin>

Furthermore, she was the first to devise the use of iron for making weapons and protective armor, such as helmets and breastplates, to shield the body in battle. Because wisdom is necessary for both the domestic arts of peace and the strategies of war, she was titled the Goddess of Wisdom. 

<insert>She is also credited with the invention of the flute and the discovery of the olive tree.</insert>

It is said that she remained a virgin her entire life, devoting herself entirely to study and the advancement of human knowledge. Her reputation for chastity and her many contributions to civilization led the ancients to build many temples in her honor.

<summary>This page introduces Minerva (Pallas), interpreting her not just as a goddess, but as a historical woman of great intellect who invented weaving and iron armor. The text emphasizes her role as a civilizing force who turned raw materials into useful goods for humanity.</summary>

<keywords>Minerva, Pallas Athene, weaving, textile history, invention of armor, Greek mythology, goddesses, women inventors</keywords>

### The Ninth Leaf

She comes next. In addition to this, she was the very first to devise **oil-pressing** <term>oil-pressing (ölschlahen)</term> for mankind; how one should crush the seeds and the kernels and afterwards press them out. And for the sake of the utility that people received from this, she won the right against Neptune to give her name to the city of **Athens**.

The ancients also claim she was the first to invent **wagon-travel** <note>This refers to the invention of the chariot</note> and its use, and how to turn iron into weapons, and how to cover the body with **armor**, and how to arrange the ranks of battle and how one should advance in them; for these things she gave laws and teachings.

They say even more: that she was the first to find all numbers, to order them, and to give them names, as they are still held to this day. Furthermore, she made sounding pipes <note>the flute or aulos</note> out of bird-bones or from the reeds of the marsh, and tuned them according to the voice. Because of this, the common folk believed the flute had been thrown down from heaven <note>original: "von himel herab geworffen"</note>, because the neck and face of the player became swollen and misshapen therefrom.

Why should I say more of such strange stories? Because of them, the errant antiquity and foolish humanity gave her the name **Goddess of Wisdom**. Therefore, the people of Athens were moved to receive their name from her, because that same city was inclined toward learning, art, and wisdom. They commended themselves to her protection and built for her an exceedingly high tower and a very large temple consecrated in her name, and they ordered a statue to be placed within it, fashioned in the form described below:

First, with **slanted eyes** <note>original: "krummen augen" — this likely refers to a piercing, sideways, or "owl-eyed" gaze, symbolizing the shrewdness of the wise</note>, because one can seldom recognize to what end a wise man’s intention is directed. Next, a **helmet** upon her head, because the counsel of a wise man should be hidden <note>kept secret</note> and armed. Afterwards, a **breastplate** <term>breastplate (pancer)</term> upon her body, because a wise man against all things...

It also begins an iconographic breakdown of her statue, linking her physical attributes (like her helmet and armor) to the virtues of a wise person.</summary>

...of the wheel of fortune—whether they be good or bad—one should be armed. <note>The text suggests that wisdom serves as armor against the unpredictable shifts of fate.</note> Furthermore, she holds a long, flexible spear or projectile in her hand, so that one may notice how the wise man <note>original: *d' weiß man*; referring to the personified philosopher or seeker of wisdom.</note> may send his projectiles of wisdom so far. This signifies that the wise man should be covered and protected by pure and clear counsel, and yet also guarded with serpentine <term>näterschen listen</term> <note>From "natter" (adder/viper); implies the biblical "wisdom of the serpent."</note> cunning, so that foolish people stand terrified before her countenance. They also gave her an owl to keep watch, because the wise man should see as well by night as by day.

At last, the praise and dignity of this same lady became so wide and so broad—and the errors of the ancients <note>The author uses "errors" to categorize pre-Christian pagan beliefs.</note> so favorable toward her—that temples were built in her honor throughout almost the entire world, and solemn feast days were established. This went so far that she was placed in a special chamber in the Capitol at Rome alongside the Best and Greatest Jupiter, and was held equal to Juno among the foremost gods of the Romans. However, there are some great men who say that these aforementioned deeds did not all belong to a single Minerva; <note>Historical scholars often identified several different mythological figures who shared the name Minerva.</note> I will gladly concede this, so that the praise of this illustrious lady may be all the more extensive and greater in number.

**V**enus was a woman from Cyprus, as some believe. But regardless of from whom she was born, she is counted among the **illustrious women** <term>illustrious women: from the Latin 'clarae mulieres,' referring to women of great fame or notoriety</term> more because of her extraordinary beauty than because of any of her meritorious deeds. For she was so beautiful of face and figure, and appeared with such elegant shapeliness and sensual delight of the body, that those who looked upon her were often deceived by a false opinion. 

For some said she was the true morning star, which is also named Venus. Others said she was a heavenly woman from the lap of Jupiter, sent down to the earth. # The 11th leaf

[Woodcut illustration depicting **Mercury** <note>original: "mercurius"</note> standing over the body of **Argus** <note>original: "argus"</note>, while **Jupiter** <note>original: "Jupiter"</note> embraces **Io** <note>original: "yo"</note>. Isis, who was previously called **Io**, was not only a queen but ultimately the most holy goddess of the Egyptians. At what time she lived, however, or from what ancestors she was born, is not certainly reported by the wise chroniclers. Some say she was a daughter of Inachus <note>Inachus was the first king of Argos and a river god</note>, the first king in Greece <note>original: "kriechen land"</note>, and a sister of Phoroneus. These men reigned during the times of Jacob, who was a son of Isaac. Others say she was a daughter of Prometheus, during the times when Phorbas ruled Greece, which was a long time after the previous period mentioned. Some say she lived in the times of Cecrops <note>original: "citropis"</note>, the king of Athens. And some say, moreover, that she lived in the times of Lynceus <note>original: "linthei"</note>, the king of the Greeks. Each of these authors sets forth particular reasons to confirm their opinion; yet she is counted by all of them among the most illustrious women as one of those most worthy of memory. And according to the opinion of the majority...

### The 12th leaf.

Of the master <note>Likely referring to a source author or authority figure mentioned in the preceding text</note>, she was a daughter of King Inachus. And although the poems of the ancient poets say how she pleased Jupiter because of her exceedingly beautiful form, and how he intimately knew her—and so that his misdeed would remain hidden, he transformed her into a cow—and how she was given to Juno, who desired her from him; and how Argus was appointed as the cow's guardian and was then slain by Mercury; and the cow was finally led into Egypt, where she regained her human form and was called **Isis**.

Yet, this same fable is not far from the truth. For as it is said, this same maiden was defiled by King Jupiter through adultery. Out of fear of her father because of the committed misdeed, she was placed by Jupiter's order into a large ship with several of her servants. Upon the ship's unfurled banner, a cow was painted, and for that reason, the ship itself was called "The Cow." 

And so this ingenious woman, possessing a masculine spirit <note>original: "manlichs gemüts"; a common historical praise for women showing leadership or bravery</note> and a desire for a kingdom, was led by a favorable wind toward Egypt. Because of her capability, she was chosen as queen of that land. While it is not recorded exactly by what specific merit she came to possess the kingdom, it is believable that she found the people there to be coarse, ignorant, and entirely unknowing of human ways—living more like beasts than rational beings. 

With great labor and courtly wisdom <note>original: "hofflicher klügheyt"</note>, she taught those same people how to till the earth, how to sow seeds, and how to harvest, grind, and bake grain. Furthermore, she gathered the wild people—who were scattered far throughout the forests—together into a community. She gave them laws <term>gesatzt</term> <note>refers to established legal statutes or ordinances</note> and taught them to live in an orderly and civil manner. And this is to be esteemed as even greater and more remarkable, being found in a woman...

<summary>This page provides a "historical" explanation for the myth of Io, suggesting she was a clever princess who fled to Egypt on a ship named 'The Cow' and eventually civilized the local population by teaching them agriculture and law.</summary>
<keywords>Io, Isis, Jupiter, Egypt, Inachus, civilization, agriculture, euhemerism, mythology, women's history</keywords>

The 12th leaf.

...she invented the first **letters** <term>letters: the alphabet or characters used for writing</term> and their forms, which were well-suited to the language of that same people. She taught them how to set these characters together so that the people could record their **laws** and bring those things that had been forgotten back into their memory. This achievement—even if I were to remain silent about her other deeds—seemed so miraculous to the people that they could easily believe Isis had not come to them from the land of **Greece**, but had been sent down from heaven itself. 

For this reason, they showed her divine honors even during her lifetime. And after her death, through the deceptions of the devil <note>original: "treügnuß des teüfels"; Boccaccio, writing from a 14th-century Christian perspective, explains the worship of ancient gods as a result of demonic influence on the "common people."</note>, she was so greatly honored by the common folk that her fame resounded as far as Rome. The Romans even had an immense temple built for her and established annual festivals to be celebrated there according to Egyptian customs. And it is certain that this same [woman]...

### ->About Europa, the queen in Crete (that is, Candia). The Ninth Chapter.<-

<note>This woodcut illustrates the abduction of Europa. Unlike the classical myth where Jupiter transforms into a bull, this version shows a ship labeled "Jupiter" with Europa being led toward it, reflecting a historical interpretation of the myth.</note>

Some say Europa was a daughter of Phoenix, but many more say she was a daughter of Agenor, the king of Phoenicia. She was of such wonderful beauty that the King of Crete (that is, Candia), Jupiter <note>The author uses "Jupiter" here as a name for a historical king of Crete, a practice called euhemerism, where gods are interpreted as ancient humans whose deeds were later mythologized.</note>, was moved to love her by the reports of her extraordinary beauty, even though he had not yet seen her. 

And because he wished to lead her away <note>original: "verfüren", meaning to lead astray or entice away</note>, he ordered a fleet of ships to travel toward her. Upon the banner of the ship, a white bull was displayed as heraldry. A man went to her to speak with her pleasantly, using such flattering words that she—without any suspicion of evil—walked with him from her father’s herd, which she was tending in the mountains, down to the Phoenician shore, listening to his deceptive words. 

There, she was snatched into the ship and swiftly carried away to Crete. 

Note here how unseemly it is for young women to wander about here and there according to their own free will, or to lend their ears to all the flattering words of young men or old women. For it is often heard and read that the discipline, modesty, and purity of young women are so stained by such things that the— <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page, mid-sentence.</note>

<summary>This page tells the story of Europa, a Phoenician princess abducted by King Jupiter of Crete using a ship decorated with a bull. The narrative serves as a moral warning to young women about the dangers of flattery and lack of supervision.</summary>
<keywords>Europa, Jupiter of Crete, Phoenicia, Candia, abduction of Europa, female modesty, moralizing literature, King Agenor</keywords>

From history comes the fable of how Mercury drove the Phoenician cattle to the shore and took Europa, and swimming through the sea, led her to Crete. However, there is some discord among the ancient writers as to what time Europa was abducted. For some write that it happened during the time when Danaus reigned in Greece; others, when Acrisius reigned. The last say it occurred during the time when Pandion reigned in Athens. 

However, I believe that this same legend is told of the Europa who was the mother of Minos, who was also seduced <note>The original term "geſchwechet" literally means weakened or defiled, commonly used in this era to describe the seduction of a woman.</note> by Jupiter, and was thereafter wed to Asterius, who was a king in Crete, and she bore by him Minos, Rhadamanthus, and Sarpedon. Clarifying this is of little use to us, except to say that this woman is called "illustrious" <note>original: "durchleűchtend"</note> because she was wed to such a god. Also, because she was of noble lineage and significant merit, and great virtue was found in her by her people—so great that the third part of the entire world is named Europe after her. And as a testament to this, the greatest philosopher, Pythagoras, had a <term>bronze statue</term> <note>original: "ärin ſaul," literally a bronze or brass pillar/statue</note> cast in her likeness and set up in the city of Taranto, and gave it the name Europa.

# The 14th leaf.

The kingdom of Scythia at that time was still uncultivated, consisting of wilderness and wasteland, surrounded by mountains and the high sea, so that it was less accessible to other peoples by way of rivers. From there, driven by the wicked desires of the land's rulers, two young men of royal lineage, Sylistus and Scolopicus, were exiled with a significant portion of their people. They came to the river Thermodon <note>original: "thermodo dontem," the Latin accusative form of the river Thermodon in modern-day Turkey</note> in Cappadocia and inhabited Tyria <note>likely referring to the region near Themiscyra, the legendary capital of the Amazons</note>. There they lived by robbery and caused great distress to the surrounding lands.

Because of these raids, and through the daily treachery of wicked enemies, many of the inhabitants of that land were killed, so that nearly all the male population had perished. When the women, finding themselves placed in the state of widowhood, bore this burden heavily and passionately sought to avenge their husbands, they—along with the few remaining men—were moved to take up weapons of war.

In their first rush of fury, they drove the enemies out of their region. Thereafter, they waged significant war against their neighbors. Finally, they began to consider whether they should marry foreign men again; they feared that doing so would only submit them to servitude <note>The text implies that marriage was viewed as a form of legal or social bondage</note>. They believed they were capable enough to fight with a warrior's hand themselves. Furthermore, because the remaining men seemed to have no kinder gods than those who had already been slain, the women all took counsel together—even those whose husbands had not been killed—deciding that they wished to kill all the remaining men.

Immediately after this deed was accomplished, they turned their angry rage toward their enemies. They pursued them so powerfully to avenge their husbands that the enemies desired nothing but peace from them. And when that peace was established, they, in the hope of [preserving] their lineage... <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing the story of how the Amazons maintained their population on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page describes the migration of royal Scythian exiles to Cappadocia and the subsequent uprising of their widows, who killed the remaining men to establish an independent, female-led warrior society.</summary>
<keywords>Scythia, Amazons, Cappadocia, Thermodon, Sylistus, Scolopicus, widowhood, female warriors, ancient history</keywords>

And whichever boy was born, they had him killed or sent away; the little girls were diligently raised for knighthood <note>original: "ritterschafft," referring here to the life of a warrior or soldier</note>. And in childhood, the budding of the right breast was burned out with fire or caustic agents <note>original: "aczung"</note> so that as they grew, it would not cause an obstruction in shooting. The left breast they left uninjured for the nourishment of the children who were to be born; because of this, they were called Amazons <note>The name "Amazon" was historically believed to derive from the Greek "a-mazos," meaning "without breast"</note>. This was also their constant exercise so that they would become skilled in performing manly deeds.

Through such skill, they not only held Tyria <note>likely referring to a legendary territory or ancestral homeland</note>, which had formerly belonged to their ancestors, but they also won through hard combat a great part of the earth toward the setting of the sun, called Europe. So much so that they devastated the land of Swabia <note>a region in southwestern Germany</note>; because of this, the inhabitants sought their dwellings on high mountains for greater safety. And after they had subjugated the land, they placed a king named Henetus at Augsburg, located in the Ries <note>The Nördlinger Ries, a large circular depression in western Bavaria</note>, which at that same time was called Vindelica, to rule the land of Swabia under their scepter. The same king also won further territories and the land of Mantua, which were called "Henecia" after his name, and they reach to where Baden and Venice now lie; and it was called "Venecia" through the changing of the letter "H" into "V," as most...

### The 15th leaf

Servius <note>Mauris Servius Honoratus was a 4th-century grammarian famous for his detailed commentary on the works of Virgil.</note>, who wrote about **Virgil**, makes it known in the first book of the *Aeneid* <note>original: "enea"; the epic poem telling the story of Aeneas.</note> regarding the verse on Antenor, that they <note>The Amazons.</note> also won much land in Asia and became formidable to everyone. 

And because their great power should not be without government, they appointed **Marpesia** and **Lampedo** <note>original: "Marsepia" and "Lampedonē"; legendary sisters and co-queens of the Amazons.</note> as queens from their mother’s lineage. Under their rule, the power of women was increased so significantly, as was written above. These same two, having been so highly ennobled through their great knightly deeds, divided the lands between themselves. 

Therefore, while one remained on the royal throne to rule the land, the other—with the remaining part of the army <note>original: "pres hores", likely meaning "des Heeres" or "of the host".</note>—practiced making other lands subject to them. And thus they increased their empires for a long time through notable renown and the conquering of lands. 

However, at last, when **Lampedo** had marched out against the enemy with the strength of the army, barbarians fell upon **Marpesia** and slew her, along with a significant portion of her maidens. But how it went for **Lampedo**, I do not know, as I have never read anything about it.

# The 16th leaf.

## Ovid in the 4th [book of] Metamorphoses. <note>original: ".iiij. ma." refers to Ovid’s Metamorphoses, Book 4, where the story of Pyramus and Thisbe is told.</note>

### Pyramus and Thisbe, the one the most beautiful of youths... <note>original Latin: "Piramus et Tisbe iuuenum pulcerrimus alter." This is the opening line of the story in Ovid's poem.</note>

## Concerning Thisbe, the maiden of Babylon. The 12th chapter.

**T**hisbe, the maiden of Babylon, has remained in the eternal memory of mankind more because of the tragic outcome of her unfortunate love than from any of her other deeds. We have also not [learned] from our ancestors what lineage she was from. However, she lived in the city of Babylon in a house that lay directly next to another house where a boy of her age named Pyramus lived. Because they were neighbors, it happened that they both shared much childhood companionship and daily association with one another. There grew in them such a childhood desire that, in their later years, they were ignited with an exceedingly great and fervent love through this childhood inclination, sparked by the great beauty of both their bodies, with which they were gifted above all others. 

They also began to show such love to one another through gestures as they approached their marriageable years. But as Thisbe grew further, her father and friends <note>In this context, "friends" often refers to the wider circle of family and kin.</note> intended to keep her under closer guard and at home for a future marriage. And as the lovers had to endure this for some time with heavy hearts, they busily sought by what means they might still be able to speak with one another. 

They found, in a common wall shared by both houses, a <term>crack</term> <note>original: "klunsen." This refers to the famous chink in the wall that allowed the legendary lovers to whisper to one another.</note> in a secret place, which was previously known to no one. They often came to this spot secretly to speak with one another. And habit brought them to the point that they widened the crack at that end.

<summary>This page introduces the story of Pyramus and Thisbe, two neighbors in Babylon whose childhood friendship blossomed into a forbidden, passionate love. It describes how they discovered a secret crack in a shared wall that allowed them to communicate despite their parents' restrictions.</summary>

<keywords>Thisbe, Pyramus, Babylon, Ovid, Metamorphoses, courtly love, forbidden love, secret communication</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the tragic lovers Pyramus and Thisbe, following their discovery of a crack in the wall between their houses through which they whispered. The previous page likely established their growing longing and the obstacles placed by their parents.</meta>

->The . .16. leaf<-

When it was most clearly to be noticed how they were sick <note>The text uses "sick" to describe the physical and emotional toll of "lovesickness," a common medieval literary trope.</note> with love: the desire of their hearts, their temptations, and their suffering began to show themselves. Their sighs and sometimes their fervent tears showed their state to one another even better. At times, they also desired a common love of the soul, wishing to embrace with their arms and touch their mouths <note>A poetic way of describing a kiss.</note> in the complete loyalty of eternal friendship. Finally, as they were fervently inflamed with passion, they thought to seek a way of flight. They agreed for the next night, as soon as they best could, to steal out from their houses and go into the forest that lay near the city, to the well of King Ninus <note>original: "ninj brunnen." In Ovid’s original Latin, this is "Ninus's tomb." The translator here identifies it as a well or spring located at the edge of the forest.</note> located at the forest. And whichever arrived there first should wait there for the other.

But Thisbe, because she was perhaps more fervent in her love, thought to leave her father’s house more readily. She went out boldly with a cloak wrapped around her during the first sleep of the night and came through the moonlight, unafraid, into the forest. And as she waited by the well, as often as she heard something, she raised her head, hoping for Pyramus’s arrival. Then she saw a lion coming toward the well, which frightened her so much that she fled into the bushes; in her haste, she did not think of her cloak lying by the well. 

But when the lion was sated from feeding on animals and his thirst was quenched, he began to toy with the cloak with his blood-stained <note>original: "schwaiſſigen." While "schweiss" means sweat in modern German, in this context it refers to the blood and gore from the lion's recent kill.</note> mouth and claws, and he worried it so much that it became entirely bloodied and torn. He left it lying there and went away. 

In the meantime, Pyramus came into the forest, having left his house later than she. He saw Thisbe’s bloodied, torn cloak and thought the lion had devoured her. Because of this, he began to mourn inwardly and lament his misfortune and misery, believing that he had given his dearest maiden the cause to suffer a grim death <note>original: "zeleidē dē grÿmen." The "grim" one likely refers to the "grim beast" or the "grim fate" of being killed.</note>.

<summary>Thisbe arrives first at their secret meeting spot but flees from a lion, leaving behind her cloak. Pyramus arrives later, finds the bloodied garment, and mistakenly concludes that Thisbe has been killed.</summary>
<keywords>Pyramus and Thisbe, King Ninus, forest meeting, the lion, the bloodied cloak, tragic misunderstanding, Early New High German literature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the tragic story of Pyramus and Thisbe. Pyramus, believing Thisbe has been killed by a lion, has just decided to end his own life.</meta>

->[Handwritten page number: 25]<-

### The 17th leaf

...death. And scorning to live any longer, he placed his drawn sword against his breast by the fountain and fell upon it to kill himself. And immediately, when Thisbe thought the lion had finished drinking and was gone—so that she would not make her lover suspicious of her absence, or that he might not take offense at her long delay—she began to creep back toward the fountain. 

And as she arrived, she heard—as can well be imagined—Pyramus still <term>thrashing</term><note>original: "zabeln." This refers to the reflexive twitching or struggling of a body in its final death throes.</note> upon the sword, and she was terrified, thinking the lion was still there, and she almost fled back again. 

But when she looked more closely, she saw by the moonlight that the one lying there was Pyramus. And as she hurried to his friendly embrace, she found that he had poured out all his blood through the wound and was now <term>giving up the soul</term><note>A common historical expression for the moment of death, when the spirit was believed to physically depart the body.</note>. 

And though she was terribly frightened at the first sight of this sorrow, she finally, with immense weeping, began to devise useless help, trying to keep the soul within the body for a while with kisses and friendly embraces. 

But when she could get no word from him, and noticed that he now disregarded her kisses—which had been so welcome to him only the day before—and saw that her lover was thus hurried toward death, she realized that he had dealt himself this death because he believed she had been torn apart by the lion. 

And moved by the love of her youth and his pain, she determined in bitter <term>fate</term><note>original: "bescherung," implying a destined or gifted lot, here meaning her tragic end.</note> to die with him. She pulled the sword by the hilt out of his wound and, with immense sighing and weeping, she called out the name of Pyramus, begging him to open his eyes so that he might at least see his Thisbe dying with him, and wait for her departing soul, so that they might be together wherever they might go. It is a wonder...

<summary>Pyramus commits suicide by the fountain, and Thisbe returns to find him dying. Realizing he killed himself out of grief for her, she decides to take her own life with the same sword to join him in death.</summary>

<keywords>Pyramus, Thisbe, suicide, fountain, tragedy, Roman mythology, star-crossed lovers</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the tragic narrative of Pyramus and Thisbe, specifically the moment of their mutual death and the narrator's moral reflection on the nature of young love.</meta>

### The seventeenth leaf

To say, when the dying—and now, from the weakness of his heart, scarcely conscious—youth <note>Pyramus</note> heard the name of his beloved maiden <note>Thisbe</note>, he could not deny her final prayer. He opened his eyes, heavy with death, and looked upon her who had entreated him; immediately she placed the sword by its hilt upon his breast and the point against her own heart and fell upon him. And with the shedding of her blood, both their souls departed from their bodies together.

And thus the envious wheel of fortune <term>wheel of fortune|The *Rota Fortunae*, a medieval concept representing the capricious and often cruel nature of fate that raises people up only to cast them down</term> could not prevent the mixing of their wretched blood, which it would not grant to happen through their physical embrace. Who would not have compassion for these young people? It would be a heart of stone that did not bestow at least a small tear upon this miserable end. They loved each other with a childlike innocence; therefore, they did not deserve such a deadly misfortune.

The love of youth is a fault, yet not so heavy for those who are single <note>original: *ledig*; referring to those who are unmarried and free to wed</note>, for they may come together in the state of marriage. Therefore, we must blame misfortune, or perhaps their wretched friends <note>original: *frewnd*; in this period, the term often refers to kinsmen or family members</note> were the cause of this evil. For one must not cast a sudden bolt <note>meaning a barrier or a lock</note> between loving youth; instead, they should be morally guided, so that they are not driven into desperate evil by a sudden restraint.

For Venus’s son, Cupid, is exceedingly strong and powerful—a sickness and a common torment of all youth. Therefore, it is somewhat to be tolerated, for the Ruler of all things <note>God</note> has given this inclination, and has granted the young more fervent stimulation toward such works of nature <note>procreation and physical intimacy</note> so that the human race remains in existence and does not perish, as would happen if such works were generally abolished.

<summary>This page concludes the tragedy of Pyramus and Thisbe with their double suicide and offers a moral defense of youthful passion as a natural force intended for the continuation of humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Pyramus and Thisbe, suicide, Wheel of Fortune, Cupid, Venus, nature of love, marriage, parental restraint</keywords>

# Concerning Hypermnestra, the Queen in the land of Greece
## And priestess of the goddess Juno. Chapter 13.

[Woodcut illustration depicting several couples in beds. Labels within the image: **Lynceus**, **Hypermnestra**] <note>The original labels are spelled 'linus' and 'Ypermestra'.</note>

**Hypermnestra**, illustrious by birth and dignity, was a daughter of **Danaus**, the King of Greece, and a wife of **Lynceus**. It is gathered from the ancient histories that once in Egypt there were two brothers who ruled a notable dominion inherited from their father, **Belus the Elder** <term>Belus: A mythical King of Egypt and ancestor of many Greek heroes</term>. One was named **Danaus** and the other **Aegyptus**. And although the number of children for both was equal, the fortune of those same children was very different; for fifty daughters were born to Danaus, and just as many sons to Aegyptus. But when Danaus had received an answer from the gods <note>An oracle or prophecy</note> that he should be killed by the hand of his brother's children, he was secretly [filled] with great [dread] <note>The text ends mid-sentence at "grosse," likely intending "great fear" or "great anxiety."</note>

<summary>This page introduces the story of Hypermnestra, one of the fifty daughters of King Danaus, and establishes the rivalry and prophetic fear between Danaus and his brother Aegyptus.</summary>
<keywords>Hypermnestra, Danaus, Aegyptus, Lynceus, Belus, Danaides, Greek mythology, oracles, underworld punishment</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Danaus and his brother Aegyptus. The previous page likely concluded with the growing tension between the two brothers or the arrival of the daughters in a new land.</meta>

# The Eighteenth Leaf

...should. It so happened that when the children of both men had grown up, Aegyptus <note>original: "egistus"</note> asked Danaus to marry all his daughters to Aegyptus's sons. Danaus willingly agreed to this because of a grim evil he had already planned. 

And when all the daughters were betrothed to his brother’s sons, the wedding day was set and all the magnificent celebrations were prepared. Then Danaus, with the utmost urgency, implored and pleaded with his daughters: if they wished to preserve their own lives and hope for favor from him, then each one must kill her husband with a blade <note>literally "with the iron"</note> during the night, while the men were bound and weighed down by sleep and wine.

Obeying their father’s will, each of them hid a sharp knife under her pillow <term>haubtpfulgen: an archaic term for a bolster or a large head-pillow</term> and, at their father's command, they murdered all their husbands, who were now fast asleep after the day's festivities. Only Hypermnestra <note>original: "ypermestra"</note> did not stain her hands with the blood of her husband, Lynceus <note>original: "lini"</note>. For the young woman had already set her heart upon him, as is the custom of daughters <note>the text suggests she had developed the natural affection a wife should have for her husband</note>.

<summary>This page describes the legendary mass-murder of the sons of Aegyptus by their brides, the daughters of Danaus, on their wedding night. It highlights the lone exception of Hypermnestra, who spared her husband Lynceus out of love.</summary>

<keywords>Danaus, Aegyptus, Hypermnestra, Lynceus, Danaïdes, Greek mythology, betrayal, wedding night, filicide, classical legends</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page continues a moralizing reflection on the vanity of earthly life and the instability of fortune, specifically transitioning into a classical example of cruelty and its consequences. The previous context likely discussed the fleeting nature of power.</meta>

[Handwritten at top right: 27]

->**The 24th leaf.**<-

...as if you could stabilize the flying **Wheel of Fortune** <note>The "Wheel of Fortune" (Rota Fortunae) was a common medieval and Renaissance symbol of the unpredictable nature of Fate.</note> with impure works. And your short, sickly life—which is shameful to even speak of—you intend to make eternal through shameful, heavy sins and misdeeds, yet you see all others hurrying in a flying race toward death. Why do you provoke the judgment of God with such terrifying deeds and such unspeakably evil works?

And leaving all others aside for now, I take as witness of such wickedness **Danaus** <note>In Greek mythology, Danaus ordered his fifty daughters to murder their husbands (the fifty sons of his brother Aegyptus) on their wedding night.</note> who, because he wished to lengthen his trembling years a little through the great bloodshed of his brother’s sons, robbed himself of the great honor and power he had obtained from them. He was instead placed in eternal shame and vice as a slandered man. 

This rogue and evil man intended to prioritize the few years of his old age over the blooming years of the youth of his brother's children. Yet they might have been more useful <note>original: "nüczlicher"</note> to the world if he had allowed them to live in honor. But to lengthen one’s old age with the blood of so many young men is against nature and is inhuman to even contemplate. 

To accomplish such an evil, which heaps even more vice upon him, he did not commission warriors or his servants; he armed his own daughters for the misdeed. Thus, he did not only destroy his brother's children, but he also cast his own daughters into eternal, shameful mourning because of the great murder committed with **defiled hands** <note>original: "vermeÿligten henden"</note>—daughters whom he could have otherwise kept in goodness and honor. And as he desired to prolong his life through misdeeds, he did not consider what a bad example, what audacity, what betrayal, and what a terrifying precedent he left for future **women** <note>original: "weÿßen"; likely referring to his daughters or women of the future, given the context of the Danaïdes.</note> through his evil deeds: to injure and under— <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at the bottom of the page.</note>

<summary>This page uses the mythological story of Danaus and his daughters to argue that attempting to extend one's life through sinful or cruel acts is unnatural and leads only to eternal shame. It contrasts the "blooming youth" of the victims with the "trembling years" of the aging perpetrator.</summary>

<keywords>Danaus, Wheel of Fortune, Danaïdes, morality, divine judgment, family betrayal, vanity of life, Early New High German literature</keywords>

### The 19th Leaf

...to crush the sweetness. For while he should have arranged the fire of friendship and love, symbolized by the burning candles in the bridal chamber, he instead ordered iron weapons for bloodshed. And where we <note>The author uses "we" to appeal to a general moral standard of fatherhood.</note> should diligently instruct children in marital love and friendship, he hardened his daughters' hearts into deadly hatred for their husbands. 

And without doubt, he would not have dared to inflict this upon them all if they had been gathered together and invincible; <note>original: "vnleschwärt," likely meaning "unextinguishable" or "unbeatable" in this context.</note> he did it to them separately, when they were heavy with sleep and wine. And what would have been terrifying to attempt by day, he commanded to be carried out by night. And what he would not have dared to attempt in an open field, he wanted to see accomplished in the bridal chamber. 

He did not consider that he brought upon himself more shameful dishonor and years of angry shame in the time to come than if he had stolen or taken the young men away through malice and treachery. Though he might have been highly honored by fifty sons-in-law, only one was preserved. 

Yet, the grim old <term>wütrich</term> <note>A "wütrich" is a bloodthirsty tyrant or a person consumed by mindless rage.</note> could not escape the justice of divine judgment. He was forced to shed his sinful blood as punishment for the evil done to his brothers. This <note>The one who survived and later took vengeance.</note> was Lynceus <note>original: "Linus"</note>, who afterwards ruled in Greece with a strong hand and great power. He released his Hypermnestra <note>original: "ÿpermestra"</note> from prison and joined her to himself in a better marital union, making her a partner in his rule over the kingdom. 

She was seen not only as a Queen of the realm, but because of her great and manifold virtues, she became a priestess of the goddess Juno; she was seen to excel in eternal and illustrious honors.

### ¶ Concerning Niobe, the Queen of the Thebans, the 14th chapter.

[Woodcut illustration: The King **Amphion** stands over the bodies of several children with a sword. To the right, Queen **Niobe** wears a crown and gestures toward the tragedy. Above them, a sun with a human face—representing Apollo—shines down.]

**Niobe** was very well known among highly renowned women, even to the common people. This was because she was the daughter of the most ancient and widely famous King of Phrygia, **Tantalus**, and a sister of **Pelops**. She was married to **Amphion**, the King of the Thebans <note>Thebes was a powerful city-state in ancient Greece</note>, who at that time was the most illustrious of men; this was because he was known as the son of **Jupiter** <note>The king of the gods in Roman mythology</note> and was considered the most eloquent speaker. It was also because, during his reign, he fathered seven sons and just as many daughters by this same Niobe. But that which should be useful to a wise person became, for this proud woman...

...turned to destruction. For because of the praiseworthy form of her children and the great nobility of her ancestors, she was so uplifted in pride and arrogance that she even dared to speak insulting words to the gods. 

And as the Thebans were diligent one day—at the command of the maiden Manto, who was a daughter of the seer Tiresias <note>In Greek mythology, Manto was a prophetess like her father; she urged the women of Thebes to worship Leto</note>—in holding festivals and sacrifices for Latona <note>The Latin name for the Greek goddess Leto</note>, who was the mother of Apollo and Diana. Both of these had been held in worthy holiness from ancient times according to their error <note>The original German "jrsal" (error/delusion) reflects the medieval Christian view that pagan worship was a spiritual mistake</note>. 

Then Niobe sprang forward wildly like a madwoman, surrounded by her children and adorned in royal garments, shouting thus: "What is this madness of the Thebans, to prepare such festivals and honor for Latona? She is but a stranger, descended from the Titans, and has born no more than two children, conceived from adultery. And yet you wish to prefer her over the daughter of Tantalus <note>Niobe refers to herself here by her father's name to emphasize her high lineage</note>, who has born fourteen children legitimately with her spouse, as if she were more worthy of honor than I." 

Thereafter, in a short time, it happened that in her very sight all her sons, in their beautiful blooming youth, were taken away by a deadly affliction—save for one. And Amphion, the father of the fourteen children, was so quickly overcome by such sorrow and pain through their deaths that he stabbed himself with his own hands with a sword. The Thebans believed all this had happened as revenge for the insulted gods. 

But Niobe, who remained as a widow, came through great mourning and sorrow into such a stubborn hardness <term>hözigkeit</term> <note>Literally "woodiness" or "hardness," describing a catatonic state of grief</note> and eternal silence, that one would more rightly have judged her an immovable stone than a woman. Therefore it was fabled by the poets that she was transformed into a stone pillar at Sipylus <note>Mount Sipylus in modern-day Turkey, where a rock formation resembling a weeping woman still exists</note>, where her children were buried. # The 21st Leaf
