**H**ypsipyle was a highly renowned woman, both for the kindness shown to her father, and for the wretched misery and the wretched death of the child of the King of Nemea <note>This refers to the infant Opheltes (or Archemorus). While Hypsipyle was serving as his nurse, she left him alone to show a spring of water to the "Seven Against Thebes" heroes, during which time the child was killed by a serpent.</note> entrusted to her. Also, for the necessary help shown toward her children in due time. She was a daughter of Thoas, the King of the Lemnians <term>Lemnians: inhabitants of the Greek island of Lemnos</term>. At the time when a grim rage possessed the minds of the women, they no longer wished to be submissive to the men at all. Then, in contempt of the power and rule of the old King, they drew Hypsipyle into their company, and by united counsel they arrived at the decision that on the following night they would kill all the men with weapons. This was indeed carried out according to their plan by all the women, except for Hypsipyle alone. She herself considered a kinder course of action and reflected on how inhumane it would be to defile her hands with her father's blood. # The 22nd Leaf.

...to Chios in a ship, so that he might escape the collective wrath of the women. She then quickly had a large funeral pyre prepared, as if she intended to burn her father upon it according to their custom, so that the other women would believe she had killed him too. And when all the women believed this, those same murderous women placed Hypsipyle upon her father's throne and made her queen.

And without doubt, the most virtuous deed to be praised far above others is the filial piety <note>The original German "ſenfftmütigkeit" suggests a gentle, humble devotion or kindness.</note> of children toward those who gave them life. For what is more fitting, what more just, what more praiseworthy, or what more human than to repay with honor and kindness those from whom we received nourishment when we were young and helpless? By whose diligence we are protected, by whose constant love we have reached our present days, being taught in arts and manners; by whose wealth and honor we are increased and elevated. This is beyond doubt.

Since all these things were observed by Hypsipyle and diligently offered to her father, she is not unjustly placed among the number of **illustrious women**. 

While she was ruling in this manner—whether it happened by the power of the winds or by design, I know not—it happened that Jason with the Argonauts <note>original: "arganauten"</note>, who wished to travel to Colchis <note>The destination of the Golden Fleece expedition.</note>, landed on her shore despite the resistance of the women. He was received by the queen not only with lodging but also in her marriage bed, and after his departure, she bore and raised two sons by him. 

Now, because of the laws established by the women of Lemnos, she had to send those same children out of the country; therefore, she intended to send them to her grandfather <note>original: "anherzen," likely referring to Hypsipyle's grandfather Ariadne's husband or Thoas's father.</note> so that he might nourish them and teach them to live in virtue, which indeed happened. But through this, the women realized that Hypsipyle had saved her father and that they had been deceived <note>original: "gelaichet," meaning tricked or cheated.</note>. ### Citations from Classical Literature

[ ¶ **Ovid's Epistles** <note>original: "Ovi. Epi." The author refers to Ovid’s *Heroides*, a collection of poetic letters written in the voices of heroines from Greek and Roman mythology.</note>

[ ¶ Why should I mention the daughters of Pelias, who were made guilty by their very devotion? <note>original: "Quid referam pelie natas pietate nocentes." This line refers to the Peliades, who were tricked by the sorceress Medea into killing and boiling their father, Pelias, under the false promise that it would restore his youth. Their "piety"—their love and desire to help their father—is what led them to commit the crime.</note>

<note>The text contains a word transcribed as "helgung," though it is likely a variant of the term for "indignation" or "anger" (*belgung*), possibly describing the emotional state of the characters involved in these tragedies.</note>

<summary>This page records the conclusion of a story regarding a woman's abduction by an army and her survival, followed by a transition into scholarly citations from Ovid concerning the daughters of Pelias.</summary>

# The 23rd leaf
## ¶ Of Medea, the Queen of Colchis. The 16th Chapter.

->[Woodcut illustration: A scene showing Medea and Jason fleeing on horseback. Medea holds a severed human head by the hair. Labels within the image identify the figures as **Medea**, **Jason**, and **Absyrtus** <note>Absyrtus was Medea's brother; according to myth, she murdered him and scattered his limbs to delay her father’s pursuit as she fled with Jason.</note> written vertically next to the head.]<-

**M**edea, the most wickedly cunning of the ancient sorceresses and a raging destroyer, was a daughter of Aeëtes, the most illustrious King in Colchis <note>Colchis was an ancient kingdom located on the eastern coast of the Black Sea, in modern-day Georgia.</note>, born to his spouse Persa. She was beautiful enough, but in the arts of sorcery, she far surpassed everyone. For though she may have had masters to teach her what they willed, she herself was so well-acquainted with the power of herbs above all other people that through them and her magic spells she could darken the air, summon winds out of their caves, create storms, and bring flowing waters to a standstill. She possessed potions to stir the mind <note>Likely a reference to love potions or draughts of madness.</note> and could burn all things passionately with fire. And—what was much worse—her spirit was a match for her arts; for whatever she knew how to do, she dared to accomplish with actual deeds. Where men were too weak to fight in wars, it was easy for her to create a warlike people <note>This refers to the myth where she helps Jason by showing him how to make warriors spring from the earth by sowing dragon's teeth.</note> and perform many similar feats. At those times, there was a youth in Thessaly <note>Referring to Jason, leader of the Argonauts.</note> who was highly esteemed for the boldness and strength of his spirit.

<summary>This page introduces Medea, the Queen of Colchis, detailing her lineage and her formidable, often terrifying, powers as a sorceress capable of controlling nature and the human mind.</summary>
<keywords>Medea, Jason, Colchis, Aeëtes, Absyrtus, sorcery, mythology, herbs, Thessaly</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Medea’s flight from her father following the theft of the Golden Fleece, specifically detailing her brutal actions to delay pursuit and her subsequent life with Jason.</meta>

[Handwritten at top right: 32]

->The 24th leaf<-

[She] had taken [her brother] with her, killed him, and also hacked him to pieces, scattering the parts far from each other in the field. Her intention was that while the sorrowful father <note>King Aeëtes of Colchis, Medea's father.</note> gathered the limbs of the pitiable child together again, mourned him, and committed him to the earth, they would be given enough time to flee. This also happened according to her plan. Finally, when she arrived in Thessaly with her Jason through great effort, labor, worry, and anxiety, she was very well received by her father-in-law <note>Aeson, the father of Jason.</note>. He was so filled with joy by the great victory of his Jason, by such a notable reputation, and by such an illustrious marriage, that he believed himself to have been restored from old age back into blooming youth <note>In many versions of the myth, Medea uses her magic to literally restore Aeson’s youth by boiling him in a cauldron with herbs.</note>. Immediately, Medea began to consider how she might place her Jason upon the throne, and through her arts, she sowed such hatred between Pelias <note>Jason's uncle, who had usurped the throne.</note> and his sons that they became mortal enemies. 

However, time brought it about that Jason began to hate Medea and took in her place Creusa, the daughter of Creon, the king in Corinth. Because of this, Medea was cast into immense grief and turmoil, constantly raging and thinking of how to avenge herself against Jason. Finally, she devised so much through her evil arts that she burned the same Creusa, along with her royal splendor <note>original: "herzlichkeit"; referring to her wedding finery or status.</note>, by means of a flying fire <note>This refers to the famous poisoned robe or crown Medea sent to her rival, which burst into flames when worn.</note>. And in the presence of Jason, she killed all the children whom Creusa had borne him <note>The text here attributes the children to Creusa, though most classical versions state Medea killed the children she herself had borne to Jason.</note>, for which reason she had to escape by fleeing to Athens.

There, she was married to King Aegeus and bore him a son who was named Medus after her husband <note>This may be a scribal error or a variant, as Medus is usually considered the namesake of the Medes or named after Medea herself.</note>. Later, she attempted to kill Theseus <note>Aegeus's son from a previous relationship and the famous hero of Athens.</note>, her stepson, with poison. Because of this, she had to undertake her third flight and returned to Thessaly, where she again earned the restored friendship of Jason.

<summary>This page recounts the darker episodes of the Medea myth, including the murder of her brother, her manipulation of Pelias's family, the lethal revenge she took on Jason’s new bride Creusa, and her eventual flight to Athens.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Medea following her flight from Corinth, moving from her mythological biography into a moralizing reflection on the fallibility of human perception.</meta>

# The 24th leaf.

But not long thereafter, she was driven out of all **Thessaly** <note>A region in northern Greece, the homeland of Jason.</note> along with **Jason** by **Aegialeus**, who was a son of **Peleus** <note>likely referring to Pelias, the king who originally sent Jason on his quest; in many versions, his son Acastus or Aegialeus drives the couple out.</note>. Because of this, she came home again to **Colchis** and restored her old, poor father to his kingdom <note>Medea's father was King Aeetes; in some traditions, she returns to help him reclaim his throne from his brother Perses.</note>. But where she finally went, or in what manner she ended her life, is not in my memory—I have never read or heard of it.

Yet, so that I do not overlook this notable point, it should be remembered that the eyes must not be permitted to see all things. For through sight, we receive the outward appearance <note>original: "den ſchein"</note>, by which we are moved to lustful inclination or hateful envy. Because of this, according to our own pleasure, we praise beauty and unjustly condemn uncleanness and poverty. 

And since the eyes are **unlearned judges** <note>a philosophical concept suggesting that the senses cannot discern moral value without the aid of reason.</note>, they judge only by the appearance of what is seen and are often so greatly deceived that they prefer shameful things to worthy ones, fictions to the truth, and anxieties to joys. And thus they praise that which should be scolded.

<summary>This page concludes the story of Medea's return to Colchis to restore her father to his throne, followed by a philosophical warning about the dangers of trusting outward appearances over moral truth.</summary>

**A**rachne of Asia <note>original: "Aragnes"; refers to the mythological figure Arachne, famous for challenging the goddess Minerva to a weaving contest</note> was born of the common people, the daughter of a cloth dyer from Colophon <note>an ancient Greek city in Ionia, famous for its luxury and dyes</note>. Although she was not of high birth, she is nevertheless to be highly praised for her various merits. Some of the ancient writers say of her that she was the first to invent the weaving of linen and its associated tools through her high intelligence. She also devised the knitting of bird nets, fishing nets, and the like.

And as her mind devised what is called <term>spinning</term> <note>the process of drawing out and twisting fibers to form thread</note> for the purpose of weaving, she produced—using those and other implements—such artistic work of all kinds of imagery, so skillfully arranged with colors, as if a painter had applied them with a brush. This was indeed a commendable example of feminine cleverness. For this reason, some wished to award her the prize above all others who lived in those times in such an art. She was highly praised and renowned throughout the whole world for such work, and because of this, her spirit became so emboldened that she... <note>the sentence breaks off here and continues on the next page</note>

placed a rope around her neck; yet she was released from it by her friend <note>original: "freünde"; the "friend" here is the goddess Minerva, who transformed Arachne into a spider rather than letting her die by suicide.</note>. However, she spent the rest of her life from then on in idleness, passing the time without any labor. Now, let any man say: what benefit can come from the pride of Arachne? She intended to bring all the honor and dignity of the entire world upon herself alone, and for that reason, she was humbled. Had she, however, kept God in her heart—who is the creator and giver of all things—and offered praise, thanks, and honor for the good bestowed upon her, He would have been more gentle and merciful toward her, opening His lap to pour even more skills into her. Therefore, this example is well worth noting for all those who are gifted by God and nature above others: that they should not carry themselves in pride above others, so that they are not brought low. For it is just as easy for God, the giver of all things, to take away as it is to give. And I would wish that Arachne alone were given to us as an example; but there are, alas, many "Arachnes" now on earth, and I would wish them self-knowledge for the common good.

# The <unclear>twenty-eighth</unclear> leaf

<note>This text describes one of the Sibyls—legendary women of antiquity whom medieval Christians believed were granted divine visions of the future, including the coming of Christ.</note>

...found in her prophecy. The merit of her intellect, her prayer, and her devotion was so great in the sight of God that, through divine grace and the influence of the Holy Spirit, she prophesied so much and so clearly that her words might better be called a **Gospel** <note>original: "ewanglium"</note> than a prophecy.

Because of their diligent entreaty, she also prophesied to the Greeks in beautiful poetry all their future troubles and, finally, the destruction of Troy. She spoke as loudly and clearly as if the entire subsequent history had been seen plainly beforehand. In the same way, she encompassed the power of the Romans and many of their future histories in a few verses, many years before the city was even founded.

And what is, in my opinion, even greater is this: that which the [Biblical] prophets had prophesied hiddenly in <term>figures</term> <note>Symbolic representations or types that foreshadow later events.</note> through the Holy Spirit, she has clearly revealed. She spoke of the mystery of the Word made flesh, the life of the only-begotten Son and his miracles; how he was to be betrayed, mocked, captured, and shamefully killed; his victory and triumph in rising again on the third day; his ascension into heaven; and finally the future judgment of all people. She prophesied all of this so plainly as if it were not a prophecy at all, but rather a description of things that had already happened. 

Because of such merit, I believe she was exceedingly dear to God and is highly venerable above all other pagan women. Some also write of her that she remained in eternal chastity, which is also good to believe; for out of a defiled <note>original: "vermeiligten," meaning stained or impure</note> heart, so much light of prophecy regarding future things could not easily have shone. However, at what time or where she died has been worn away by age <note>meaning the information has been lost to time</note>.

# The 28th leaf
### Lucan in the ninth book
### The fields of Medusa, daughter of Phorcys, lay desolate far and wide. <note>original: "Squalebant late phorcynidos arua meduse"</note>
## **Of Medusa, the 20th Chapter.**

->[Woodcut illustration depicting **Perseus** on a winged horse, and a man labeled **Neptune** embracing a woman labeled **Medusa**.]<-

**Medusa** was a daughter and heir of the most wealthy King Phorcys of the most abundant kingdom located in the Atlantic Sea, <note>Phorcys is a primordial sea god in Greek mythology, often depicted as the father of sea monsters.</note> which some say were the islands of the <term>Hesperides</term> <note>The Hesperides were nymphs who tended a garden with golden apples at the western edge of the world.</note>. This same Medusa was of such wonderful beauty that she not only surpassed all others in lovely adornment, but she also moved many people to travel just to see her for her beauty; she was esteemed by many to be desirable beyond the powers of nature. Her head was adorned with gold-colored hair; she possessed a singular beauty of the face and a seemly height of the body. Among other things, the power of her eyes was so very lovely and mighty that all men who looked upon her with favor were transfixed by her, standing as if they were immovable and forgetting themselves and their senses. <note>This account humanizes the myth of the Gorgon, suggesting that her ability to "turn men to stone" was actually an allegory for her breathtaking beauty which left suitors paralyzed with admiration.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the story of Medusa, describing her not as a monster, but as a princess of extraordinary beauty whose gaze was so captivating it rendered men motionless.</summary>
<keywords>Medusa, Phorcys, Hesperides, Perseus, Neptune, Greek mythology, beauty, Atlantic Sea</keywords>

--- Page 278 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous leaf, likely discussing the origins or character of a historical figure who became the basis for a myth. The first word "unknown" completes a sentence from the previous page regarding her origins or early life.</meta>

->The 28th Leaf<-

unknown. But this is found concerning her: that she was knowledgeable above others regarding the world <note>original: "erdbawes," literally "earth-building" or the inhabited world</note>, for which reason she was called **Gorgonis**. Through this, and with her great cleverness and reason, she not only maintained her father’s estate in good standing but also greatly increased it—so much so that with her treasure she surpassed the riches of all kings in the West. Thus, she was renowned in the farthest lands for her wonderful beauty, her vast wealth, and her far-reaching reason and cleverness. 

However, her great praise was brought before the Greeks. Among them was **Perseus**, who was more elegant than others and a flower of charming youth; from hearing such reports, he fell into a great desire to see the most beautiful of women and to seize the treasures. Therefore, he set out for the West with a prepared fleet. He was seen in a ship that was marked with a **flying horse** <note>The author provides a "Euhemeristic" explanation here, suggesting the mythical Pegasus was actually just a ship with a horse figurehead</note>, and he arrived there with great speed. With bold foresight and a strongly armed hand, he troubled the queen and burdened his ships with gold and various spoils, and traveled back home to his people. 

From this history, poets were given the occasion to compose fables <note>original: "tzetichten," meaning to create poetry or fictionalize</note>. As we read, **Medusa** turned people who looked at her into stone through sorcery; and how **Minerva**, moved to anger against her, transformed her hair into snakes because she had lain with the holy **Neptune** in Minerva's temple. From him, she gave birth to a flying horse, upon which Perseus, flying with his people across the sea, came into her land and overcame her with sword and shield. 

O, you unhappy gold! Where you lie hidden from your master, you are useless. But if you are brought to light, shining, you breed so much envy and desire that you cause a thousandfold sorrow.

world. And as Eurytus <note>The OCR reads 'eurithius', referring to Eurytus, King of Oechalia</note> had promised to give [Iole] to him as a wife through the advice of his son, he did not wish to keep his word. Because of this, [Hercules] waged a great war against him and took from him his land, his people, and also his life. And he led away with him his beloved Iole. 

But Iole's mind was always more occupied with her father's death than with love for the one by whom he was killed, and she became eager for revenge. Yet she knew how to hide this so well with such a wonderful display of feigned love, with such pleasure and cleverness, with so much flattery, and with so much unchaste enticement to love, that she completely inflamed Hercules and set him so afire that he could no longer perceive her ill intentions. 

Instead, she believed that whatever she might desire from him, she should be granted. Immediately, she acted as if she felt some horror at his rough clothing and the wildness of his nature, and she first commanded him to lay aside the club with which he had tamed the wild, terrifying beasts. 

Afterward, she had him take off his clothing, which was a skin he had stripped from a lion in the Nemean forest <note>The Nemean Lion was the first of Hercules' twelve labors; its skin was legendary for being impenetrable</note>, which was a visible sign of his strength. After that, he laid aside the little wreath made of white poplar branches <note>original: "alberczweig"; the white poplar was sacred to Hercules</note>, which had been placed upon him as a sign of victory. She also left neither quiver nor arrow upon him, which he had used frequently. 

Yet this did not seem enough to her; instead, she constantly considered how she might further approach the unarmed man playfully. And with great premeditation, she boldly "leaped into the battle" <note>A metaphor for her seductive strategy</note> and first adorned his hands with very precious rings of gold and gemstones. Afterward, she had his rough head—disheveled with tight curls <note>original: "morlöcken," literally 'Moorish locks,' likely describing dark, coiled hair</note>—brushed, straightened, and combed, and had his hair stroked with fragrant ointments.

<meta>This page continues the moralizing commentary on the story of Hercules, specifically focusing on how even the strongest hero was overcome by love/lust (represented by Cupid). It serves as a warning to the reader about the dangers of idleness and the "soft life."</meta>

### The 30th Page

A severe, impure sickness <note>In this context, the "sickness" refers to the overwhelming and morally corrupting power of lust or obsessive love, often called "lovesickness" in medieval and Renaissance literature.</note> also usually comes to those soft and excessively delicately raised young women. Likewise, it affects the idle, softly raised youths. For **Cupid**, the child of Venus, is a sworn enemy of all work, all fortitude, all reason, and wisdom; he seeks only to companion himself with a soft life, pleasure, joy, and amorous flattery. Therefore, that this same child could slip into the exceedingly hard heart of **Hercules** is a much greater wonder than the deeds he performed by his own hand <note>original: "p̈e," likely an abbreviation for "propre," meaning "properly" or "by himself."</note>.

Therefore, this story should not be a small example to those who wish to consider their own salvation <note>original: "eÿgen heÿl," referring here to one's moral well-being or spiritual health.</note>; for every person may well be terrified by it, as we see these enemies standing before our eyes at all times. Because of this, we should strengthen our hearts and build strong **bulwarks** <term>bulwarks: "bruͤſtwerÿ," defensive walls or breastworks, here used metaphorically for moral defenses.</term> against it and resist the first assault. 

For where Cupid perceives resistance, he loses his strength. First, tame the eyes so that they do not look upon vanity; stop the ears—like the **adder before the charmer** <note>A reference to the biblical image of the "deaf adder" from Psalm 58, which refuses to hear the voice of the snake charmer; used here as a metaphor for ignoring seductive or shameful speech.</note>—so that shameful words are not heard; and overcome the laziness of the body with hard work. 

For this child shows himself friendly and flattering toward all the world; at first sight, he is pleasing and welcome to everyone. At the first entry, he is received with desire and beauty. He gives counsel to adorn the body, to maintain good manners, to have honorable gestures, to practice good conduct, to speak pleasant words, and to sing verses and songs; he seeks and devises joy, pastime, **dainties** <term>dainties: "schleckmeuͤlin," meaning sweet treats or delicacies, implying a life of luxury and excess.</term>, and many such things. But when he sees the person proven and established in such folly, and has now made the free spirit subject to him, then he binds them with hard bonds and stirs up sighing and lamentation in the night when one [should] rest...

<summary>This page uses the figure of Cupid to warn against the dangers of idleness and luxury, arguing that even the mighty Hercules was brought low by love's "impure sickness." It advises readers to build moral defenses and stay busy with work to resist temptation.</summary>

## Concerning Deianira, the wife of Hercules, the 22nd chapter

**D**eianira was the daughter of King Oeneus <note>original: "cenei"</note> of the Aetolians <note>original: "etholorū"</note> and the sister of Meleager. She was gifted with such great beauty that, for her hand in marriage, a battle between Achelous <note>original: "atheolum," a river god who could change his shape</note> and Hercules was promised and carried out, with the condition that she should be given as a wife to the one who proved victorious.

When Hercules won her in that combat, her beauty so moved the centaur Nessus to love that he followed after Hercules as the hero led her home from Calydon.

And when they came to the river Evenus <note>original: "ebanum"</note>, they found it so swollen with floodwaters that it was not easy to cross. Nessus, because he was "well-mounted" <note>The text uses "wolgeritten." This is a play on words or a literal description of the centaur’s nature, as he is half-horse himself.</note>, offered himself serviceably to Hercules, saying he would gladly carry Deianira across the water on his horse <note>referring to his own equine back</note>. 

As Hercules consented to this, intending to swim across after them, Nessus hurried over the water like one who had achieved his heart's desire, and he fled away with the woman whom his heart loved...

<summary>This page tells the beginning of the story of Deianira, whose beauty causes a conflict between Hercules and Achelous, and leads to her attempted abduction by the centaur Nessus at the river Evenus.</summary>

<keywords>Deianira, Hercules, Nessus, Achelous, Centaur, Greek Mythology, Evenus River, marriage contest</keywords>

garment, which was stained by the blood of the wounds, and 
said to her: "If you can manage to have Hercules 
put on these clothes, then all his anger toward you will be 
taken away." Deianira believed those same words and

she sent him the garment secretly by his servant, Lichas,

that he burned himself in a fire. 
And thus was Deianira placed in a widow’s chair <note>A traditional expression for becoming a widow</note>, 
when she intended to bind her husband to her in love; 
and Nessus was also avenged.

### ¶ Chapter 19: Concerning Jocasta, Queen of the Thebans

**J**ocasta, the Queen of the Thebans, has remained in the memory of humanity more for her great misfortune than for any notable merit or strict rule. Her ancient lineage came from the illustrious founders of the city of Thebes, and she was married to Laius, the king of that place. She conceived and bore a son by him. However, she was forced to cast that same son out to be devoured by wild beasts because of the command of her husband, Laius. He had received an answer from the gods that he would be killed by his own son.

Although the mother did this most unwillingly, she nevertheless had him cast away out of obedience to her husband. And while she believed the beasts had torn him apart, he had been brought to the King in Corinth, who ordered him to be raised, nourished, and educated as his own child; he was named Oedipus. <note>The name Oedipus means "swollen foot," referring to how his feet were bound or pierced when he was abandoned as an infant.</note> But as he grew up, trained in knighthood, and became strong, the King sent him out on a military expedition against the Phocians. By chance, he came to fight against his unrecognized father, Laius, and struck him dead. 

Afterward, he won the widow—his own mother—as his wife, with both parties being unknown to the other. And she bore him two sons, Eteocles and Polynices, and as many daughters, Ismene and Antigone. And when she now believed that she had become entirely lucky and blessed because of the kingdom and her children, one time, when she sought an answer from the gods regarding her state of being, it was...

<summary>This page recounts the early tragedy of Jocasta, Queen of Thebes, detailing the abandonment of her son Oedipus to avoid a prophecy, and the accidental fulfillment of that prophecy through his return, the killing of his father, and their incestuous marriage.</summary>
<keywords>Jocasta, Oedipus, King Laius, Thebes, Greek Mythology, prophecy, Eteocles, Polynices, Ismene, Antigone</keywords>

...it was made known through her that he was her biological son, whom she had taken as her husband. And although this heavily troubled and distressed the lady's mind, he <note>referring to Oedipus</note> was placed in far greater misery and sorrow—so much so that, out of great shame for the sin he had committed, he desired eternal death. And he gouged out his eyes and departed from his kingdom. Because of this, the brothers—his children—became divided and waged war among themselves. 

And although Jocasta often intervened between them in great distress, intending to reconcile them, they were moved by such grim hatred that they both died from wounds they had inflicted upon one another and were carried before their mother. From this sorrow and pain, the wretched mother and grandmother <note>original: "anfraw." This term emphasizes the dual role Jocasta played as both mother and grandmother to her children/grandchildren due to the incestuous nature of her marriage to Oedipus.</note> was so moved in her spirit that she knew no other way to end her misfortune than with iron. 

And she left both her daughters entangled in the wheel of fortune <term>glückrad: a common medieval metaphor representing the unpredictable and often cruel nature of fate</term> and left her brother Creon to rule. She ended her anguish and distress with death by the sword, with which she drove her soul—now aged—along with her life out of her body. 

Although some say she could not endure such adversity for so long, but as soon as she saw her son Oedipus cast out his own eyes, she also raged against herself with the sword, ending her life.

**The 34th leaf**

[Woodcut illustration: A king, labeled vertically as **Tarquinius Priscus** <note>original: "tarquiniꝰ prisci"</note>, sits on a throne on the left. He gestures toward a woman labeled **Amalthea** in a banner above her. Between them, a fire consumes several volumes labeled **books** <note>original: "libri"</note>.]

**A**malthea the virgin—called by some Deiphobe, the daughter of Glaucus—had her first origin from the most ancient city of Cumae, located in Campania. She was also a Sibyl <note>A Sibyl was a woman in antiquity believed to possess powers of prophecy.</note> and lived during the time of the destruction of Troy. She grew so old that she lived to see the time of Tarquinius Priscus, the Roman king, as many of the ancient wise men truly believe. And they say, moreover, that her chastity was so pure and clear that in all the many hundreds of years she lived, she was never defiled by any man.

And although the verses of the poets say she was loved by the god Apollo, and was therefore gifted by him with long life and made holy, this is properly understood in a natural sense: that she was born under the best influences of the sun (since Apollo signifies the sun). Therefore, a good <term>constitution</term> <note>original: "complexion"; in medieval medicine, this refers to the balance of bodily humors determining health and character.</note> inclined toward great things was given to her, into which the true Sun <note>The author here transitions from the pagan god Apollo to a Christian interpretation of the "true sun" as God or Divine Light.</note>—which enlightens all people of this world—poured a true light. Through this light, she recognized God and prophesied much, and with His help, she desired to maintain eternal chastity. This same Sibyl had a particular dwelling place near Babylon on the shore...

<summary>This page describes the life and legendary longevity of the Cumaean Sibyl, Amalthea, explaining her prophetic gifts through both astrological influence and divine grace. The accompanying woodcut depicts her famous encounter with King Tarquinius Priscus regarding the Sibylline Books.</summary>

at Lake Avernus <note>original: "ſee auerni," a volcanic crater lake in Italy believed by Romans to be the entrance to the underworld</note>, where she revealed her divine prophecy and which keeps her name to this very day. And although that same house of prayer <note>original: "bethauß," referring to the Sibyl's cave or temple</note> has almost perished from great age and abandonment, one can still observe from the remaining ruins and the multitude of boldly crafted stones the great honor and majesty in which it once stood—so much so that those who see it today are filled with great wonder. 

Virgil <note>Publius Vergilius Maro, the Roman poet</note> tells of the Sibyl in the sixth book of the *Aeneid* <note>original: "enea"</note> regarding where she led him; I shall leave that in its place <note>the author chooses not to recount the famous descent into the underworld here</note>. The next day, she requested from him for the six remaining books the same price she had previously asked for the nine. She said that if he did not give it to her, she would burn the other three books before his face, and on the third day, the last ones. Thus Tarquinius gave her what she desired and took the books from her. In these books, posterity found the entire history of the Romans clearly prophesied. Therefore, in future times, the Romans kept these same books with total diligence and in high honor; whenever they desired to know their fate, they paid special attention to the sayings in these books, just as they would to the answers of the gods. 

It is found written of her that she ended her final days in Sicily <note>original: "cecilia"</note>, where her grave was shown for many years afterward. From this you may observe that no human can become more illustrious through any matter than through diligent learning joined with divine grace. Both are denied to no one who makes themselves worthy of them; but whoever [desires]... <note>The sentence ends mid-thought at the bottom of the page</note>

->The 35th leaf.<-

Whoever considers this will find how childishly the old, ignorant, and unlearned man is carried to his grave in a dazed and senseless state <note>original: "tozother gestalt." This refers to a state of mental numbness or being "dead to the world" due to a lack of education.</note> because of his neglect of learning. Furthermore, when we see that a woman, through such diligent study, has so exercised her nature by divine grace that she has been raised to divinity—that is, to holiness—then men should all the more apply themselves to this task. For nature (which is God) has given men a quicker aptitude <term>aptitude: "geschicklikeyt," referring to a natural talent or readiness for learning</term> to acquire such knowledge, provided they are willing to drive ignorance away from themselves. Therefore, all men should rightly weep, mourn, and wither in their frustration when such a gift is withheld from them because of their stubborn ignorance. Such men should rightly judge themselves to be like unmoving rocks among the living, as will happen when they are forced to silently admit their own failings.

The <unclear>seventy-fourth</unclear> leaf <note>original: "lxxiv. blat"; this refers to the folio number in the original manuscript</note>

...Arcadia married; others say she was his son's wife. This same woman appeared illustrious not only because of her royal lineage, but also because she was so skilled in the Greek tongue. She practiced such high intellect and arts at all times that she was praised for her prophecy and was held to be a prophetess above all others of that time. And whenever anyone desired her to speak of such things, she gave her answers through beautifully composed verses, which in Latin are called songs <note>original: "Carmina"; this refers to rhythmic verses or incantations from which her name "Carmenta" is derived</note>. For this reason, her name Nicostrata was forgotten, and she was called Carmenta. 

She was the mother of Evander, the king in Arcadia. And the ancients say that the god Mercury was his father; however, one should understand this correctly, so that you do not think the gods performed impure acts with women. Rather, it means that such children were born under the best influence of the planet Mercury <note>The author is offering a "rationalized" or astrological explanation for myths, suggesting planetary influence rather than literal divine fatherhood</note>. Children born under this influence are found to be ingenious, highly knowledgeable, resourceful, and very eloquent, as is also mentioned in the next chapter. This same Evander, because...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Carmenta and Evander settling in Italy, focusing on the invention of the Latin alphabet and the civilizing of the local population.</meta>

<margin>44</margin>

->**The 36th leaf**<-

the **Palatine hill** <note>original: "stat pallantina"; this refers to one of the seven hills of Rome where Evander and Carmenta settled.</note> where he lived with his mother for a long time, he built from the ground up. In that same place, many years later, the mighty city of **Rome** was established. However, **Carmenta** found the inhabitants of that land to be rough, coarse, and ignorant of how to live humanely; they were beastly and nearly like wild animals. Although **Saturn**, who had come to them as a fugitive from **Greece** <note>original: "kriechen land"</note> long before, had taught them something of farming <note>original: "kozen bawes," likely referring to grain cultivation or agriculture.</note>, they still had no knowledge of Greek writing, or at least very little practice in it. Recognizing this with her divine spirit and foreseeing things far in the future, she rightly considered that the great glory, history, power, and wonders of that city and region—ordained by God—should be recorded in a distinct language and script in the future. With diligent exercise of her innermost intellect and her full strength, she set about inventing a unique language and script, entirely separate from all others, for the people. God was also helpful to her undertaking, for through His grace it was accomplished, and so were the letters through which she taught the people the **Latin language**. At first, there were no more than **16 letters**, just as many as **Cadmus**, a founder of the city of **Thebes**, had discovered for the Greeks long ago. The Romans and we still use these to this very day, and they are called Latin letters, given to us by her. Although some of the ancient wise men later added more letters for good reason, and though this woman’s prophecy was seen as wonderful by everyone, this invention of letters and language was so highly regarded by all the people in **Italy** that the coarse folk did not believe Carmenta was a human, but rather a goddess sent from heaven.

<summary>This page describes how the prophetess Carmenta civilized the primitive inhabitants of the Palatine Hill by inventing the 16-letter Latin alphabet, an act so revolutionary that the people came to worship her as a goddess.</summary>

# The [thirty-sixth] leaf

...sent to teach. Therefore, after her death—even though they had shown her divine honors during her lifetime—they had a temple built in her name at the very base of the Capitoline Hill <note>The hill in Rome where the temple of Jupiter stood and where the city's archives were kept</note>, where she had lived for a long time, as an eternal memorial. And the surrounding lands were named **Carmentalia** after her. When the city of Rome was later built up to be great, it did not want this memory to fade; rather, when the need arose for the Romans to place a city gate at that spot, they called it the **Carmental Gate** <note>original: "Carmentalem portam"</note> after her name, which it kept for many years. 

The land of Italy, and later its capital city, Rome, was once blooming with great gifts above all other cities in the world, and was shining with a "heavenly light," as one might say. For from Asia came the greatest wealth, royal furnishings, and nobility of blood. And although the Greeks significantly increased these things, the beginning of it all came there from **Troy**. 

The arts of **Arithmetic** <note>original: "Arismetrica"</note> and **Geometry** were given by the Egyptians. **Philosophy** and **Rhetoric**—that is, the art of orderly speech—and nearly all crafts were taken from the Greeks. But agriculture <term>baw: referring here to farming and the cultivation of the land</term>, which at that time was known to few people, was brought there by the first **Saturn**, who had been driven out of the land of Greece. The unholy worship and invocation of the gods first arose from the Etruscans <note>The civilization in central Italy that preceded the Romans</note> and came from **Numa Pompilius** <note>The legendary second king of Rome, credited with establishing many of its religious institutions</note>. 

Common laws were first given by those of Athens, and afterwards the supreme authorities and emperors expanded them and established them according to the course of the times. The highest priesthood and spiritual life, according to proper order, is from...

<summary>This page details the commemoration of the prophetess Carmenta in Rome and outlines how Roman civilization was a synthesis of Trojan nobility, Egyptian science, and Greek philosophy and agriculture.</summary>

And although grammar could not be so perfectly or completely established by her at first, she nevertheless was the first to sow the seeds which were then planted and cultivated by the ancient masters who followed her. 

This resulted in a great part of the **Hebrew** and **Greek** tongues and teachings being recorded <note>original: "getruckt" — while later used for printing, here it refers to the physical act of impressing or writing text on the page</note> in books with ink and pen. ### Chapter 26: Concerning Procris

Therefore, she once spoke angrily to him: "You will regret it, Cephalus, that you have loved **Procris** so fervently, once you discover that she will place the love of gold above your own, if she were to be tested in that way." When Cephalus heard this, he immediately became eager to investigate the matter. Feigning a long pilgrimage, he departed from her; and when he had ridden only a short distance, he turned his journey around and came home in secret. He then arranged for a youth to test **Procris** in her <term>constancy</term><note>The original German "stättikeit" refers to her marital' fidelity and moral steadfastness.</note> again and again with gifts. And although for a long time she remained steadfast and immovable, despising all offers of gold and gifts, yet at last she was so softened by increased gifts and many promises that she was willing to grant the youth his desire, provided that he brought the promised gifts with him. To this end, a place and time were appointed. I also cannot imagine that greater folly could be 
seen than when a person strives after that which,
once found and acquired, may bring only regret;
both of which the frivolous woman has well proven, whereby
she stained herself with an indelible mark for all 
eternity, and also sought death, which she 
found, and yet desired it little. But leaving 
the immoderate desire for gold aside, to which
all fools are drawn, I ask those who are hardened in such
desire to tell me what use, what ornament,
what praise, what glory they receive from it. 
Truly, to my knowledge, this desire is nothing else
than a shameful sickness of the mind sprung from 
frivolity, and is found only among those
who can easily endure that all other 
people, whoever they may be, are set above them.

### The 39th Leaf

...was very troubled. She thought of his anxieties and tribulations, wishing also to have compassion and to share in them. And she comforted her old father and strengthened him with her tears, and prayed for him to march against Eteocles <note>The brother of Polynices; the two died fighting each other for the throne of Thebes</note>, who at that same time, through his own power and against all right, ruled the kingdom of the Thebans <note>original: "thebanorū"</note> terribly alongside his brother. 

And so that those events might proceed which were seen to happen as an answer from the gods, and would not be diminished, she wished to be seen as generous beyond the common nature of women. And she gave, unasked, to Eurydice <note>The text names her Eurydice, though in classical myth the wife of the seer is Eriphyle</note>, the wife of the seer Amphiaraus <note>original: "amphiomari"</note>, that costly jewel <note>The Necklace of Harmonia, a cursed object used as a bribe</note> which had once been so unlucky for the women of Thebes. Through this gift, Amphiaraus, who lay hidden, was revealed by his wife. And because of his prophecy, he was drawn toward Thebes in a great company, yet with great misfortune. 

For after a truly great slaughter, when other captains had perished and Adrastus was stripped of all help and turned to flight, the dead body of Polynices remained lying on the field among other stinking corpses, unburied. And when his sorrowful wife heard this, she rose up from the royal hall and cast from her the shining attire <term>wat: an archaic word for clothing or raiment</term> and soft clothes, setting aside womanly weakness, and went with few companions to the battlefield <term>walstat: the place of the slain</term> of the strife. 

She allowed neither the enemy nor any kind of horror to frighten her away; and what is even greater, she did not allow herself to be deterred by the great command of King Creon, who had secured the victory. In that decree, he forbade—under penalty of death—anyone to go onto the battlefield or to bury anyone. But she went fervently, with such a sorrowful spirit, at midnight to the place of the slain, stinking corpses, and searched... now this—

->Leaf 40<-

...then one after another, for so long until she, with a small little light, recognized the pitiful countenance of her dearest husband. O, what a wonderful deed by this woman! That face—wounded by weapons, defiled by the dirt of the earth and the dust, and covered in foul sweat so that it might have been unrecognizable to almost anyone—could not hide itself from his most faithful spouse. The uncleanness of that defiled face could not even drive away her loving kisses. 

The command of <term>Creon</term><note>The King of Thebes who had decreed that the bodies of the fallen invaders must be left to rot unburied.</note> also could not prevent her from carrying out her will. For after she had desired, with her manifold and eager embracing and kissing of his mouth, to move the soul back into his body, and had bathed all his limbs with her tears—so that she would leave no act of kindness toward him undone—she placed the dead corpse into the fire prepared for it. 

And after the burning, she collected his ashes in a jar and had him laid to rest in the earth with royal honors according to her custom. It has indeed often happened that women have wept for their husbands' sickness, imprisonment, poverty, and many kinds of misfortune; yet they always did so in hope of better fortune for which they were waiting, and for an end to the misfortune. 

While such behavior is also to be praised, nonetheless it cannot serve as a testimony of the highest love in the way that the devotion <note>original: "dienstbarkeit"</note> of **Argia** <note>Argia was the wife of Polynices, one of the Seven Against Thebes.</note> showed to her husband. For she went out onto the <term>battlefield</term><note>original: "walstat"</note> whereas she might well have mourned her husband inside the house. She turned over the stinking corpses with her own hands, a task she could easily have commanded others to do. She had him laid to rest with royal honors by fire, even though, given the state of things and the danger of the time, it would have been enough to simply bury him in the earth. She wept for him with wailing and crying, which she could have easily passed over in silence; and yet there was no word of benefit to her from...

### About Manto, the 28th Chapter

[Woodcut: A woman kneeling in prayer or prophecy before a fire. Behind her are two demonic figures, one of which is riding a goat-like beast. A label above the woman reads "mantho".]

**Manto** was a daughter of Tiresias, the greatest soothsayer of the city of Thebes. During the times of King Oedipus and his sons, she was held in high regard. She was also of such perceptive mind and reason that she completely learned <term>pyromancy</term><note>From the Greek 'pyr' (fire) and 'manteia' (divination); the practice of predicting the future by observing flames or smoke.</note>—that is, the art of soothsaying from fire—from her father. This art was discovered in ancient years by the Chaldeans <note>An ancient people of Mesopotamia famous in the Middle Ages for their knowledge of astronomy and magic.</note> (as some would have it) or by King Nimrod (as others say). She mastered it so thoroughly that in her time no one could be found...
